Where are you right now?

You left. Now what? Start with the part that fits today.

The short answer

You don’t have to read this book in order. Pick the line below that sounds most like today, and it will open the three chapters that matter most for what to do after leaving an abusive relationship. Nothing is stored, and you can come back to a different line tomorrow.

Most people looking for what to do after leaving an abusive relationship open this book in the middle of something. A message just arrived. A court letter came. It’s 2am and the house is too quiet. So the book starts with you, not with chapter one.

Pick the line that fits. You can pick a different one next week.

I left in the last few days

Start with The first 30 days, a list for the first month. Then A safety plan for after you’ve left, because the weeks after leaving can be the most dangerous. Then Leaving was the middle, for why it doesn’t feel like the ending.

They keep contacting me

Read Hoovering to recognise the pattern. Words you can use has replies you can copy. Messages you don’t have to answer helps you sort what needs a reply from what doesn’t.

We have children together

Start with Co-parenting or parallel parenting?. Then Your children, and what to say. Then Children as the channel, for the ways children get pulled into it.

There’s a court case

Start with What to document and how. Then Family court, honestly. Then Your day in court.

I don’t feel safe

Go to If you’re not safe right now first. Then A safety plan for after you’ve left and Warning signs to take seriously.

I can’t stop thinking about them

Read The trauma bond. Then What you’re allowed to feel about someone you left. Then Checking their profile.

I can’t sleep, or I’m not okay

Read What your nervous system is doing and Sleep. If it’s more than a hard week, go straight to When it’s more than aftermath.

They left me

Start with If they left you. Then Hoovering, because people who leave often come back. Then The grief nobody will let you have.

We still live together

Read Still under the same roof. Then A safety plan for after you’ve left, which works before the move too.

I’ve been accused of something

Read When they say you’re the abuser. Then What to document and how. Then Legal help with little or no money.

I’m thinking of going back

Read If you go back, which has no judgment in it. Then The trauma bond and Hoovering.

I’m under 18

You matter just as much. Read If your story doesn’t look like the usual one, which lists help made for young people. Then If you’re not safe right now.

They follow me or turn up

Read Stalking and harassment after a breakup first. Then Warning signs to take seriously, because following and turning up are among the signs that matter most. Then What to document and how, so each time counts.

Money is the problem

If they control what you get, start with Money as the channel. If you’re starting again with nothing, read Money from zero. For help with benefits or a home, go to Housing and benefits.

I’m pregnant or have a new baby

Read Pregnant or with a new baby. Pregnancy can raise the risk, so read A safety plan for after you’ve left as well, and tell your midwife or doctor what’s happening.

They have a new partner

Read The new partner. It covers the comparison, the warning you may want to give, and why the new relationship rarely changes the pattern. If it’s pulling you under, Checking their profile helps with the urge to look.

My family or friends are on their side

Read Your family and mutual friends and The friends who didn’t believe you. If the story they’re telling is the problem, add The narrative campaign.

It was years ago and it still hurts

Nothing has gone wrong. Read The anniversary effect and Memory, and doubting yourself. What “recovered” means offers a way to think about it that isn’t a finish line.

I’m thinking about dating again

Read Dating, when you’re ready and when you’re not. Then Trust as a practice, which is about trusting yourself as much as anyone new.

I’m helping someone

Read Helping someone in year one. It’s short and written to be forwarded. Then What post-separation abuse is.

If you only have five minutes

Whatever brought you here, these three pages do the most in the least time:

  1. If you’re not safe right now: the numbers, and how to use this book on a phone someone else might check.
  2. The record: four lines you can write tonight that make every later step easier.
  3. Messages you don’t have to answer: how to tell what needs a reply.

Then put the phone down. The rest will still be here.

Something else

Read How to use this book for how it works. Or use the search at the top of the page. Type it the way you’d say it: “he keeps texting my sister”, “she won’t pay”, “restraining order”. Or open the A to Z or Questions people ask.

If you're not safe right now

Where do I go for domestic abuse help today?

The short answer

If you are in danger now, call your emergency number. For domestic abuse help that isn’t an emergency, a domestic abuse helpline can talk you through what to do next. They are free and confidential, and many are open all hours. You don’t need to be sure it’s serious enough to call.

If someone is hurting you, threatening you, or outside your door, call your emergency number now. Nothing in this book matters more than that call.

You don’t need to be sure it’s “bad enough.” Call handlers would much rather hear from you early.

Emergency numbers

  • United States and Canada: 911
  • United Kingdom: 999. If you can’t speak, call 999 from a mobile, then press 55 when the recording asks. This tells the operator you need help. It doesn’t share your location, so say where you are if you can. It doesn’t work from a landline.
  • Australia: 000
  • Ireland: 112 or 999
  • Anywhere else: search “[your country] emergency number” now, and save it in your phone under a plain name.

Domestic abuse helplines

These lines are free and confidential. They can help you plan, find a refuge or shelter, and explain your options where you live.

  • United States: National Domestic Violence Hotline, 1-800-799-7233, or text START to 88788.
  • United Kingdom: National Domestic Abuse Helpline (England), 0808 2000 247. Scotland: 0800 027 1234. Wales: Live Fear Free, 0808 80 10 800. Men: Men’s Advice Line, 0808 801 0327. LGBT+ people: Galop, 0800 999 5428. Honour-based abuse and forced marriage: Karma Nirvana, 0800 5999 247.
  • Canada: find your nearest shelter at sheltersafe.ca, or call 211 to be connected to local services.
  • Australia: 1800RESPECT, 1800 737 732.
  • Ireland: Women’s Aid, 1800 341 900. Men’s Aid, 01 554 3811.

Anywhere else, search: “[your country] domestic abuse helpline.” The national charity will come up near the top.

What happens when you call a helpline

A trained person answers. They won’t judge you, and they won’t tell you to leave or to stay. They’ll ask whether you’re safe to talk, then listen.

You don’t have to give your name. You don’t have to know what you want. You can say, “I don’t know if this counts,” and they’ll take it from there. Most people who call say something like that.

They can help you think through what happens next. That might be a safety plan, a refuge or shelter, legal help, or someone to talk to again next week. If you need to stop, hang up. You can call back whenever you want.

If you can’t speak or can’t call

  • United States: in many areas you can text 911. If it isn’t available where you are, you’ll get a message back telling you to call. The National Domestic Violence Hotline also has live chat on its website.
  • United Kingdom: emergencySMS lets you text 999, but you have to register first. Text “register” to 999 now, while it’s quiet, so it works when you need it. The National Domestic Abuse Helpline has live chat at set hours.
  • Canada: text service for 911 is for people who are Deaf, hard of hearing or speech-impaired, and they register in advance.
  • Australia: the National Relay Service connects calls for people who can’t hear or speak on the phone. 1800RESPECT has online chat.
  • Anywhere: if you call and can’t talk, stay on the line. Tap the phone, cough, or answer yes and no questions by pressing keys if asked.

If you have to leave right now

Take only what you can carry without being noticed:

  • your phone and charger
  • keys
  • any cash and one bank card
  • your ID, if you can reach it quickly
  • medicine you need today
  • the children, and one comfort item each

Everything else can wait. Documents can be replaced and things can be collected later, often with a police escort. Go to a public place, a friend, or a neighbour, then call.

If they’re at the door

  • Don’t open it.
  • Call your emergency number. Say: “My ex-partner is outside my door. I’m frightened. I have [a protective order / children here].”
  • Move to a room with a lock and a way out, away from the kitchen, with your phone.
  • If they get in, get out if you can. Your safety comes before your things and your home.

Afterwards, write down what happened, with times. What to document and how shows how.

If you’re thinking about harming yourself

Call a crisis line now:

  • United States and Canada: call or text 988
  • United Kingdom and Ireland: Samaritans, 116 123
  • Australia: Lifeline, 13 11 14

Then read When it’s more than aftermath, which was written for this moment.

If this device isn’t private

The Leave button at the top of every page blanks this page at once. Press Esc on a keyboard to do the same. The browser’s back button can still bring the book back, so close the tab when you safely can.

A private or incognito window keeps this book out of your history. Browsers can still be monitored in other ways, so if someone has had access to your phone, use a phone they’ve never touched: a friend’s, a work phone, a library computer. Surveillance and technology explains how to check.

What to do next

When the danger has passed, make a plan for the next time. A safety plan for after you’ve left walks through it, and Find help has more numbers and the words to search for local services.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine has a short version for the hardest moments: The 30-Minute Rescue.

How to use this book, and what it is not

What will this post-separation abuse book do, and what won’t it do?

The short answer

The Long After is a free post-separation abuse book for anyone who has left a partner, a marriage or a controlling family and found that leaving wasn’t the end. Read it in any order. It is not therapy, not legal advice and not a timeline.

This is a post-separation abuse book: it is for the part after you leave. You left a partner, a marriage, or a family that controlled you. Maybe it was last week. Maybe it was eight years ago and a chapter here still lands. Either way, something kept going after the door closed, and you want to understand it and deal with it.

How it works

Every chapter stands alone. Most people arrive in the middle, from a search, at a bad moment. So each chapter opens with the short answer: the plain version, in a few lines. Everything under it’s the detail.

The headings are the questions people ask. If you came here wondering “does it ever end?” or “is this a crime?”, there’s a heading for that.

When you need a tool rather than a chapter, the Toolbox has things to print or copy: a four-line record, a safety plan, a device check, a court-day list, and replies you can send.

If you don’t know where to start, go to Where are you right now?.

How each chapter is built

Every chapter has the same frame, so you always know where you are:

  • The short answer. A few lines at the top. If that’s all you read, you’ll still have the main thing.
  • Questions as headings. Each heading is a question people ask. Scan them and skip to the one that’s yours.
  • “Say” boxes. Words you can use, in messages, on the phone or face to face. Change them to sound like you.
  • Links that open the right page. A phrase in colour takes you to the chapter that covers it. Links to the Pattern Decoder open the page for that tactic. Links to The Sovereign Divine Feminine open the exact passage.
  • Honest checks. In some chapters, a short set of private questions marked An honest check, just for you. They appear where hurt, longing or anger can bend how things look. They’re about what’s in your hands now, never about blame for what was done to you. Nobody sees your answers.
  • Sources. At the bottom of every chapter, listed plainly, so you or an advocate can check them.
  • Next, if this is you. Two or three pages that usually come next for someone in your place.

If reading is hard right now

After abuse, many people find it hard to read for long. Concentration goes. Words slide off the page. That’s common, and it usually comes back.

  • Read the short answers only. Every chapter works at that level.
  • Use The card. It’s the whole book in a few lines.
  • Read with someone. A friend can read a chapter aloud, or read it first and tell you the part you need.
  • Print one page. Paper can be easier than a screen, and you can underline it. Keep it somewhere private.
  • Stop when you’ve had enough. The book doesn’t need finishing.

The words this book uses

  • “The person you left,” “your ex,” “the other parent.” These all mean the same person. The book uses whichever fits the sentence.
  • “Abuse.” This book uses the word for patterns of control, fear and punishment, with or without violence. Most legal definitions now do the same.
  • “Survivor” and “victim.” The book uses neither much. You don’t have to call yourself anything.
  • Gender. Abuse happens to people of every gender, from people of every gender. The examples change from page to page on purpose.

What this book is not

It isn’t therapy. If you need steady support from someone trained, a book can’t replace that. Finding a therapist who understands this explains what to look for.

It isn’t legal advice. Laws differ by country, and often by state or region. Every legal chapter tells you what a thing is, gives examples from a few countries, and tells you exactly what to search to find your local version.

It isn’t a timeline. No page here tells you how you ought to feel by six months, or a year, or five. Any number like that would be wrong for a lot of the people reading it. People heal at different speeds, and the speed says nothing about how well you’re doing.

It isn’t a verdict on whether you’re “over it.” There’s no test to pass. What “recovered” means offers a working definition, and it isn’t a finish line.

It will never describe ways to harm yourself. If you’re in crisis, When it’s more than aftermath is written for you. You don’t need to read anything else first.

Who wrote it, and how it’s checked

The Long After is written by Shae Stovell as part of a free library. Every chapter lists its sources. Where experts disagree, the chapter says so. Where the book is giving its own judgment, it says that too. What this book might have wrong collects the weak spots in one place, and Every source in this book lists all of the sources together.

Keeping it private

Nothing you do here is saved. There are no accounts and no tracking of what you read. The Leave button at the top clears the page instantly. If someone might check your phone, a private window helps, and Surveillance and technology explains the rest.

Sharing this book safely

You can send any page to someone by copying its address. Each chapter has its own.

  • To a friend who’s in it: send one chapter, not the whole book. What post-separation abuse is or Hoovering are good first pages. Think about who else reads their messages.
  • To someone helping you: Helping someone in year one was written to be forwarded.
  • To a professional: For professionals explains how the book can be shared with clients.
  • In print: every page prints cleanly, with its sources.

If you print pages

Paper is easy to find. Keep printed pages somewhere the person you left can’t reach: at work, with a friend, or in a folder that doesn’t look like what it is. Shred what you don’t need.

The rest of the library

This book picks up where The Sovereign Divine Feminine leaves off. That book covers the relationship and the leaving; this one covers what comes after. The Silence is written for men. The Pattern Decoder names hundreds of tactics, one page each, and many chapters here link to it. Advocates and therapists can find sharing details in For professionals.

Leaving was the middle

Why is it still so hard after I left?

The short answer

After leaving an abusive relationship, many people find that the hardest part isn’t over. The control often moves to new channels, your body stays on alert, and the people around you expect relief. None of that means leaving was a mistake.

You left. In every story you’ve ever heard, that was the ending. The door closes, the music changes, and the healing starts.

For a lot of people, it doesn’t go like that. Leaving turns out to be the middle.

Why does it still feel this hard?

Three things usually happen at once after leaving an abusive relationship.

The control changes shape. The person you left loses most of their reach in a single day. Often they look for it somewhere else: through the children, through money, through what they tell your friends, through the courts. It looks different from what happened at home, so it can take a while to recognise it as the same thing. What post-separation abuse is names it, and The shape it takes when they can’t reach you directly shows how to spot it.

Your body hasn’t caught up. For months or years, staying alert kept you safe. Your nervous system learned that lesson well. It doesn’t unlearn it because you changed address. The jolt when your phone buzzes, the sleepless nights, the startle at a raised voice: What your nervous system is doing explains why these hang on.

The people around you expected a different story. They expected relief, maybe some sadness, then a steady climb to fine. When you miss the person, or fall apart months later, or go quiet, they don’t know what to do with it. Sometimes neither do you.

If leaving didn’t feel like the ending you were promised, you didn’t do it wrong. Most people find there’s more after it.

Was leaving a mistake, then?

No. When someone who relied on control loses it, things often get harder for a while. That difficulty says nothing about whether you should have stayed. For many people the first months are the roughest stretch, and Why it often gets worse first explains what drives that and how to stay safe through it.

Leaving was a real achievement, and it still wasn’t the end. Both of those are true.

What should I expect after leaving an emotionally abusive relationship?

People describe some of the same things, whether the abuse was physical, emotional or both:

  • doubting your own memory, or whether it was “that bad”
  • missing them, sometimes badly
  • jumping at a message tone or a car that looks like theirs
  • tiredness, illness, or not being able to sleep
  • anger that arrives late
  • them trying harder to reach you, not less

None of this means you got it wrong. Emotional abuse can be harder to name than a bruise, so the doubt can last longer. The chapters on the trauma bond, your nervous system and anger arriving late cover each of these.

What changes in the first months, and what doesn’t

Some things change the day you leave. You choose when the lights go off. Nobody checks your phone. You can buy the cheap shampoo or the expensive one without explaining.

Other things take longer. Your body may still brace when a key turns in a lock. You may still rehearse what you’d say to them. You may still feel guilty for things that weren’t your fault. The habits you built to survive don’t know yet that you’ve left.

And some things start only after you leave: the court letters, the messages through other people, the grief. Expect a mix of all three for a while. It’s normal for the first months to feel harder than you thought, and for some days to feel lighter than you thought you’d get.

Why do people around you think it’s over?

Because from the outside, leaving looks like the end. Your friends saw the bags packed. Your family heard the news. They’re relieved, and they want you to be relieved too.

So when you say the messages haven’t stopped, or that you miss them, or that the court case is wearing you down, you may get blank looks. “But you left.” That gap between what people expect and what you’re living through is lonely.

You don’t owe anyone a neat story. If it helps, send the people closest to you one page: Helping someone in year one. It explains, without you having to.

What can you stop expecting of yourself?

  • To feel only relief.
  • To know exactly how you feel about them.
  • To be “over it” by any date.
  • To handle the court, the children, work and your own feelings all at full strength.
  • To never wonder whether you did the right thing.

What you can expect instead is smaller and steadier: to get through today, to write down what happens, and to ask for help with one thing at a time.

Signs you’re moving, even when it doesn’t feel like it

Progress after leaving rarely feels like progress. It looks like this:

  • You noticed a message and decided not to answer it straight away.
  • You slept through one night.
  • You laughed at something and didn’t feel guilty for a minute.
  • You made a small choice without imagining their reaction.
  • You caught yourself thinking “that wasn’t my fault,” and half believed it.

None of these feels big on the day. Together they’re how the long after gets shorter.

What does “the long after” cover?

This book stays with you after the door closes. It covers:

  • What is happening: the ways control continues once direct contact stops.
  • Staying safe: a safety plan for life after leaving, technology, stalking, your address.
  • How it continues: children, money, the story they tell, friends and family, the new partner, the return.
  • Contact: how much, how, and what to say.
  • Courts and the systems: records, legal help, family court, protective orders, the police, housing.
  • Children: co-parenting, handovers, what to tell them, how they react.
  • Feelings: the trauma bond, grief, guilt, anger, closure.
  • Body, health and help: sleep, your nervous system, therapy, and what to do when it’s more than a hard week.
  • People and rebuilding: friends, new people, money, work, home, trust, dating, and who you are now.

Who is this for?

Anyone who has left, at any distance in time. A partner, a husband or wife, a girlfriend or boyfriend, a parent or a family that ran your life. The examples often say “your ex,” because that’s the most common case. If your story is different, If your story doesn’t look like the usual one is for you.

If you’re still deciding whether to leave, some of this will give you an honest preview. The Sovereign Divine Feminine covers the leaving itself, and its chapter on post-separation control describes, from the inside, what can start once you go.

Where should I start?

Wherever today is. Where are you right now? sorts the book by situation, and How to use this book explains what it will and won’t do. If you left in the last few days, start with The first 30 days.

What can the first months look like?

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Maya left in March. The first week felt like relief: her own bed at her sister’s, no one checking her phone. By April the messages started: some furious, some tender. In May a solicitor’s letter arrived about the children. Her friends kept saying, “At least it’s over.” She stopped telling them about the letters.

In June she noticed she’d slept through the night twice. In July she ignored a message for a whole day and nothing terrible happened. By August the court process was still going, but she had a routine, a record and two friends who understood. It wasn’t over. It was hers.

What leaving already proved

Leaving was a real achievement, even if the months after it are hard. Whatever comes next, you’ve already done the part many people never manage.

What post-separation abuse is

What is post-separation abuse, and what does it look like?

The short answer

Post-separation abuse is when someone keeps trying to control, frighten or punish a former partner after the relationship ends. It often runs through children, money, the courts, technology and other people. It is a recognised form of domestic abuse, and in some places parts of it are crimes.

You left, and the control didn’t stop. It went somewhere else. Post-separation abuse is the name for that.

What is post-separation abuse?

Post-separation abuse is a pattern of behaviour by a former partner that keeps control over you after the relationship is over. The aim, conscious or not, is the same as before: to keep you reachable, unsettled and answering to them.

Researchers describe it as coercive control carried on by other means. When the person you left can no longer walk into your kitchen, they use the roads still open to them. The shape it takes when they can’t reach you directly maps those roads.

Whether or not they want you back, they often want you reachable.

What does post-separation abuse look like?

Here are the common forms. Each has its own chapter.

  • Through the children. Arguments at every handover. Questions to the children about your life. Running you down to them. Last-minute changes to the schedule. See Children as the channel and the Decoder’s page on children used as leverage.
  • Through money. Child support paid late or not at all. Debts left in your name. A house sale dragged out for years. See Money as the channel and financial control.
  • Through the courts. Filing after filing. Delays. Applications that drain your time and savings. This is often called litigation abuse or legal abuse. See Systems abuse and litigation abuse and the Decoder’s legal abuse.
  • Through the story. Telling friends, family, your employer or the school that you’re unstable, dangerous or lying. See The narrative campaign and smear campaigns.
  • Through other people. Messages passed through your sister. Mutual friends who start “checking in.” See Your family and mutual friends.
  • Through technology. Tracking apps, shared accounts, a smart doorbell they still control. See Surveillance and technology.
  • By turning up. Following, watching, appearing where you are. See Stalking and harassment after a breakup.
  • By coming back. The warm message on your birthday, the apology, the emergency. See Hoovering.

Is post-separation abuse domestic violence?

Yes. Post-separation abuse is domestic abuse that continues after the relationship has ended. Most definitions of domestic abuse include former partners, and many include psychological, emotional and economic abuse as well as physical violence.

In England and Wales, for example, the Domestic Abuse Act 2021 covers people who are, or have been, partners. Its definition of domestic abuse includes controlling or coercive behaviour and economic abuse.

Is post-separation abuse a crime?

Some of it is, depending on where you live.

Threats, assault, stalking, harassment, criminal damage and breaking a court order are crimes almost everywhere. A pattern of controlling behaviour is a crime in a growing number of places:

  • England and Wales: controlling or coercive behaviour became a crime in 2015. Since 2023 the law also covers former partners who no longer live together.
  • Scotland: the Domestic Abuse (Scotland) Act 2018 covers partners and ex-partners.
  • Ireland: coercive control became a crime under the Domestic Violence Act 2018.
  • New South Wales, Australia: coercive control in current and former relationships became a crime in July 2024.
  • United States: there’s no federal coercive control crime. Several states, including California, Hawaii and Connecticut, now allow courts to consider coercive control in protective order or custody decisions.
  • Canada: the Divorce Act has included coercive and controlling behaviour in its definition of family violence since 2021.

Laws change. Search “[your country or state] coercive control law” for the current position, and see Police and reporting.

How is this different from a high-conflict divorce?

Plenty of separations are hard. People argue about money and children, say things they regret, and grieve. That is conflict, and it usually runs both ways and eases with time.

Post-separation abuse is one-directional and purposeful. One person keeps creating problems, and the problems keep landing at the moments that hurt you most. Calling it a “high-conflict divorce” can hide that, because it makes two people sound equally responsible. Abuse, or a hard breakup? gives a fair test.

Is verbal and emotional abuse after separation still abuse?

Yes. A lot of post-separation abuse is words: insults in messages, threats dressed up as concern, constant criticism of your parenting. Leaving an emotionally abusive relationship doesn’t always end the emotional abuse. It can move into texts, emails and handovers.

You don’t need bruises for it to count. Most legal definitions of domestic abuse include psychological and emotional abuse. Keep the messages. What to document and how explains what to save.

What is the post-separation power and control wheel?

The Power and Control Wheel is a diagram made in 1984 by the Domestic Abuse Intervention Programs in Duluth, Minnesota. It shows physical violence on the rim and the everyday tactics of control as spokes: intimidation, isolation, using the children, money, and more.

Services have since adapted it for life after separation. Those versions add spokes such as using the courts, using the children’s schedule, and using other people to watch you. Search “post-separation power and control wheel” to see one. Many people find that seeing it on one page makes the pattern easier to explain.

Why do they keep going?

People ask this more than almost anything else. If the relationship is over, why won’t they stop?

There’s rarely one answer, and you can’t know what’s in their head. These are the patterns that researchers and advocates describe most:

  • Control. For someone used to running another person’s life, losing that is intolerable. The behaviour is an attempt to get it back.
  • Entitlement. A belief that they’re owed your time, your body, the children, the house or your silence.
  • Punishment. Leaving is treated as a betrayal that has to cost you something.
  • Image. If you tell the truth, people might believe you. Getting their version in first protects how they look.

None of these is about something you did or could fix. That matters, because a lot of people spend years trying to find the reply, the tone or the compromise that will make it stop. Often there isn’t one, because the goal was never the issue being argued about.

A checklist: does this sound like post-separation abuse?

Tick any that fit since you separated:

  • Contact keeps coming after you’ve asked for less, or for none.
  • Arrangements about the children change at the last minute, again and again.
  • Money you’re owed arrives late, short or not at all, often around important dates.
  • You’ve had more court applications, letters or complaints than the situation seems to need.
  • People you know have been told things about you that aren’t true.
  • They seem to know where you’ve been, or who you’ve seen.
  • You feel watched, even when you can’t say how.
  • You change your plans, your route or your words to avoid a reaction.

One or two can happen in a hard separation. Several, together and over months, look like a pattern. Bring the list to a domestic abuse service and ask, “Does this sound like post-separation abuse to you?”

What does it do to you?

Post-separation abuse is often described as a slow drain, not a single blow.

  • Tiredness. Constant alertness wears the body out. See What your nervous system is doing.
  • Money. Legal costs, lost work and unpaid support add up over years.
  • Health. Many people get ill more often after leaving. See Getting sick after leaving.
  • Work. Court dates, messages in work hours and poor sleep make jobs harder to hold.
  • The children. They feel the strain in both homes. See How children react.
  • Your sense of yourself. Being doubted by courts or friends can make you doubt yourself.

Naming the drain helps. It lets you plan for it and ask for the right help, without blaming yourself for being tired.

Why do systems miss it?

Courts, police and schools are often set up to look at single incidents. Post-separation abuse rarely has a single incident that looks serious on its own. A late handover, a sharp email and a complaint to the school can each seem small.

Many systems also start from the idea that both parents are equally responsible for “conflict.” That framing can hide one person controlling the other.

What helps is showing the pattern: dates, what happened, and what it changed for you or the children. What to document and how and Family court, honestly cover how.

How common is post-separation abuse?

There’s no single reliable figure. Studies use different definitions and groups of people. What research consistently finds is that separation is a time of raised risk, and that many people who experienced coercive control report it continuing after they left. Why it often gets worse first covers the risk.

Does it end?

For many people it fades or stops. For some it continues for years in a smaller form. When it stops is honest about the range and what tends to help.

What can I do about it?

  • Write things down as they happen: What to document and how.
  • Make a safety plan for life after leaving: A safety plan for after you’ve left.
  • Decide how much contact you’ll have, and keep it in writing: No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid.
  • Get legal advice from someone who understands this pattern: Legal help with little or no money.
  • Look up a word or a question: A to Z and Questions people ask.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine describes the same pattern, which it calls the retaliation phase, in its chapter on post-separation control.

One year, one page

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

After Daniel left, his ex-wife texted constantly at first, then stopped when he moved contact to an app. Three months later she applied to court to change the children’s arrangements. The application was dismissed. Two months after that, his employer received an anonymous complaint about him. Then child support became irregular, always late in the weeks before school trips.

No single event looked like abuse to his friends. When Daniel laid his record out on one page, the pattern was clear: each time one road closed, another opened. That page was what finally made his solicitor and the court understand.

Three questions about your own year

  • Since we separated, has contact gone down, or found new routes?
  • How much of my week goes on dealing with them?
  • If a friend described this to me, what would I call it?

Why it often gets worse first

Is leaving an abusive relationship the most dangerous time?

The short answer

Leaving an abusive relationship can be the most dangerous time, especially with a very controlling partner. Losing control is what drives the escalation. So plan for safety now; staying doesn’t make you safer.

You expected things to calm down. Instead the messages doubled, the threats got sharper, or the court papers arrived. Many people find the first months after leaving are the hardest of all.

Read this as a reason to plan, and never as a reason to go back. If you’re in danger now, go to If you’re not safe right now.

Is leaving an abusive relationship the most dangerous time?

For many people, yes. Leaving an abusive relationship is linked with a higher risk of serious harm, especially in the weeks and months straight after, and especially when the partner was very controlling.

A large US study of women killed by partners found that separating from a highly controlling partner after living together was one of the strongest risk factors. Other studies of separated women report the same pattern: harassment and threats often rise around the time of leaving.

This doesn’t mean harm is likely for you. Most people who leave aren’t seriously hurt. It means this period deserves a plan.

Why does it get worse after leaving?

Control was the point of the relationship for the person you left. When you go, they lose most of it at once. Some people respond by trying harder to get it back. The more of their sense of themselves depended on controlling you, the harder they push.

You may notice:

  • more messages, calls and contact through other people
  • threats, sometimes about the children, money or your reputation
  • “emergencies” that need your attention right now
  • turning up at your work, home or the school gate
  • court applications that arrive in a cluster
  • a sudden switch to charm and apology, which is covered in Hoovering

The Decoder’s page on intimidation describes the threats and shows of force that often come with this.

When is the risk usually highest?

Risk tends to rise around moments when the person you left feels their control slipping further:

  • the first weeks and months after you leave
  • when they learn where you live
  • court dates and decisions about the children
  • when you start a new relationship, or they hear about one
  • anniversaries, birthdays and holidays
  • when you stop replying

Knowing these moments in advance lets you plan around them. A safety plan for after you’ve left covers how.

Add to that list: handovers of the children, and the day an order is served or a decision goes against them. For each moment, ask yourself three things. Who knows where I’ll be? How will I get there and back? What will I do if they turn up?

How do services assess risk?

Domestic abuse services and police often use a structured set of questions to judge risk. You may be asked them, and it helps to know why.

  • United Kingdom: many services use the DASH checklist. Police in England and Wales increasingly use a newer tool called DARA. A high score can lead to a MARAC, a meeting where police, health, housing and support services plan together to keep you safe.
  • United States and Canada: many services use the Danger Assessment, developed by Jacquelyn Campbell, or a short version used by police called the Lethality Assessment Program.
  • Elsewhere: ask, “Do you use a risk assessment? Can we do one together?”

The questions cover things like strangulation, threats to kill, weapons, stalking, jealousy and whether the violence is getting worse. Answer as honestly as you can. A higher score isn’t a judgment on you. It opens more help.

What if they seem calm?

Sometimes the person you left goes quiet, or becomes friendly. That can be real. It can also be a pause, or a way to get you to drop your guard.

Calm on its own isn’t a reason to drop your plan. Watch what they do over weeks, not what they say in one message. If the calm comes with questions about where you live, who you’re seeing, or a sudden wish to “talk properly, just once,” keep your safety steps in place. See Hoovering.

Telling police or a service about risk

Say exactly what happened, plainly. Professionals are trained to act on facts, not adjectives.

Say: “I left my partner on [date]. Since then they have [threatened to kill me / followed me / turned up at my home]. In the past they [strangled me / used a weapon / hit me while I was pregnant]. I’m frightened for my safety. I’d like a risk assessment.”

If anything in the past included strangulation or choking, say so even if it was once and long ago. It’s one of the strongest warning signs there is.

What signs mean I should get help quickly?

Some signs carry more weight than others. Some signs call for help now, not later: threats to kill you or themselves, past strangling or choking, access to a weapon, stalking, and violence that’s getting worse. Call a domestic abuse service or the police. Warning signs to take seriously lists them and explains what to do.

Does this mean I left badly?

No. The escalation comes from their loss of control, not from anything you did wrong. There’s no way of leaving that guarantees a calm response from someone who relied on controlling you.

Knowing this can’t make it easier, but it can take away one weight: the idea that you caused it.

What helps this week?

  1. Make a plan. Use A safety plan for after you’ve left.
  2. Keep a record. Write down each incident the same day. What to document and how shows a four-line format.
  3. Talk to a domestic abuse service. They know the local options, from refuges to protective orders. Find help has numbers.
  4. Narrow the contact. Fewer channels means fewer openings. See No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine lists the same risk questions in its chapter on post-separation control.

What does planning for a risky moment look like?

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Priya’s court hearing was on a Thursday. Her ex had been calm for weeks, but she knew the hearing could change that. She told her domestic abuse worker, who arranged a separate waiting room. Her brother drove her there and back. She told her workplace she’d be out, and asked reception not to confirm her schedule to anyone. She left the children with a friend whose address her ex didn’t know.

The hearing went badly for him. That night there were thirty messages. Because of the plan, she was at her brother’s, the children were safe, and she screenshotted the messages for the record instead of answering them.

Your next three risky dates

Write down the next three dates that might be risky, and next to each one, who will know where you are.

The shape it takes when they can't reach you directly

Why is my ex still controlling me after the breakup?

The short answer

Post-separation control is what happens when one way of reaching you closes and the person you left finds another. It can look very different from what happened at home, which is why it’s easy to miss. The test is what it does, not what it looks like.

You blocked the number. The messages stopped. Then your mother called to say they’d been round “just to talk.” Then the school forwarded an email. Then a letter arrived from a solicitor.

One door closed. Others opened.

Why is my ex still controlling me?

Control in a coercive relationship was never about one method. It was about keeping influence over you. When the obvious route closes, the need behind it doesn’t go away. It finds the next route.

This isn’t always a cold plan. Sometimes it is. Often it’s a habit, a sense of being owed, or a refusal to accept that you get to decide. Either way, the effect on you is the same.

“Just go no contact” is sometimes complete protection. Sometimes it closes one door while several others stay open.

What are the channels?

Post-separation control usually travels along one or more of these roads.

ChannelWhat it can look likeChapter
Childrenhandover arguments, questioning the children, schedule gamesChildren as the channel
Moneylate support, debts in your name, a house sale that never endsMoney as the channel
The storytelling people you’re unstable or dangerousThe narrative campaign
Other peoplerelatives and friends carrying messagesYour family and mutual friends
The courtsrepeated filings and delaysSystems abuse and litigation abuse
Technologytracking, account access, camerasSurveillance and technology
The returnapologies, emergencies, nostalgiaHoovering

Two Decoder pages describe the mechanics. Punishment by proxy is when other people or systems deliver the pressure. Monitoring and surveillance covers the watching.

Why is it so easy to miss?

Because it looks nothing like the old arguments. A custody application doesn’t feel like being shouted at. A late payment doesn’t feel like being watched. Even professionals meant to help can treat each incident as a separate, ordinary dispute.

The pattern only shows when you line the incidents up. That’s one reason a record matters so much: What to document and how.

How can I tell control from ordinary friction?

Ask one question of each incident: does it work to keep control over me, or to unsettle me, whatever it looks like?

A late payment during a real job loss is a problem. A late payment that always lands the week of your exams, every time you set a boundary, is a message.

Not every hard moment after a separation is abuse. Separations involve real disagreements. Abuse, or a hard breakup? helps you sort one from the other honestly, which also helps you be believed.

One month of reach: a worked example

Here’s how it can look when no single thing seems serious.

  • Week 1: a polite email about swapping a weekend. You say no. Two days later, a message to your sister: “Is she okay? I’m worried about her.”
  • Week 2: the child support payment arrives four days late. A comment on a photo you’re tagged in.
  • Week 3: a letter from their solicitor raising your parenting. The school calls to “check in” after a conversation with your ex.
  • Week 4: a warm message on your birthday. When you don’t reply, a long one about how you’re hurting the children.

Taken one at a time, each could be explained. Put on one page, they form a shape: a person keeping you busy, doubted and answering to them. That page is the most useful thing you can make.

How do the channels feed each other?

The channels rarely stay separate. A late payment leads to a message about money. The message leads to an argument at the handover. The argument becomes a story told to friends. The story turns up in a court statement.

That’s why handling each problem alone can feel endless. When you see the links, you can close the doors that matter most: move contact to writing, put money arrangements through an official service, and keep a record. Grey rock and written-only contact and Money as the channel cover two of the biggest.

When a professional becomes the channel

Sometimes the reach comes through people whose job is to help. A solicitor writes letters that seem designed to provoke. A therapist they’re seeing contacts you. A teacher repeats something your ex told them.

These professionals may not know what’s going on. Stay calm and factual with them:

Say: “Thank you for getting in touch. There’s a history of domestic abuse, and I’m keeping contact with [name] to arrangements about the children, in writing. I’m happy to answer questions about [child]’s schooling.”

You don’t have to argue your whole case. You can ask them to put questions in writing. If a professional repeatedly takes sides without knowing the facts, you can complain to their organisation.

What to write down when you spot a new channel

Add it to your record in four lines:

  1. When: the date and time.
  2. What: what happened, in plain words, or a screenshot.
  3. Who: who saw it or passed it on.
  4. What it changed: what you had to do, miss or explain because of it.

Over time, a list of channels with dates is far more persuasive than any single incident. The record has a template.

What do I do once I see the pattern?

  • Name it, privately. Seeing the shape takes some of its power away.
  • Close the channels you can. Some you can shut completely; others, like contact about children, you can narrow to writing. See No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid.
  • Write it down. Dates and facts, kept somewhere safe.
  • Respond less. Each reply teaches them which channel still works. See Messages you don’t have to answer.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine sorts the same channels into stalking, legal abuse, reputation and proxies in its chapter on post-separation control.

Which door would you close first?

  • Which channels does the person I left still use?
  • Which one costs me the most time, money or sleep?
  • Which single door could I close first?

Closing one channel won’t end the pattern. It gives you back some ground, and shows you which way they’ll try next.

Is my ex a narcissist?

What does a narcissist do after a breakup, and does the label matter?

The short answer

People search “narcissist after breakup” because the word names a pattern they lived through. Only a clinician can diagnose narcissistic personality disorder, and you don’t need a diagnosis to protect yourself. What matters is the behaviour, and the behaviour after a breakup is predictable.

If you’ve typed “narcissist” into a search bar at 3am, you’re in very large company. The word has become the shorthand for a certain kind of person: charming, then cruel; sorry, then cruel again; never wrong.

It’s a useful word. It’s also a limited one.

Is my ex a narcissist?

Maybe. Maybe not. Narcissistic personality disorder is a diagnosis that a qualified clinician makes after assessing someone. Most people who behave badly after a breakup will never be assessed, and many controlling people wouldn’t meet the full criteria.

What you can know is what they did, how often, and what it did to you. That is enough.

You don’t need to know what’s wrong with them to protect yourself from what they do.

Why does the word help so much?

Because it names something. Before you had the word, you may have thought each awful moment was a one-off, or your fault. The word told you it was a pattern, that other people have seen it, and that it has a shape. That recognition matters.

What are the limits of the label?

  • Courts and services work with behaviour, not labels. “He’s a narcissist” will get you less traction than “he has sent 140 messages since the order, including 12 threats.” See What to document and how.
  • It can make the person sound more powerful than they are. Online content about narcissists can make them seem all-knowing. They aren’t.
  • It can keep your attention on them. Months of reading about narcissism can become another way your life revolves around them.

What does a narcissist do after a breakup?

Whatever the diagnosis, the patterns people describe after a breakup with a controlling partner are consistent. Here they are in plain language, with where to read more.

They go silent, or they block you. The silent treatment is a punishment and a test: will you chase? See Messages you don’t have to answer.

They tell a story about you. You’re unstable, you cheated, you’re keeping the children from them. See The narrative campaign and the Decoder’s page on smear campaigns.

They turn it around. You’re the abuser, you’re the one who hurt them. This is often called DARVO: deny, attack, reverse victim and offender. See the Decoder’s DARVO and When they say you’re the abuser.

They move on fast. A new partner appears within weeks, sometimes days. It can feel like proof you were nothing. It usually means they needed someone, and you weren’t available any more. See If they left you.

They come back. The warm message, the apology, the emergency, usually when you’ve just started to feel steady. See Hoovering and the Decoder’s hoovering.

Do they come back?

Often, yes. A return after silence is so common it has a name. The timing tends to follow your recovery, not their growth. Hoovering covers the forms it takes and how to answer, or not.

Why did they block me?

Blocking can be a way of having the last word, of punishing you, or of making you chase. It can also simply mean they’ve moved their attention. Whatever the reason, a block does one useful thing: it closes a channel. You don’t have to reopen it.

What is the clinical diagnosis?

Picture a psychiatrist’s office, an assessment that takes hours, and a manual open on the desk. That’s what a diagnosis involves. Narcissistic personality disorder (NPD) is a mental health diagnosis in the DSM-5, the main psychiatric manual used in the US and many other countries. To be diagnosed, a person shows a long-standing pattern of grandiosity, a need for admiration and a lack of empathy, across most areas of life, starting by early adulthood. Only a trained clinician who assesses them can make the diagnosis.

The diagnosable disorder is uncommon. Estimates vary a lot between studies, from under 1 in 100 people to around 6 in 100. Many more people have some narcissistic traits without having the disorder.

So when you search “narcissist,” you’re mostly reading about a pattern of behaviour, not a diagnosis. That’s fine. The pattern is what affects you.

Narcissist, psychopath, sociopath, borderline: why the labels blur

Type any of these words into your phone at 2am and the same videos come up, often with the same thumbnails. Online, these words get used almost interchangeably. Clinically they’re different:

  • Narcissistic personality disorder: grandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy.
  • “Psychopath” and “sociopath”: not diagnoses in the DSM. They usually refer to antisocial personality disorder, which involves disregard for others’ rights, deceit and lack of remorse.
  • Borderline personality disorder: intense fear of abandonment, unstable relationships and emotions. Many people with this diagnosis are far more often harmed than harmful.

For your safety and your case, the label matters less than the behaviour. “She sent 60 messages in a day after I said no” is clearer to everyone than any diagnosis.

What if they have a real diagnosis or mental illness?

Some abusive people do have mental health conditions. Most people with mental health conditions aren’t abusive.

A condition can explain some things, like mood or impulsivity. It rarely explains a pattern of control: the planning, the choice of targets, the way the behaviour changes when other people are watching. Writers who work with abusive men, such as Lundy Bancroft in Why Does He Do That?, describe abuse as coming from beliefs and entitlement more than from illness.

You can feel compassion for someone’s struggles and still keep yourself safe from what they do. Their diagnosis doesn’t make their behaviour your job to manage.

What if they call you the narcissist?

It might arrive as a text, a comment to a mutual friend, or a line in a court statement. It’s common. After a break-up, the person who controlled the relationship sometimes uses the language of abuse against the person who left. It can be a deliberate tactic, or it can be how they see it.

If it shakes you, read Abuse, or a hard breakup? and its section “Am I the abuser?” People who are worried they might be abusive, and look at it honestly, are usually not the person running the pattern. If you’re being accused to professionals, read When they say you’re the abuser.

What does “narcissistic abuse recovery” mean?

It’s a popular phrase for recovering from a relationship with someone who controlled, belittled and manipulated you. You’ll find books, videos and groups built around it.

Some of that material is helpful. Some sells quick fixes, or keeps you focused on the other person’s diagnosis for years. A good sign is anything that moves your attention back to your own life: your safety, your sleep, your people, your choices. That’s what this book tries to do. Start with What “recovered” means.

Can a narcissist change?

People can change, but deep change in long-standing patterns is slow and rare. It shows up as consistent behaviour over a long time, not as promises. If you’re wondering whether someone has changed, look for accountability without pressure on you. Trust as a practice offers a careful way to judge.

So what should I call it?

Call it what helps you. In court, with a lawyer or with a domestic abuse service, describe behaviour. In your own head, use the word that makes it make sense. The Weighing, another book in this library, covers how to judge people fairly when you’re not sure.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine lays out the full cycle these patterns belong to in The Manipulation Cycle.

When the label starts doing your thinking

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

A label can bring huge relief. It can also start to steer what you see. Ask yourself:

  • Do I now read everything they do as proof, even the ordinary things?
  • Am I spending more time studying them than looking after myself?
  • Would my next step change if they had no label at all?
  • Is the label helping me act, or keeping me stuck in the past?

If the label helps you name a pattern and protect yourself, keep it. If it’s become a full-time study of them, gently turn your attention back to your own days.

When the label takes over

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

For a year after leaving, Jo watched videos about narcissists every night. Each one explained something, and each one made her feel worse. She spent hours trying to work out whether her ex was “covert” or “malignant,” and whether he’d ever change.

What helped was a support worker who asked a different question: “Whatever he is, what do you need to be safe and well?” Jo started writing down what he did, not what he was. She set up written contact and a safety plan. The label stopped mattering much. Her life started to matter more.

Behaviour is enough

You don’t need to diagnose anyone to protect yourself. Behaviour is enough.

Abuse, or a hard breakup?

How do I tell a normal breakup from a narcissist breakup, or from abuse?

The short answer

The difference between a normal breakup and a narcissist breakup, or any abusive one, is pattern and purpose. Ordinary conflict runs both ways and eases with time. Abuse runs one way, keeps landing where it hurts most, and serves to keep control.

Every separation hurts. People argue over money and children, say things they’d take back, and grieve. So how do you know when it’s more than that?

You can test it fairly, without waiting for anyone else’s verdict. Being fair matters for your own peace of mind, and it matters when you need other people, including a court, to believe you.

What does a normal breakup look like?

Normal breakups can be ugly. They often include:

  • arguments about money, the house, the children and who gets what
  • anger, blame and bad days on both sides
  • one person grieving more, or longer, than the other
  • slow, clumsy agreements that eventually stick
  • contact that gets less heated over months

What marks an ordinary breakup is that the conflict goes both ways and runs down. Both people are hurt. Both, at times, behave badly. Over time it settles.

What does a narcissist breakup, or an abusive one, look like?

Whatever word you use for the person, an abusive breakup has a different shape:

  • One direction. The problems keep coming from one side.
  • Timing. Trouble arrives when you’re doing well, when you set a limit, or when you won’t answer.
  • Purpose. Each incident leaves you more drained, more afraid or more tied to them.
  • No settling. Agreements get broken or reopened. Calm stretches end the moment you relax.
  • Fear. You plan your life around their reactions.

In ordinary conflict, people argue about the issue. In control, the issue keeps changing and the target stays you.

The three-question test

For any incident, ask:

  1. Pattern: has this, or something like it, happened before? How often?
  2. Purpose: what did it achieve for them? Contact, control, punishment, an audience?
  3. Effect: what did it do to you? Did it change what you did, where you went, what you said?

One late payment is a late payment. Late payments that always arrive in the week of your job interview, after every boundary you set, for two years, are a pattern with a purpose. Write the answers down. Over time, the record will show you which you’re dealing with. See What to document and how.

What if it’s both?

Often it is. A real disagreement about schooling can sit alongside a pattern of control. You can take the disagreement seriously and still name the control.

Be careful of a word that gets used a lot here: “high-conflict.” It makes two people sound equally responsible. Where one person is driving it, the Decoder calls that framing a false equivalence. You can say plainly: “There are real disagreements, and there’s also a pattern of control coming from one side.”

Am I the abuser?

Many people who were controlled ask this. Years of being told that everything was your fault can leave you checking yourself for blame. The Decoder describes how that self-doubt gets planted in self-gaslighting induction.

Some honest questions:

  • Were you afraid of them, or were they afraid of you?
  • Did you change your life to avoid their reactions, or did they change theirs to avoid yours?
  • When you shouted or snapped, was it after months of provocation? (This is sometimes called reactive abuse. See When they say you’re the abuser.)
  • Do you want to control what they do now, or do you want them to leave you alone?

If you read those and feel relief, that tells you something. If you read them and recognise yourself in the other side, that’s worth facing too. The Weighing has a careful chapter, Am I the one doing it?, and The Repair is written for people who want to stop harming someone.

Side by side: a hard breakup and an abusive one

In a hard breakupIn an abusive one
Arguments are about an issue, and the issue can be settledThe issue keeps changing; settling one starts another
Both people are upset, then gradually less soOne person’s anger grows when the other moves on
Contact drops off over timeContact rises when you set limits
Each person tells friends their sideOne person works to turn friends against the other
Children’s arrangements settle into a routineArrangements are used to unsettle you
Money is argued about, then agreedMoney is withheld, delayed or used to punish
You feel sad, angry, relievedYou feel watched, frightened, or that you can’t win

No one fits one column perfectly. Look at which column describes most of the last six months.

A week of noticing

If you’re unsure, keep a short note each day for a week. It takes two minutes:

  • What contact did we have, and who started it?
  • Did anything happen that I had to change my plans for?
  • Did I feel afraid, or careful about what I said?
  • Did anything they said to others reach me?
  • How did I feel after the contact: settled, or shaken for hours?

At the end of the week, read it back as if a friend had written it. What would you say to them?

What does a professional look at?

Domestic abuse workers and some family court professionals are trained to look past single arguments. They usually ask about:

  • Fear. Is one person frightened of the other?
  • Control. Does one person restrict the other’s money, movement, friends or decisions?
  • Pattern. Is this repeated over time, or a few bad weeks?
  • Escalation. Is it getting worse after separation?
  • Children. Are the children being used, or put at risk?

If most of your answers point one way, that’s useful information. Take it to a domestic abuse service, which can talk it through without anyone else involved.

If you were both at your worst at the end

Many separations end badly on both sides. You may have shouted, said cruel things, or done something you’re ashamed of. That doesn’t automatically make it mutual abuse, and it doesn’t erase what happened to you.

The question is the pattern across the relationship, not the worst week of it. Who was afraid? Who adjusted their life to avoid the other’s reactions? Who controlled the money and the decisions? If you reacted to months of provocation, read the section on reactive abuse in When they say you’re the abuser.

Owning what you did at the end is a strength. It’s also separate from accepting blame for a pattern you didn’t create.

Why does getting this right matter?

Because it protects you. Professionals see a lot of separations where both people claim to be the victim. A calm account of a pattern, with dates and details, stands out from a list of grievances. It also keeps your own thinking clear on the days when they’re charming and you start to doubt yourself.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine runs the same check, which it calls manipulation versus friction, in its reality check.

What next?

  • If it’s a pattern: What post-separation abuse is.
  • If it’s mostly ordinary conflict: No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid still helps you keep it calm.
  • If you’re unsure: start a record and look again in a month.

Looking at it straight, both ways

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

Pain bends the view in both directions. Some people shrink what happened so they don’t have to face it. Others, hurt and exhausted, see control in every late reply. Ask yourself:

  • If a friend told me this exact story, what would I call it?
  • Am I leaving out the worst parts when I tell it, or adding to the hard parts?
  • When I picture their behaviour, am I thinking of the pattern, or one bad night?
  • Is there anything I did that I’d want to own, separate from what they did?

There’s no score. If the answers surprise you, take them to a domestic abuse service or a counsellor and think them through out loud.

A week of notes, read back

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Sam and Alex’s breakup was messy. They both said cruel things in the last month. Sam wondered whether he was being dramatic to call it abuse. He kept a week of notes. Alex had messaged forty times, mostly at night. Three messages mentioned where Sam had been that day. Alex had told Sam’s mother he was drinking again, which wasn’t true. When Sam set a limit, the messages doubled.

Reading it back, Sam saw a pattern he hadn’t let himself see. He called a helpline, not to label Alex, but to ask what to do next.

Three questions that cut through the noise

  • Who was afraid of whom, most of the time?
  • When I set a limit, what happened next?
  • Am I calmer or more frightened as time passes?

If your story doesn't look like the usual one

Does post-separation abuse happen to men, LGBTQ+ people, and people leaving their families?

The short answer

Post-separation abuse against men is real, and so is abuse after same-sex relationships, abuse by parents and families, and abuse that uses immigration status or disability. Most of this book applies to you. This chapter covers what’s different and where to find help made for your situation.

A lot of writing about leaving abuse assumes one story: a woman leaving a man she lived with. That story is common. It isn’t the only one.

If the examples in this book don’t look like your life, the patterns probably still do. Here’s what changes when your story is different.

Does post-separation abuse happen to men?

Men are abused by partners too, by women and by men. In England and Wales, official estimates suggest around one in three adults who experience domestic abuse in a year are men.

Men often meet extra barriers after leaving:

  • being disbelieved, or laughed at
  • being treated as the likely aggressor by default
  • fewer services, and fewer refuges that take men
  • a story from the ex that he’s the dangerous one, which people find easy to believe

The patterns in this book apply in full. The Silence, another book in this library, is written for men and covers recognition and help in depth. In the UK, the Men’s Advice Line is on 0808 801 0327. In the US, the National Domestic Violence Hotline serves everyone.

What about after a same-sex or queer relationship?

Abuse happens in lesbian, gay, bi and trans relationships too. Rates of abuse in LGBTQ+ relationships are similar to, or higher than, rates in straight relationships. Some tactics are particular to these relationships:

  • threatening to out you to family, work or your community
  • using a small community against you, where everyone knows both of you
  • telling services it was “mutual,” and services that expect a man and a woman and miss what happened
  • for trans people, misgendering, attacks on transition, or threats about documents and healthcare

The Decoder’s page on identity-based isolation covers tactics that use who you are against you. LGBTQ+ domestic abuse services exist in many places; in the UK, Galop runs a helpline on 0800 999 5428. Elsewhere, search “LGBTQ domestic abuse support [your area].”

Leaving a controlling parent or family

Many people reading this left a family, not a partner. A parent who controlled money, friends, faith or marriage. Siblings who report back.

The patterns are close cousins: guilt, contact through relatives, a story about you told at every gathering, and returns dressed as “we just miss you.” Read Your family and mutual friends and Your parents.

Honour-based abuse and forced marriage

If your family or community controls you in the name of honour, or is pressuring you to marry, the danger after leaving can come from several people at once. Specialist services understand this. In the UK, Karma Nirvana runs a helpline on 0800 5999 247, and the Forced Marriage Unit gives advice, including for people at risk of being taken abroad. Elsewhere, search “forced marriage help [your country].”

When immigration status is used against you

“Leave and you’ll be deported” is a common threat. It’s often untrue, and many countries have protections for people whose status depended on an abusive partner:

  • United States: the Violence Against Women Act lets some survivors apply for status without the abuser’s help (a self-petition), and there are U visas for victims of certain crimes.
  • United Kingdom: the Migrant Victims of Domestic Abuse Concession gives some people temporary access to benefits while they apply to stay.

Get advice from an immigration lawyer or a domestic abuse service before acting. The Decoder’s page on status threats describes how this pressure works.

If you’re pregnant or have a new baby

See Pregnant, or with a new baby.

If you’re disabled, or you were their carer, or they were yours

Abuse can involve controlling medication, mobility aids, care or money. Leaving can mean losing care at the same time. Ask a domestic abuse service about accessible refuge, and ask your local social care team about emergency support.

If you’re under 18

Abuse in teenage relationships is real abuse, and you deserve help made for you. In the US, love is respect offers support by phone (1-866-331-9474) or text (LOVEIS to 22522). In the UK, Childline is on 0800 1111. Elsewhere, search “teen dating abuse help [your country].” If you’re in danger now, see If you’re not safe right now.

Older readers, rural readers, readers of faith

  • Older readers: The Slow Take covers coercion in later life, including money.
  • Rural readers: distance, few services and a small town where everyone knows your ex make everything harder. Phone and online services can fill some of the gap. See When you share a town, a workplace or a community.
  • Readers of faith: a congregation can be a support or a pressure. The Sacred Divide looks at control inside religious institutions.

If you’re neurodivergent

Autistic people and people with ADHD describe some particular difficulties:

  • being told your memory or perception is wrong, when you already doubt how you read people
  • routines being disrupted on purpose
  • sensory overload used against you, or your reactions to it called “aggression”
  • services that read a flat tone or direct speech as a sign you’re lying

You’re allowed to ask services for adjustments: written information, extra time, a quieter room, a support person. In court, you can ask the judge for adjustments to how you give evidence. Write things down as they happen; it helps everyone, and it helps you trust your own record. See What to document and how.

If English isn’t your first language

Most national domestic abuse helplines can arrange an interpreter. You don’t need to speak English well to call. Say the name of your language and wait.

  • Ask for a professional interpreter, not a family member or friend, especially your children. The person you left may know your community’s interpreters; you can ask for someone from outside it.
  • Many services have information translated into common languages. Ask for it.
  • Courts usually provide interpreters for hearings. Ask early.

If you or they are in the armed forces

Military life adds its own pressures: frequent moves, living on a base, a chain of command, and fear that reporting will end a career.

  • United States: each installation has a Family Advocacy Program for domestic abuse. You can choose a restricted report, which gets you support without starting an investigation, or an unrestricted one.
  • United Kingdom and elsewhere: service welfare teams and civilian domestic abuse services both help. You don’t have to go through the chain of command to get support.

A civilian helpline can explain your options before you decide anything.

If you have no money, no papers or a record

None of these stops you getting help.

  • No money: helplines, refuges and many legal services are free. See Legal help with little or no money.
  • No papers, or a visa tied to them: read “When immigration status is used against you” above. In the UK, ask about the Migrant Victims of Domestic Abuse Concession. In the US, ask about VAWA and U visas. Talk to an immigration adviser before you tell an authority about your status.
  • A criminal record: many refuges and services still accept you. Ask. Past convictions don’t remove your right to protection.

If the person was an adult child or another relative

Abuse doesn’t only come from partners. An adult son or daughter, a sibling, an in-law or a grandchild can control, frighten or take money from you. Leaving or cutting contact with a family member brings its own grief and guilt.

Most of this book still applies: the safety planning, the record, contact in writing, the legal protections. Many protective orders cover family members as well as partners. If you’re older, read “Older readers” above, and see The Slow Take, another book in this library, which covers coercion in later life.

If you’re a teenager and your parent has left

If one of your parents has left an abusive relationship, this is your story too. You may feel relieved, angry, guilty, protective or confused, sometimes all at once.

  • It isn’t your job to protect your parent or fix the situation.
  • You’re allowed to love both parents, even if one of them hurt the family.
  • You don’t have to pass messages, report on either home or choose sides.
  • If you feel unsafe at either home, tell a trusted adult: a teacher, a school counsellor, a relative.
  • In the UK you can call Childline on 0800 1111. In the US you can text love is respect or call a youth crisis line.

You can read any chapter in this book. Your children, and what to say and How children react are written for parents, but you might find yourself in them.

The rest of this book is yours too

Whoever you are and whoever you left, the chapters on safety, contact, records, courts, your body and rebuilding apply. Start at Where are you right now?.

Ask anyway

If your story doesn’t match the examples you’ve seen, the help is still meant for you. Ask the question anyway: “Does this sound like abuse to you?” Most helplines would far rather hear from you than not.

The first 30 days

What do I do in the first month after leaving an abusive relationship?

The short answer

This is an after leaving an abusive relationship checklist for the first month: safety first, then your phone and accounts, documents, money, children, and your record. Do what you can. Nothing on this list has to be done in a single day.

The first weeks after leaving are a blur. This after leaving an abusive relationship checklist is for that blur. You’re tired, you may be in a new place, and everything feels urgent. This list puts things in a sensible order. Tick off what applies. Skip what doesn’t. Come back to it.

Keep the list somewhere the person you left can’t see it.

What do I do in the first three days?

  • Save emergency and helpline numbers under plain names. See If you’re not safe right now.
  • Tell one or two trusted people where you are and agree a code word that means “call for help.”
  • Make a basic safety plan: routes, routines, who collects the children. A safety plan for after you’ve left.
  • If there’s any risk of violence, talk to a domestic abuse service or the police about a protective order. Protective orders.

Days 1 to 7: your phone and accounts

Start with your email. It’s the key that resets everything else.

  • Change your email password. Turn on two-step login. Store the backup codes on paper.
  • Then change passwords for banking, social media, cloud storage, shopping and streaming.
  • Check which devices are logged in to each account, and log out the ones you don’t recognise.
  • Turn off location sharing with them on your phone, in apps and on social media.
  • Check for tracking: Surveillance and technology.

One warning: sudden changes can alert someone who is watching. If you’re worried about how they’ll react, plan the order with a domestic abuse service. The Decoder’s page on account control explains how shared accounts become a lead.

Days 1 to 14: documents

Gather originals, or photograph them if you can’t take them yet:

  • passports, ID, birth certificates (yours and the children’s)
  • marriage, divorce, custody or protective orders
  • bank statements, pay slips, tax records, pension details
  • tenancy or mortgage papers, car documents
  • medical records and prescriptions
  • any evidence of abuse you already have

Keep one copy somewhere safe away from home and one encrypted copy online in an account only you can open.

Days 1 to 30: money

  • Open a bank account in your name only, at a bank you didn’t share.
  • Move your pay to it, and change any direct debits that matter.
  • Get your credit report (free in most countries) and look for accounts you didn’t open. See Money as the channel.
  • List what you owe and what’s in joint names. Untangling: the house, the car, the accounts and the paperwork covers the longer job.
  • Ask about emergency funds and benefits. See Housing and benefits.

Days 1 to 30: children

  • Tell the school or nursery who may and may not collect your children. Give them a copy of any court order.
  • Decide how contact with the other parent will happen, ideally in writing. See Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.
  • Give the children simple, honest reassurance. See Your children, and what to say.

Days 1 to 30: start your record

Start a record now, even if nothing has happened yet. Date, what happened, who saw it, what it changed. The record has a template. A month of plain notes is worth more later than a long account written from memory.

Days 7 to 30: people and paperwork

  • Change your address with your doctor, bank and employer. If your address must stay hidden, see Your address, your records, your mail.
  • Update emergency contacts at work, at school and on medical records.
  • Check who is named as beneficiary on any life insurance or pension.
  • Ask your employer about leave or safety support. See Work again.

The one-page version

Copy this, or print it, and tick as you go:

  • ☐ Somewhere safe to sleep tonight
  • ☐ Emergency number and one helpline saved under plain names
  • ☐ Passwords changed on email, bank and phone account; location sharing off
