Can a court stop repeated applications?

Yes, in many places:

  • England and Wales: family courts can make a barring order under section 91(14) of the Children Act 1989, so a parent needs permission before making new applications. The Domestic Abuse Act 2021 made clear these orders can be used to protect victims of abuse. Civil courts can also make civil restraint orders.
  • United States: many states let courts declare someone a vexatious litigant, so they need permission to file. Some states, including Georgia and Washington, have laws aimed at abusive litigation in family cases.
  • Australia: family courts can restrict proceedings considered vexatious.
  • Elsewhere: search “[your country] vexatious litigant family court.”

Ask your lawyer whether it’s worth applying for one.

What is legal abuse syndrome?

“Legal abuse syndrome” is a term made popular by Karin Huffer, a US family therapist, in a 1995 book. It describes the stress, anxiety and sense of injustice that long or abusive court cases can cause.

It isn’t a medical diagnosis, and it isn’t in the DSM-5, the main psychiatric manual, despite some claims online. That doesn’t mean what you feel isn’t real. Years of filings, hearings and waiting can cause symptoms that look a lot like trauma: dread when the post arrives, poor sleep, trouble thinking straight.

You can’t usually sue someone for “legal abuse syndrome” itself. What you can do is ask the court to deal with the litigation: costs orders, limits on new applications, or a finding that the case is vexatious. The section above on stopping repeated applications explains how. For the effects on your health, see What your nervous system is doing.

The common forms

Litigation and systems abuse can take many shapes:

  • repeated applications to change arrangements for the children
  • appeals of every decision
  • refusing to agree anything, so every small matter goes to court
  • deliberate delays, missed deadlines and last-minute adjournments
  • complaints about you to police, social services, your employer or a professional regulator
  • reports to child protection that turn out to have no basis
  • piles of documents served at the last minute

Any one of these can happen in a normal case. A run of them, aimed at you, is a pattern.

Complaints to your employer or regulator

If you work in a regulated job, like nursing, teaching or law, the person you left may complain about you to your regulator. Tell your employer or regulator early that there’s a history of domestic abuse and an ongoing court case. Keep a copy of everything. Most regulators see malicious complaints and know how to handle them. A union or professional body can help.

Orders that limit repeated applications

  • England and Wales: courts can make a section 91(14) order under the Children Act 1989, which stops someone applying again without permission. Since the Domestic Abuse Act 2021, the law makes clear these orders can be used to protect a child or a parent from harm caused by repeated applications.
  • United States: many states have vexatious litigant rules that require someone to get a judge’s permission before filing new cases.
  • Elsewhere: search “[your country] restrict repeated family court applications.”

Ask your lawyer, or raise it yourself at a hearing: “I’m asking the court to consider an order stopping further applications without permission.”

A one-page pattern summary for the court

Judges have little time. A one-page table can show what pages of statements can’t:

DateApplication or actionOutcomeEffect on the children or me
JanApplication to change contactDismissedThree days off work
MarComplaint to schoolNo actionChild questioned at school
MayAppealRefusedLegal costs

Ask whether you can file it with your statement.

Keeping your costs and energy down

  • One folder, physical or digital, for everything, in date order.
  • One list of deadlines, checked every Monday.
  • Answer what the court asks, no more.
  • Use free legal help lines between hearings.
  • Protect some time each week that has nothing to do with the case.

What helps while it’s happening?

  • A lawyer who has seen this before, and who treats each filing as part of a pattern rather than a fresh crisis. See Legal help with little or no money.
  • Respond to every deadline. Missing one can hand them a win.
  • Don’t match them. Counter-applications can make you both look high-conflict.
  • Pace yourself. This is often long. Plan your energy as if it will be.
  • Ask about protections in court, so you don’t have to face them alone. See Your day in court.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine calls it “paper terrorism” in its chapter on post-separation control, and shows how paperwork becomes a trap in The Administrative Cage.

When the applications kept coming

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

In eighteen months, Fiona’s ex made five applications about the children, appealed twice and complained to her professional body. Each one cost her time off work and nights of sleep. Her new solicitor asked her to make a one-page table: date, application, outcome, effect. At the next hearing, the judge read the table and made an order stopping further applications without permission.

It didn’t undo the eighteen months. It did give her the next two years back.

Make the table this week

Even if you have no hearing coming up, start the one-page table now. It’s much easier to keep up than to build in a hurry.

Legal help with little or no money

Where can I get free legal help for domestic violence?

The short answer

Free legal help for domestic violence exists in most countries: legal aid, domestic abuse legal services, law school clinics, free advice lines and court help desks. Many people who qualify never ask. Call one, say you’ve experienced domestic abuse, and ask what they can offer.

You look up a family lawyer’s fees, see the hourly rate, and close the page. The cost of a lawyer stops many people from asking for protections they’re entitled to. You may have more options than you think, and none of them are well advertised.

Where can I get free legal help for domestic violence?

Check each of these, one at a time.

Legal aid. Government-funded legal help for people on lower incomes. Many countries give priority or extra eligibility to people who have experienced domestic abuse.

  • England and Wales: legal aid for family cases is usually available if you pass the means test and can show evidence of domestic abuse. Evidence can be a protective order, a police report, or a letter from a doctor or a domestic abuse service.
  • Scotland: apply through the Scottish Legal Aid Board.
  • United States: legal aid organisations in every state help with protective orders and family law. LawHelp.org lists them by state.
  • Canada: each province has a legal aid plan, and many prioritise family violence cases.
  • Australia: Legal Aid commissions in each state, community legal centres, and specialist family violence legal services.
  • Ireland: the Legal Aid Board provides family law services.

Domestic abuse legal services. Many domestic abuse organisations have their own lawyers or advocates, or refer to lawyers who specialise in abuse.

Law school clinics. Supervised students give free help, often with family law and protective orders.

Pro bono schemes. Some law firms take on cases for free. Ask your local bar association or law society.

Court help desks and self-help centres. Many courts have free help with forms and procedures, and some domestic abuse services provide court support workers.

Free advice lines. In the UK, Rights of Women runs a family law advice line for women. Many countries have similar lines.

When you call, say “I’ve experienced domestic abuse.” It can open doors that a general enquiry doesn’t.

What do I say on the first call?

Say: “I’ve left an abusive relationship and I need help with [a protective order / custody / divorce / a court date on (date)]. My income is about [amount]. What can you offer, and what do I need to bring?”

Have ready:

  • any court papers and deadlines
  • your income and benefits details
  • any evidence of abuse you already have, such as police reference numbers or your record. See What to document and how.

What if I don’t qualify for free help?

  • Limited-scope help: some lawyers will do one part of a case, like a single hearing or checking your forms, for a fixed fee.
  • A one-off consultation: often cheaper, and enough to understand your options.
  • Representing yourself, with support from a court help desk or a domestic abuse advocate.

What if I have to represent myself?

Many people do. Some things help:

  • Read the court’s guide for people without lawyers. Most courts publish one.
  • Keep everything in a folder, in date order.
  • Ask for protections if you’re afraid of the other side. In England and Wales, an abuser can’t cross-examine you in person in family court. See Your day in court.
  • Bring a supporter. Many courts allow a McKenzie friend or support person.

How do I find the right lawyer?

Look for someone with experience of domestic abuse and coercive control, not just family law. Ask:

Say: “How much of your work involves domestic abuse or coercive control? How would you approach repeated applications from the other side?”

See Family court, honestly for why this matters, and Where to find the right body for other places that can help.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine lists legal help among the first steps in Appendix B, Liberation Logistics.

Other questions worth asking before you hire anyone:

  • How would you present a pattern, not just incidents?
  • How do you charge, and roughly what will this cost?
  • How quickly do you reply, and how will you keep me informed?
  • What will you need from me?

If they talk over you, dismiss the abuse, or push you to settle without hearing why you’re worried, keep looking if you can.

Legal aid when there’s been domestic abuse

  • England and Wales: legal aid for family cases, like child arrangements and finances, usually needs evidence of domestic abuse. Accepted evidence includes letters from a doctor, police, a domestic abuse service or a social worker, a protective order or a relevant conviction. You’ll also need to pass a means test. For protective orders, the financial rules can be more flexible.
  • Scotland and Northern Ireland: have their own legal aid systems. Ask a solicitor or the legal aid board.
  • United States: there’s no general right to a lawyer in family cases. Legal aid organisations, law school clinics, bar association referral services and domestic violence legal advocates can help. Many domestic violence programs have lawyers or can refer you.
  • Australia and Canada: legal aid commissions and community legal centres offer help, often with priority for family violence.

Keeping costs down

  • Arrive organised: a timeline, copies of key documents, your questions in writing.
  • Send one clear email with several questions, not many short ones.
  • Ask what you can do yourself, like collecting documents or drafting your timeline.
  • Ask for an estimate before each stage.

Help with only part of a case

Some lawyers offer limited-scope or “unbundled” help: advice for an hour, drafting one document, or representing you at one hearing, while you handle the rest. It can make expert help affordable for the parts that matter most.

If your lawyer isn’t listening

Tell them plainly in writing: “I’m worried that the history of abuse isn’t being presented to the court. I’d like to discuss how we’ll do that.” If nothing changes, you can change lawyers. In most places you can also complain to the lawyer’s firm, then to a regulator.

How Ahmed found help with no money

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Ahmed had no savings and a court date in three weeks. He called a domestic abuse helpline, which told him about a legal advice clinic at the local law school. The clinic helped him fill in the forms and explained what would happen at the hearing. His support worker wrote a letter confirming the abuse, which made him eligible for legal aid for the next stage.

He didn’t have a lawyer for every hearing. He had help at the moments that mattered most.

Your first call

Call one free legal help service this week, even if your hearing is months away. Ask, “What help can I get, and what do I need to bring?”

Family court, honestly

Does family court recognise coercive control?

The short answer

Family courts vary a lot in how well they recognise coercive control, as opposed to physical violence. Some have made real progress; others still push for contact with both parents and miss non-physical abuse. Get advice from a lawyer who knows coercive control, and present a pattern with dates.

You may have heard that family court will see through them. You may have heard that it never does. Neither is reliably true. What helps is an honest picture, so you can plan for the court you’ll stand in.

Does family court recognise coercive control?

More than it used to, and less than it should.

Some places have made real changes. In England and Wales, a major government review in 2020 looked at how family courts handle abuse. It found that courts often minimised domestic abuse, put too much weight on contact with both parents, and didn’t hear victims well. Reforms followed, including the Domestic Abuse Act 2021. In Canada, the Divorce Act now lists coercive and controlling behaviour as family violence that courts must consider. Several US states, including California, Hawaii and Connecticut, have written coercive control into their family or protective order laws. Australia’s family law changed in 2023 to put children’s safety first.

But practice varies by country, by court and by judge. Many courts still focus mostly on physical violence, and patterns of control without violence can be missed. The Decoder’s page on children used as leverage and legal abuse describe what courts often fail to see.

Neither “the court will understand” nor “the court never believes victims” is safe to assume. Find out how your court works.

Who will I meet?

Depending on where you are:

  • A judge or magistrates, who make the decisions.
  • A child’s representative: a guardian, a children’s lawyer or an independent children’s lawyer, who speaks for the child’s interests.
  • A family court adviser or evaluator, who interviews parents and children and writes a report. In England and Wales this is often Cafcass; in the US, a custody evaluator; in Australia, a family consultant.
  • A mediator, who tries to help parents agree.

Each of them is trying to work out what’s best for the children. A calm, clear account of the pattern, with the effect on the children, helps them most.

Should I go to mediation?

Not necessarily. Mediation assumes two people can negotiate fairly. Where there’s coercive control, that often isn’t true.

  • England and Wales: you usually have to attend a mediation meeting before applying to court, but domestic abuse is an exemption.
  • United States: many states let you opt out of mandatory mediation where there’s been domestic violence.
  • Australia: family dispute resolution is usually required, but there are exemptions for family violence.

If you do mediate, ask for separate rooms (“shuttle mediation”), and say you don’t feel safe negotiating directly.

Say: “There’s a history of domestic abuse and I don’t feel able to negotiate safely in the same room. I’d like an exemption, or shuttle mediation.”

How do I present a pattern rather than incidents?

Courts hear a lot of competing claims. What stands out is a calm summary backed by records:

  • Start with the pattern in one or two sentences. “Since we separated in May 2023, my ex has used the children’s handovers and the courts to keep control. There have been 23 late or missed handovers, 140 messages outside the agreed app, and five applications, four dismissed.”
  • Then the effect on the children. Courts decide for children, so tie each point to them.
  • Then the evidence, organised by type.
  • Avoid labels. Describe behaviour, not diagnoses. See Is my ex a narcissist?.

What to document and how shows how to build the timeline.

What about “parental alienation”?

You may be accused of turning the children against the other parent, especially if a child is reluctant to see them. This is one of the most disputed areas in family law.

What’s agreed: a parent can wrongly turn a child against the other parent, and that harms children. What’s disputed: how often it happens, how it should be judged, and whether claims of alienation are being used to discredit parents who raise abuse. Research in the US has found that when mothers alleged abuse and fathers then claimed alienation, courts often disbelieved the abuse.

If you’re accused:

  • stay calm and focus on the children
  • show that you support the children’s relationship with the other parent wherever it’s safe
  • explain the child’s reluctance with facts, not your interpretation
  • get a lawyer who knows this area well

See also When your child is pulled into it and When they say you’re the abuser.

How often do narcissists win in court?

People ask this a lot. There’s no reliable figure, and the word “narcissist” won’t be used in court. What’s true is that charming, confident people can do well in short hearings, and that calm, organised parents with clear records do better than anxious ones with long lists of grievances. You can’t control how they come across. You can control how prepared you are.

How decisions about children are made

Family courts make decisions based on the child’s welfare or “best interests.” The details differ by country, but courts usually consider:

  • the child’s needs, and their wishes depending on age and understanding
  • any harm the child has suffered or is at risk of
  • each parent’s ability to meet the child’s needs
  • the effect of any change

Domestic abuse is meant to be central to that assessment. In England and Wales, courts follow guidance called Practice Direction 12J when abuse is raised. In many US states, a finding of domestic violence affects custody decisions, sometimes with a presumption against custody for the abusive parent. Ask your lawyer or advocate how this works where you live.

Reports and evaluators

Before deciding, courts often ask a professional to report.

  • England and Wales: a Cafcass officer may write a “section 7” report after speaking to both parents and, often, the children.
  • United States: courts may appoint a custody evaluator or a guardian ad litem.
  • Elsewhere: family consultants or social workers play a similar role.

When you speak to them, be calm and factual, bring your record, and focus on the children’s experience. Say clearly what you’re worried about and why.

Protections in court

You can ask for protections if you’re frightened of the other person:

  • separate waiting rooms and different arrival times
  • a screen in the courtroom, or giving evidence by video
  • in England and Wales, people accused of abuse can’t cross-examine the person they’re accused of abusing in person. The court appoints a lawyer to ask the questions instead.

Ask before the hearing. You don’t need to be sure you’ll need them.

Contact centres and supervised contact

If there are concerns about a parent’s behaviour, courts may order time with the children to be supervised, or to take place at a contact centre. In the UK, accredited centres are listed by the National Association of Child Contact Centres. In the US, search “supervised visitation center [your area].” Handovers through a centre can also keep parents apart.

If the decision goes against you

  • Ask your lawyer to explain the reasons, in writing if possible.
  • Appeals usually need permission and have short time limits, often 21 days in England and Wales. Get advice quickly.
  • If circumstances change later, like new incidents, you may be able to apply to change the order.
  • Look after yourself first. A bad decision can feel like being abused again. Call a helpline that night, not a lawyer at midnight.

What can I control?

  • How well documented the pattern is.
  • How calm and child-focused you are.
  • Whether you have a lawyer who understands coercive control. See Legal help with little or no money.
  • Whether you ask for protections at court. See Your day in court.
  • Whether you meet every deadline.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine shows how systems and paperwork get turned into weapons in The Administrative Cage.

What can I share about the case?

Be careful before you post, publish or pass on anything from family court. The rules are strict, and breaking them can be serious.

  • England and Wales: cases about children are private. Publishing information from them, or sharing court documents with people outside a permitted list, can be contempt of court. You can usually share information with your lawyer, a McKenzie friend, a doctor or therapist, and a domestic abuse adviser, for advice and support. Posting about the case on social media, even without names, can break the rules. Identifying the children is never allowed.
  • United States: rules vary by state. Family court records are sometimes sealed, and judges can make orders limiting what parents say publicly. Ask your lawyer before you post anything.
  • Elsewhere: assume family court matters are private until a lawyer tells you otherwise.

You can still talk about your own experience of abuse in general terms with friends and support services. If you want to speak out publicly, get legal advice first.

Checking what’s driving your case

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

Court can turn a hard situation into a contest. Every so often, ask yourself:

  • Is what I’m asking for about my children’s safety and needs, or about winning?
  • Am I opposing something because it’s harmful, or because it came from them?
  • What would my children say they need, if I asked in a way that didn’t put them in the middle?
  • Could I explain each request to a fair stranger in one calm sentence?

Courts notice which parent keeps the children at the centre. So do children, later.

What helped in Carla’s case

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Carla went into family court expecting not to be believed. She brought a two-page timeline with dates and references to evidence: police logs, school records, messages. She asked for a separate waiting room and a screen. When the Cafcass officer interviewed her, she spoke about her daughter’s experience: the nightmares, the fear before handovers. She didn’t call her ex names.

The court ordered contact at a supervised centre for six months, with a review. It wasn’t everything she hoped for. It was safe, and it was based on what had happened.

What you can control, in one line

Your preparation, your calm and your focus on the children. Spend your energy there.

Your day in court

What should I expect at a family court hearing with an abusive ex?

The short answer

What to expect at a family court hearing: a waiting area, often a short hearing, and a judge who has read some of the papers. Prepare a folder, ask in advance for protections like a separate waiting room or a screen, bring a supporter, and plan how you’ll arrive and leave safely.

The date is set. You’ve been dreading it for weeks, and you want to know what to expect at a family court hearing. Here’s the day, step by step, so at least the practical parts hold no surprises.

What should I do before the hearing?

Ask for protections early. Contact the court, your lawyer, or a court support worker at least a week before, and ask what’s available:

  • a separate waiting room
  • a separate entrance, or arriving and leaving at different times
  • a screen so you can’t see the other person, or giving evidence by video link
  • in England and Wales, victims of domestic abuse are automatically eligible for these measures in family court, and the other person can’t cross-examine you in person

Say: “There’s a history of domestic abuse in this case and I’m frightened of the other party. Which protections can I have at the hearing, and how do I request them?”

Prepare a folder.

  • every court document, in date order
  • your timeline summary on top. See What to document and how.
  • copies of key evidence, flagged
  • a list of the points you want to make, in order
  • a notebook and pen

Plan the day. How you’ll get there, where you’ll park, who’s coming with you, and how you’ll leave without meeting them.

Bring a supporter. A friend, a family member, or a domestic abuse court advocate. Ask the court in advance whether they can come into the hearing.

Wear something comfortable that you’d wear to a work meeting.

The court day checklist puts this on one page.

What happens on the day?

  • Arrive early. Tell security or the usher you’ve asked for a separate waiting room.
  • You may wait a long time. Bring water, a snack, something to read, and your charger.
  • Lawyers may negotiate outside the room. You don’t have to agree to anything you’re not sure about. Ask for time.
  • The hearing itself may be short, sometimes 20 or 30 minutes. The judge has usually read some of the papers.

The Decoder’s page on intimidation describes looks, gestures and comments meant to unsettle you. If anything happens at court, tell your lawyer or the usher straight away, and write it down afterwards.

How do I speak in the hearing?

  • Speak slowly, and address the judge as the court tells you to.
  • Answer the question asked, and stop.
  • Stick to facts, dates and the effect on the children.
  • If you don’t understand a question, say so.
  • If you freeze or cry, that’s all right. Ask for a moment, take a breath, look at your notes.
  • Don’t respond to anything the other side says directly to you. Speak only to the judge.

You don’t have to be perfect. You have to be clear, calm enough, and focused on the children.

What if they’re representing themselves?

That can mean they’re allowed to speak a lot, or try to question you. In England and Wales, they can’t cross-examine you in person if there’s evidence of domestic abuse; the court appoints a lawyer to ask their questions instead. Elsewhere, ask the court in advance what protections apply.

What to wear and bring

Wear something clean and comfortable that you’d wear to an important meeting. Bring:

  • your court papers and any order
  • your timeline and record
  • a pen and paper for notes
  • water and a snack
  • something calming: a photo, a smooth stone, a card with a breathing reminder
  • your phone, charged, on silent

Getting there safely

  • Arrange childcare well in advance.
  • Plan your route, and if you’re worried, ask the court about a separate entrance or waiting area.
  • Bring someone with you if you can. Many domestic abuse services offer court support workers.
  • Don’t wait outside where you might meet them. Go straight in.

If you’re overwhelmed in the hearing

It’s okay to ask for a moment.

Say: “I’m sorry, could I have a moment?” or “Could I have some water, please?”

Breathe slowly, feet on the floor. If you’re giving evidence and you’re very distressed, you can ask for a short break. Judges see this often.

Saying “I don’t understand”

Legal language can be confusing, especially under stress. You’re allowed to ask.

Say: “I’m sorry, I didn’t understand that. Could you explain it in a different way?”

Never agree to something you don’t understand. If you’re representing yourself, ask the court staff or a help desk to explain the next steps before you leave.

The evening after a hard result

Plan something for that evening before you go: a friend, a meal, a phone call. Don’t read their messages. Don’t make big decisions. Write down what happened while you remember it, then put it away. Tomorrow you can ask for advice on what comes next.

What happens after?

  • Get a copy of any order before you leave, or ask when it will arrive.
  • Read it carefully. Check dates, times and conditions.
  • Give copies to the school or anyone else who needs them.
  • Plan for the next few days. Court dates are among the times when risk can rise. See A safety plan for after you’ve left.
  • Look after yourself. Most people feel wrecked after a hearing, whatever the result. Plan something gentle.

If the case continues, see Family court, honestly for the longer view.

Your court-day card

Write these on a small card and keep it in your pocket:

  • my case number and courtroom
  • my support person’s number
  • three points I must say
  • “Could you explain that again, please?”
  • “Could I have a moment?”
  • where I’m going straight afterwards

Divorce and separation proceedings

What should I know about divorcing a narcissist or an abusive spouse?

The short answer

Divorcing a narcissist or any controlling spouse often takes longer and costs more than an ordinary divorce, because delay, hidden money and conflict can be tactics. Get advice from a lawyer who understands abuse, gather financial records early, ask for exemptions from mediation if needed, and expect it to take time.

A divorce is hard. A divorce from someone who wants to keep control can feel endless. Here’s what tends to be different, and what helps.

What is different about divorcing a narcissist?

Whatever word fits the person, controlling spouses often:

  • delay: missing deadlines, refusing to sign, asking for adjournments
  • hide money: moving it, undervaluing a business, “forgetting” accounts
  • fight everything: refusing reasonable offers so every issue needs a hearing
  • use the children as bargaining chips in the financial case. See Children as the channel.
  • play for sympathy: presenting as the calm, wronged party

The Decoder’s pages on financial control and time control describe the moves.

Delay is often a tactic. Plan as if it will be.

How does divorce work, broadly?

The details differ by country and state, but the main stages are similar:

  1. Starting the divorce. Many places now allow no-fault divorce, so you don’t have to prove what they did. England and Wales introduced no-fault divorce in 2022.
  2. Arrangements for the children. Agreed between you, through mediation, or decided by a court.
  3. The financial settlement. Both sides disclose their finances; then the money, property and pensions are divided by agreement or by a court.
  4. The final order, which ends the marriage legally.

Separating from an unmarried partner usually has no divorce stage, but property and children may still need court orders.

What about hidden money?

Both sides usually have to give full financial disclosure, under oath or with a statement of truth. Gather what you can early:

  • bank, savings and credit card statements
  • pay slips, tax returns, business accounts
  • pension statements
  • mortgage, property and loan documents
  • photos of anything valuable in the home

If you think money is hidden, tell your lawyer. Courts can order more disclosure, and some can penalise someone who hides assets. A forensic accountant may help in larger cases.

Do I have to go to mediation?

Not always. Where there’s domestic abuse, many places offer exemptions, and mediation may not be safe. See the mediation section of Family court, honestly.

In England and Wales, you usually have to attend a mediation information meeting before applying to court about children or money, but domestic abuse is an exemption, with evidence. In many US states, judges can waive mediation where there’s domestic violence. If you do mediate, ask for separate rooms or shuttle mediation, and never agree to anything on the spot.

How do I keep costs down?

  • Use free and low-cost legal help where you can: Legal help with little or no money.
  • Prepare well, so your lawyer’s time goes further: organised documents, a clear timeline, a list of questions.
  • Don’t use your lawyer for emotional support. A friend, a domestic abuse advocate or a therapist costs less.
  • Ask whether parts of the case can be settled separately.
  • If they keep filing, see Systems abuse and litigation abuse.

Is coercive control grounds for divorce?

In many places you don’t need grounds at all. England and Wales, Scotland, Australia, Canada and every US state now allow some form of no-fault divorce. You can end the marriage without proving what they did.

That doesn’t make the abuse irrelevant. Coercive control can matter for arrangements for the children, protective orders, and sometimes money. In a few US states, fault grounds such as cruelty still exist and can be used. Ask a family lawyer where you live whether raising it helps your case. See Legal help with little or no money.

How long will it take?

Longer than an ordinary divorce, usually. Some take months; some take years. You can’t control their delays. You can control your own deadlines, your records and your energy.

What about the house, the car and the accounts?

The divorce settles who gets what. You’ll still need to change names, remove yourself from accounts and update beneficiaries. See Untangling: the house, the car, the accounts and the paperwork.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine shows how money and paperwork become a cage in The Administrative Cage.

The steps in England and Wales

Since April 2022, divorce in England and Wales has been no-fault and mostly online:

  1. Application. One of you, or both together, applies online. You don’t have to give reasons or blame anyone.
  2. The other person is notified. They can respond, but they can only dispute the divorce on very narrow grounds, such as the marriage not being valid.
  3. Wait 20 weeks, then apply for a conditional order.
  4. Wait six weeks and a day, then apply for the final order, which ends the marriage.

Money and the children are dealt with separately, by agreement or by court order. Get financial arrangements made into a court order; a divorce alone doesn’t end financial claims.

Elsewhere, search “[your country or state] how to file for divorce.” Most court websites have step-by-step guides.

Financial disclosure

Before agreeing money, both sides should share full details of income, savings, debts, property and pensions. In England and Wales this is usually done on a form called Form E. If you suspect hidden assets, say so and ask for documents. Agreeing without full disclosure can leave you worse off, and is hard to undo.

Pensions

Pensions are often one of the biggest assets in a marriage, and one of the most overlooked. Courts can split them, through pension sharing or similar orders. Ask for a valuation. If the person you left had control of the money, the pension may be bigger than you think.

If they won’t respond

Some people ignore divorce papers to delay or to keep control. In most places the process can go ahead without them. Courts have ways to prove they were sent the papers, and to continue if they don’t take part. Ask the court or a lawyer about the next step, and keep proof of every attempt to notify them.

Negotiating safely

  • You don’t have to be in the same room. Ask for shuttle mediation, where the mediator moves between rooms, or for lawyer-to-lawyer negotiation.
  • Put offers and agreements in writing.
  • Don’t agree anything on the spot, especially under pressure. Say: “I’ll consider it and reply in writing.”
  • If they threaten you during negotiations, record it and tell your lawyer.

How Mei kept the divorce from becoming the abuse

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Mei’s husband refused to answer letters about the divorce, then sent long emails accusing her of ruining his life. Her solicitor handled all communication. Mei applied for the divorce on her own; the court confirmed he had received the papers. When it came to money, she asked for full financial disclosure, which showed a pension and an account she hadn’t known about. Negotiation happened in separate rooms, with a mediator moving between them.

It took eleven months. She never had to sit across a table from him.

Religious divorce

If you married in a religious ceremony, you may need a religious divorce as well as a civil one, such as a Jewish get or an Islamic divorce. Some people refuse to grant a religious divorce as a way to keep control. In England and Wales, for some faiths, a court can delay the final civil divorce until the religious divorce is given. Government guidance on domestic abuse recognises withholding a religious divorce as a form of abuse, and it has formed part of at least one conviction for controlling behaviour. Advice services within your faith community can help. Ask your lawyer early.

Protective orders

How does a protective order work, and is it worth getting?

The short answer

A protective order is a court order that tells someone to stay away from you and stop contacting you, and breaking it can lead to arrest. How a protective order works depends on where you live, but most places offer emergency, temporary and longer-term orders. It’s a real tool, not a guarantee, so pair it with a safety plan.

If the person you left keeps contacting you, turning up or threatening you, a protective order can put a legal line around you. Crossing it becomes a matter for the police.

If you’re in danger right now, call your emergency number. If you’re not safe right now has the numbers.

How does a protective order work?

A protective order is a court order that can:

  • ban contact, including messages and contact through other people
  • order them to stay a set distance from you, your home, your work or your children’s school
  • order them out of a shared home
  • in some places, deal with temporary custody, firearms, or a shared pet

If they break it, the police can act. In many places a breach is a crime in itself.

The names differ:

WhereCommon names
United Statesprotective order, restraining order, order of protection
England and Walesnon-molestation order, occupation order; domestic abuse protection order (being introduced)
Scotlandinterdict, non-harassment order
Irelandsafety order, protection order, barring order, emergency barring order
Canadaemergency protection order (by province), peace bond
AustraliaAVO (NSW), IVO (Victoria), DVO (Queensland), FVRO (WA), and others by state

Search “[your country or state] protective order domestic abuse” for your local version.

What types are there?

Most places have three levels:

  1. Emergency orders. Issued fast, sometimes by police or a judge on the same day, often without the other person present. They last days.
  2. Temporary or interim orders. Last until a full hearing, usually weeks.
  3. Longer-term or final orders. Made after a hearing where both sides can be heard. They often last months or years, and can be renewed.

How do I apply?

  • Through a court: most courts have forms and help desks for protective orders. Domestic abuse services can help you fill them in, and in many places there’s no fee.
  • Through the police: in some places the police can apply for you, or issue an emergency order themselves.
  • Through a lawyer, often free for protective orders. See Legal help with little or no money.

What do I write in the application?

  • The most recent incident, in plain words, with the date.
  • The pattern: earlier incidents, with dates. Your record does this work. See What to document and how.
  • Any threats, weapons, strangling or stalking. These carry weight. See Warning signs to take seriously.
  • What you’re afraid will happen.
  • What you’re asking the court to order.

Stick to what happened. Leave out labels and long histories that don’t relate to your safety.

What happens at the hearing?

Usually short. The judge reads your application, may ask you questions, and hears from the other person if they attend. Bring your evidence and a supporter. Ask for protections such as a separate waiting room. See Your day in court.

The other person may deny everything, or apply for an order against you. Stay calm. See When they say you’re the abuser.

How long does a protective order last?

It depends on the type and the place. Emergency orders last days, temporary orders weeks, and longer orders anywhere from months to several years. Note the end date, and diary a reminder a month before so you can ask for a renewal if you still need it.

Do protective orders work?

They help many people. They also have limits.

What an order can do: create a legal basis for the police to act; make a formal record; deter many people, especially those who care about consequences.

What it can’t do: physically stop someone determined to break it. Police responses also vary by place and by officer.

A protective order is a real tool, and it can’t physically stop anyone. Plan for the gap between a breach and the response.

What do I do if they break it?

  1. If you’re in danger, call your emergency number and say there’s an order.
  2. Write down what happened, when and where, and who saw it.
  3. Keep messages, call logs and photos.
  4. Report every breach, however small, and ask for a reference number each time.

A pattern of recorded breaches is often what produces stronger action. The Decoder’s page on intimidation describes the borderline behaviour people use to test an order.

The main orders in the UK

  • Non-molestation order (England, Wales, Northern Ireland): stops someone using or threatening violence, harassing or intimidating you. Breaking it is a crime.
  • Occupation order: decides who can live in the home. See Still under the same roof.
  • Domestic Violence Protection Notice and Order: short-term protection that police can put in place quickly after an incident.
  • Domestic Abuse Protection Orders: a newer, more flexible order introduced by the Domestic Abuse Act 2021, being brought in across areas from late 2024. It can include requirements like tagging or attending a programme.
  • Scotland: interdicts, non-harassment orders and exclusion orders.

Serving the order

An order usually only takes effect once the other person knows about it. This is called service. Ask the court how it will be served and when. Don’t serve it yourself. Until you know it’s been served, keep your safety plan as if it hasn’t.

Telling the people who need to know

Give a copy, or tell, those who may need to act on it:

  • the children’s school or nursery
  • your employer and building security
  • neighbours you trust
  • your local police station, if they don’t already have it

Keep a copy with you, one at home somewhere safe, and a photo on your phone.

Changing or renewing an order

Orders often last for a fixed time. If you still need protection as the end date approaches, apply to extend it before it runs out. If circumstances change, like a move or new incidents, you can apply to vary it. Don’t agree informally to “ignore” an order because they’ve asked you to. Only a court can change it.

Orders across borders

  • United States: under federal law, a protective order made in one state must be enforced in every other state, as well as tribal lands and territories.
  • European Union: protection orders can be recognised in other EU countries.
  • Elsewhere: if you’re moving country, ask a lawyer before you go whether your order will be recognised, and how to register it.

Where should I keep the order?

Keep a copy on you, and give copies to your children’s school, your workplace and anyone else who might need to call the police. Photograph it on your phone.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine lists protective orders among your core documents in Appendix B, Liberation Logistics.

What happened when the order was broken

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Three weeks after Sara’s non-molestation order was served, her ex sent a message through her cousin. Sara screenshotted the cousin’s message, wrote down the time and called the police, giving the order’s reference number. The officer recorded a breach. When it happened again, he was arrested.

Sara had worried that reporting a “small” breach would seem petty. Her support worker told her that small breaches, reported each time, are how orders keep their strength.

Keep the order close

Put a photo of your order in your phone, under a plain name, so you can show it to police in seconds.

Police and reporting

Can I report post-separation abuse to the police?

The short answer

Yes, you can report post-separation abuse to the police. Threats, stalking, harassment, criminal damage, breaking an order and, in some places, coercive control are crimes. Report early, describe the pattern with dates, and ask for a reference number every time.

Many people wait until something dramatic happens before calling the police. But much of post-separation abuse is made of smaller things, and some of those smaller things are crimes.

If you’re in danger now, call your emergency number.

Can I report post-separation abuse to the police?

Yes. You can report:

  • threats, including threats sent by message
  • assault, of any kind
  • stalking and harassment. See Stalking and harassment after a breakup.
  • criminal damage to your home, car or belongings
  • breaking a protective order. See Protective orders.
  • sharing or threatening to share intimate images. See Intimate images and online threats.
  • in some places, controlling or coercive behaviour itself, including after separation. See What post-separation abuse is.

What do I say so they hear the pattern?

Officers often see one incident at a time. Help them see the whole picture:

Say: “I’m reporting [today’s incident]. It’s part of a pattern since we separated in [month and year]. I have a record of [number] incidents, including [threats / turning up / messages after the order]. I’d like this recorded as domestic abuse.”

Bring or offer your record and evidence. See What to document and how.

What should I ask for?

  • A reference or incident number. Every time.
  • The officer’s name and badge number.
  • What happens next, and when you’ll hear.
  • A referral to a domestic abuse service, if you don’t already have one.
  • Whether a risk assessment will be done. Many police services use one for domestic abuse.

What is Clare’s Law?

Some places let you ask the police whether someone has a history of abuse. In England and Wales this is the Domestic Violence Disclosure Scheme, known as Clare’s Law. Scotland and parts of Australia have similar schemes. It can help if you’re worried about a new partner, or about someone your ex is now seeing.

What if they make a false report about me?

Stay calm, get legal advice before an interview, and bring your record. The Decoder’s page on false reports describes the tactic, and When they say you’re the abuser covers what to do.

What if the police response is poor?

It happens. If you were dismissed, not believed, or the report wasn’t recorded:

  • ask to speak to a supervisor, or to a specialist domestic abuse officer
  • ask a domestic abuse service to advocate for you
  • make a formal complaint. Most police forces have a complaints process, and many countries have an independent police complaints body.

Keep reporting. Each report adds to the record, even when nothing seems to happen.

What happens after you report

  • An officer takes your account and records it. Ask for the crime or incident reference number.
  • They may ask questions to assess risk. See Why it often gets worse first.
  • They may speak to the person you reported, arrest them, or gather more evidence first.
  • In many places, a specialist domestic abuse officer or advocate will contact you.
  • You should be told about decisions, such as whether there will be a charge.

Your rights as a victim

  • England and Wales: the Victims’ Code sets out your rights. They include being kept informed, being referred to support, and asking for a review if a decision is made not to charge.
  • United States: prosecutors’ offices often have victim advocates. Many states let you sign up for notifications when an offender is released.
  • Elsewhere: search “[your country] victims’ rights domestic abuse.”

If you don’t want a prosecution

You may want the abuse recorded and to be safe, without the person being prosecuted. Tell the police what you want. Police and prosecutors may still decide to go ahead if there’s enough evidence without you, because domestic abuse is treated as a crime against the public too. Your safety and your wishes should still be considered.

Reporting online

Many police forces let you report non-emergency crimes online, which can be easier if you can’t talk on the phone safely. Never use online reporting for an emergency.

Complaints

If the police response was poor, you can complain to the force. In England and Wales, serious complaints can go to the Independent Office for Police Conduct. In the US, contact the department’s internal affairs or professional standards unit. A domestic abuse service can help you complain.

Do I have to report?

No. Some people don’t feel safe involving the police, or have had bad experiences with them. That’s your decision. You can still keep a record, get a protective order through a court, and get support from a domestic abuse service.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine sets out how to keep evidence for systems in Appendix B, Liberation Logistics.

If the case goes to criminal court

If the person is charged, you may be asked to give evidence as a witness. You’ll usually be supported by a witness care service or a victim advocate who explains each step and tells you dates.

You can ask for protections, often called special measures: a screen so you don’t see the defendant, giving evidence by video link from another room, or a supporter sitting nearby. Ask early. You may be able to visit the court beforehand to see the room.

Bail conditions and orders after the case

  • Bail conditions: before a trial, a court can set conditions, such as no contact with you and staying away from your home. If they’re broken, tell the police straight away.
  • After the case: in England and Wales, a court can make a restraining order when someone is convicted, and in some cases even when they’re found not guilty. Other countries have similar orders.

Telling the court how it affected you

You can usually make a statement about how the crime affected you, called a victim personal statement in England and Wales, or a victim impact statement in the US. You can read it out, have it read, or give it in writing. It’s your chance to say, in your own words, what this has done to your life.

What a good report sounded like

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

When Daniela called the police after her ex appeared outside her flat for the third time in a week, she had her record open. She said: “I’m reporting stalking by my ex-partner. Since we separated in January there have been fourteen incidents, including three times outside my home this week. I’d like it recorded as domestic abuse and stalking, and I’d like a reference number.”

The officer asked for her record, and passed the case to a domestic abuse team the next day.

Your three sentences

Write three sentences now that describe the pattern, so you can read them out if you ever need to report.

Housing and benefits

Where can I get housing help after domestic abuse?

The short answer

Housing help after domestic abuse can include refuge, emergency housing, priority for social housing, the right to end a lease early, and help to stay safely in your own home. Benefits and child support can provide a floor. A local domestic abuse service usually knows exactly what’s available where you live.

Maybe you’re on a friend’s sofa, or in a refuge, or in a home you can’t afford alone. Money and housing are often the biggest reasons people feel stuck. There’s help for the months after you leave, as well as the first days.

Where can I get housing help after domestic abuse?

The options usually include:

  • Refuge or shelter: safe, confidential housing, usually short term, often with support workers.
  • Emergency housing through your local council or housing authority.
  • Priority for social housing. In England, people made homeless by domestic abuse automatically have priority need for housing help.
  • Help to stay in your own home safely: better locks, alarms and other changes, sometimes called a sanctuary scheme.
  • Transitional housing: longer stays with support, in some places.

Your local domestic abuse service is the best first call. They know what’s free near you, and how long the waits are.

The Decoder’s page on financial dependence by design explains how control often leaves people without money or housing of their own. That’s a common result of abuse, not a personal failing.

Can I end my lease early?

In many places, yes:

  • United States: many states let survivors end a lease early with notice and documentation. In federally assisted housing, the Violence Against Women Act gives protections, including emergency transfers and removing an abuser from a lease.
  • England and Wales: options depend on your tenancy. Councils and housing associations often have domestic abuse policies, and joint tenancies can sometimes be transferred into your name.
  • Australia: most states let victims end a tenancy early with evidence of family violence.
  • Canada: several provinces, including Ontario and British Columbia, let survivors end a tenancy early with notice.

Search “[your state or country] break lease domestic violence,” or ask a tenancy advice service.

What benefits might help?

Rules vary, but look at:

  • Benefits for single parents and people on low incomes.
  • Child support. Use the official service so you don’t deal with them directly. See Money as the channel.
  • Emergency funds from domestic abuse charities.
  • Help with legal costs. See Legal help with little or no money.

If you apply for benefits, tell the agency you’ve left an abusive relationship. Some have rules that help. You may get a break from work rules for a while, or a way to keep your address private.

What if my immigration status depends on my ex?

Many countries have protections for this. See the immigration section in If your story doesn’t look like the usual one.

In the UK, the Migrant Victims of Domestic Abuse Concession can give short-term access to benefits while you apply to settle. In the US, VAWA lets some people apply for status without their abuser, and U visas exist for victims of certain crimes. Other countries have their own routes. Get advice from an immigration adviser or a domestic abuse service before you contact any authority about your status.

What are refuges and shelters like?

A refuge or shelter is a safe house for people escaping abuse. Its address is kept secret. You usually have your own room, and you share kitchens and living spaces. There are staff who help with safety, money, housing, court and children.

Stays range from a few weeks to several months. Some take children; some take older boys only in certain cases; some take pets. To find a space, call a national domestic abuse helpline. They can search for spaces across the country.

Homelessness help and priority need

  • England and Wales: if you’re homeless because of domestic abuse, local councils must treat you as being in “priority need” for housing, under the Domestic Abuse Act 2021. Ask the council’s housing team for a homelessness assessment.
  • Scotland: councils must help people who are homeless, with guidance of its own on domestic abuse.
  • United States: contact your local domestic violence program and the local homelessness system. Ask about rapid rehousing and transitional housing for survivors.

Staying put safely

If you want to stay in your home, ask about “sanctuary schemes” (UK) or similar programmes. These add extra security, like stronger doors, locks, alarms and window guards, so you can stay where you are. You may also be able to get an occupation order. See Police and protective orders.

Moving social housing

If you’re in social or council housing, you can ask for a management transfer or a move to a different area for safety. Explain the risk and provide any evidence you have.

Renting with no references

Leaving abuse can leave you with no rental history, debt or poor credit. Ask domestic abuse services about deposit schemes, rent guarantors and landlords who accept a support worker’s letter. Explain briefly to a landlord: “I’ve recently left an abusive relationship, which is why I don’t have a previous landlord reference.”

US housing protections under VAWA

In housing covered by federal programmes, like public housing and Section 8, the Violence Against Women Act protects you. You can’t be evicted or denied housing because you’re a victim of domestic violence. It also provides for emergency transfers. Many states add protections, like ending a lease early.

Where do I start with housing?

Call a domestic abuse service and say:

Say: “I’ve left an abusive relationship and I need help with housing and money. Can you tell me what’s available locally and help me apply?”

Then see Where to find the right body for other agencies that can help.

From refuge to a home

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Zainab arrived at a refuge with her two children and one suitcase. Over four months, staff helped her apply for benefits, register with a doctor and get the children into a new school. The council accepted that she was homeless because of domestic abuse and offered her a flat. A furniture charity provided beds and a cooker.

The flat was small and far from her old friends. It was also the first place in years where she chose what went on the walls.

Ask for the assessment

If you’ve lost your home because of abuse, ask your local council or housing authority for a homelessness assessment. Say clearly that domestic abuse is the reason.

Where to find the right body

Where do I get help after domestic abuse, and who can make my ex stop?

The short answer

Where to get help after domestic abuse depends on the problem: courts for protective orders and children, the police for crimes, child support services for money, housing authorities for a home, and domestic abuse services for almost everything. This chapter shows which body can do what, and what to search to find yours.

Every chapter in this part has said “it depends where you live.” This one helps you find the answer for where you live.

Where do I get help after domestic abuse?

Start with a domestic abuse service. They know your area better than any book, and they can point you to the rest. Then use this table.

ProblemWho can actWhat to search
Immediate dangerPoliceyour emergency number
Threats, stalking, breaking an orderPolice“[area] police report domestic abuse”

Children’s arrangementsFamily court“[area] family court child arrangements”
Repeated court applicationsFamily court (barring or vexatious orders)“[area] vexatious litigant family court”

Online abuse, imagesPlatform; online safety regulator“[area] intimate image abuse report”
Legal adviceLegal aid, clinics, domestic abuse legal services“[area] legal aid domestic abuse”

The Decoder’s page on administrative escalation explains how controlling people use agencies against you. This table is the same system, used for you.

Who can make my ex stop?

No single body can. But together:

  • Courts can order them to stay away and stop contacting you, and limit repeat applications. See Protective orders and Systems abuse and litigation abuse.
  • Police can arrest and charge for crimes, including breaches of orders. See Police and reporting.
  • Child support agencies can take payments from wages.
  • Platforms can remove content and accounts.

What do I search for?

Use “[your country or state] domestic abuse support” first. National charities usually come up at the top, and they know who does what locally. The Find help page lists the main ones for the US, UK, Canada, Australia and Ireland.

Problem → who handles it

ProblemUsually handled by
Immediate dangerEmergency services
Threats, stalking, breaking an orderPolice
A protective orderCourt
Arrangements for the childrenFamily court, or agreement through mediation where safe
Child supportChild support service
HousingLocal council or housing authority, domestic abuse services
BenefitsBenefits agency
Debt in your nameLender, then a free debt advice service
Your health and sleepDoctor
Children’s distressSchool, doctor, child mental health services
Planning, advice and supportDomestic abuse services

Who to call first

If you don’t know where to start, start with a domestic abuse helpline. They know which agencies work where you live, and can refer you directly or tell you what to say.

One sheet to keep track

Agencies multiply quickly. Keep a single page with:

  • each agency’s name and phone number
  • the name of the person you spoke to
  • your reference number
  • what they said they’d do, and by when

When you call again, you can say, “I spoke to [name] on [date], reference [number].”

When agencies disagree

Sometimes the police, the court and children’s services see things differently. Ask each one to put its decision in writing, and share decisions between them where you’re allowed to. An advocate can help agencies talk to each other.

Advocates who coordinate

  • United Kingdom: an Independent Domestic Violence Adviser (IDVA) supports people at higher risk, attends meetings and works with police, courts and housing on your behalf.
  • United States: domestic violence programs have advocates who help with protective orders, court, housing and safety.
  • Elsewhere: ask a domestic abuse service for “an advocate who can help me deal with the agencies.”

What if an agency won’t help?

Ask for a decision in writing, and ask how to complain or appeal. Ask a domestic abuse advocate to contact them for you. Agencies often respond differently when an advocate is involved.

What advocates can and can’t do

Domestic abuse advocates are among the most useful people you’ll meet. It helps to know their limits, so you’re not let down:

  • They can explain options, help you plan, come to meetings, and speak to agencies with your permission.
  • They usually can’t give legal advice, make the police or a court act, or guarantee housing.
  • They can tell you who can do those things, and help you ask in the right way.

When Sean didn’t know where to start

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Sean had six problems at once: threatening messages, unpaid child support, a joint debt, his son’s anxiety, no stable home and no sleep. He called a domestic abuse helpline, which referred him to a local advocate. Together they made a list: police for the threats, the child support service for the money, a free debt adviser for the loan, the school and doctor for his son, the council for housing, his own doctor for sleep.

Six problems still felt like a lot. Six names and numbers on one sheet felt manageable.

Make your sheet

Write down every problem you’re facing, then the agency next to each one. Leave blanks where you don’t know yet, and ask a helpline to fill them in.

Untangling: the house, the car, the accounts and the paperwork

What do I need to change after leaving an abusive relationship?

The short answer

After leaving an abusive relationship, change more than your passwords: beneficiaries on insurance and pensions, your will, emergency contacts, medical consent, joint accounts, the phone plan, the car, and who can access your records. This chapter is the full list, in a sensible order.

The first month is about safety. After that comes the slow job of untangling your life from theirs. It’s boring, and it matters: a forgotten joint account or an old insurance form can keep you tied to them for years.

What should I change after leaving an abusive relationship?

Work through this list over a few months. Tick what applies.

Safety and access first

  • Email, phone and cloud passwords, from a device they never touched. See Surveillance and technology.
  • Recovery phone numbers and emails on every account.
  • Security questions they could answer (“first pet,” “mother’s maiden name”).
  • Remove them as a trusted contact, family member or authorised user anywhere.

Money

  • Open accounts in your name only. See Money as the channel.
  • Close or freeze joint accounts and credit cards, or ask the bank to require both signatures.
  • Remove them as an authorised user on your cards.
  • Check your credit report for accounts you didn’t open.

The phone plan and subscriptions

  • Move your number off a shared family plan. Many carriers will help survivors do this.
  • Cancel or move streaming, shopping and delivery accounts they can see.

The home

  • Rented: ask your landlord or housing provider about taking over the tenancy, or ending a joint one. See Housing and benefits.
  • Owned jointly: you usually can’t remove a name from a mortgage without the lender’s agreement, or a court order. Get legal advice; it will often be part of the divorce settlement. See Divorce and separation proceedings.
  • Change locks, alarm codes and garage door codes.
  • Pets and belongings still with them: see Pets and the things left behind.

The car

  • Change the registration and insurance into your name if you’re keeping it.
  • Remove them from the car’s app, and check for trackers.

Insurance and pensions

  • Change beneficiaries on life insurance, pensions and workplace death-in-service benefits.
  • Check health insurance: who’s covered, and who receives the paperwork.

Your will and legal powers

  • Make or update your will. In some places divorce cancels gifts to an ex in a will, but separation alone doesn’t.
  • Cancel any power of attorney or health care proxy that names them, and make new ones.

Medical and school records

  • Update emergency contacts with your doctor, dentist, the children’s school and your workplace.
  • Ask your doctor and the school to keep your address private.

Public records

  • Voter registration, driving licence and government records: see Your address, your records, your mail.

Social media and photos

  • Remove them from shared albums and cloud photo libraries.
  • Review tagged photos and old posts that show your routine.

The Decoder describes how tangled money and accounts keep people tied: financial enmeshment and account control.

Does any of this need their agreement?

Some things do: joint mortgages, joint loans, some joint accounts, and anything already covered by a court order. Don’t stop paying shared debts without advice, because you may both remain liable. Your lawyer or a debt advice service can help.

A checklist by category

Money

  • ☐ Bank accounts in your name only; joint accounts frozen or closed with advice
  • ☐ New PINs and passwords
  • ☐ Credit report checked
  • ☐ Direct debits and standing orders reviewed

Home

  • ☐ Lease, mortgage and utilities updated
  • ☐ Insurance for home and contents in your name
  • ☐ Locks changed where you’re allowed to

Health

  • ☐ Doctor, dentist and pharmacy updated, with a note not to share your address
  • ☐ Emergency contact changed
  • ☐ Medical consent and next of kin updated

Legal

  • ☐ Will updated
  • ☐ Power of attorney revoked or changed
  • ☐ Life insurance and pension beneficiaries updated

Digital

  • ☐ Email, phone, cloud and social accounts secured
  • ☐ Shared subscriptions and streaming services separated
  • ☐ Photos backed up to an account only you control

Insurance and pensions

Many people forget that the person they left is still named as the beneficiary on life insurance, pensions or workplace death-in-service benefits. Change these as soon as you’re allowed to. During a divorce, some changes may need to wait until the end, so check with a lawyer first.

Your will and next of kin

If you have a will naming the person you left, they may inherit if something happens to you. In some places marriage or divorce changes a will automatically; in others it doesn’t. Make a new will, even a simple one. Change your next of kin with your doctor, employer and any hospital records.

Medical consent and emergency contacts

If you were hurt or ill, who would the hospital call? Make sure it’s someone safe. If you have children, tell the school and doctor who can consent to treatment. Where a court order allows, also tell them who must not be given information about the children’s health without your agreement.

Vehicles and shared pets

  • Transfer car ownership and insurance into your own name, or agree how the car will be dealt with.
  • Make sure pet microchips and vet records list you.
  • Keep receipts showing who paid for what, in case ownership is disputed later.

How do I keep track?

Keep a simple list: what you changed, when, and the reference number. It’s also useful evidence if they later claim you hid something.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine covers gathering documents and separating money in Appendix B, Liberation Logistics.

An afternoon a week

Pick one category from the checklist each week, money, home, health, legal or digital, and work through it in a single afternoon. In five weeks, most of the paperwork will be done, without it taking over your life.

Co-parenting or parallel parenting?

Can you co-parent with a narcissist, or is parallel parenting better?

The short answer

Co-parenting with a narcissist or any controlling ex often fails, because co-parenting needs trust and good-faith talk. Parallel parenting is the alternative: each parent runs their own home, contact between you is minimal and written, and the plan spells out the details so there’s little to argue about.

Every parenting guide says the same thing: work together for the children. That’s good advice when both parents act in good faith. When one parent uses every conversation to control or punish, “working together” can become another channel for abuse.

Can you co-parent with a narcissist?

Usually not in the way the guides mean. Co-parenting assumes you can talk, compromise, swap weekends and make decisions together. With a controlling ex, those conversations become openings: for criticism, for pressure, for using the children. The Decoder calls this children used as leverage.

That doesn’t mean your children lose. It means you need a different model.

What if they’re a covert narcissist?

“Covert narcissist” is a popular term for someone who seems shy, sensitive or hard done by, but still needs control and admiration. Researchers call this “vulnerable” narcissism, as opposed to the loud, grandiose kind.

In co-parenting, this can look like:

  • playing the victim to the school, the court or your family
  • agreeing in person, then quietly not following through
  • small, deniable digs that are hard to describe to anyone else
  • sulking or silence when you hold a boundary

You don’t need to prove a diagnosis. Courts and schools respond to behaviour, written down. Parallel parenting works for the quiet version too, because it cuts the number of moments where those tactics can land. Is my ex a narcissist? covers the label and its limits.

What is parallel parenting?

Parallel parenting is a way of raising children in two homes with as little contact between the parents as possible. Psychologists who work with high-conflict families developed it. In practice:

  • Each home has its own rules. You don’t try to agree on bedtimes, screens or food. You run your home; they run theirs.
  • Contact is minimal and written. One channel, usually a co-parenting app or email, used for logistics only.
  • The plan is detailed. Exchange times and places, holidays, who decides what, how to handle illness and school events. The more the plan settles in advance, the less there is to fight about.
  • Decisions are split. One parent may handle schooling and the other medical appointments, or decisions follow a set process.
  • Handovers are neutral. At school, in a public place, or through a third person. See Handovers.

Parallel parenting leaves far fewer places in your children’s week where a fight can start.

Co-parenting vs parallel parenting

Co-parentingParallel parenting
Contact between parentsregular, flexibleminimal, written only
Decisionsmade togethersplit, or set out in the plan
House rulesagreed and sharedeach home decides
Handoversface to face, often at homeneutral place or third person
Works best whenboth parents cooperateone or both use conflict

Is parallel parenting bad for kids?

Research on children and divorce consistently finds that ongoing conflict between parents harms children. Parallel parenting reduces that conflict. Children adapt well to two homes with different rules, as long as each home is warm and predictable. What hurts them is being in the middle.

Can parallel parenting be court ordered?

In many places courts can order arrangements that work like parallel parenting: communication only through an app, exchanges at school or a contact centre, and decision-making split between parents. Some US states use the term in their parenting plans. Ask your lawyer how to request it, and explain why with your record. See What to document and how.

Do I have to take a co-parenting class?

Sometimes. Many courts send separating parents on a parenting course, and some require it before a final order.

  • United States: many states or counties require a parenting class for divorcing parents with children. They’re often online. Search “[your state] court approved parenting class.”
  • England and Wales: the court may refer you to an online course called Planning Together for Children. Cafcass is meant to consider domestic abuse before a referral.
  • Elsewhere: search “[your country] parenting after separation course court.”

If there has been abuse, say so to the court or the officer who makes the referral. Ask to attend separately, or online, and never in a joint session with your ex. A course aimed at “reducing conflict” can assume the two of you share the blame. You can take the class and still keep your record of what happened. See Family court, honestly.

How do I start parallel parenting?

  1. Move all contact to one written channel. See Apps, calendars and the parenting plan.
  2. Write down the current arrangements in detail, even if they’re informal.
  3. Stop discussing anything that isn’t logistics. The Decoder’s page on controlled communication shows how talk gets used; parallel parenting removes the talk.
  4. Let go of their home. You can’t control what happens there, unless it’s unsafe. Focus on making yours steady.
  5. Ask a court to formalise it if they won’t stick to it.

What does a parallel parenting plan contain?

A good parallel parenting plan is detailed, so there’s less to negotiate. It usually covers:

  • The regular schedule: which days and nights the children spend where, with exact times.
  • Handovers: where, when and how, ideally through school or a neutral place.
  • Holidays and special days: birthdays, religious holidays, school breaks, Mother’s and Father’s Day, alternating years.
  • Communication: one method, usually an app or email, and what it’s for.
  • Decisions: who makes which decisions, and how disagreements are settled.
  • Travel: notice periods and consent for trips away.
  • Changes: how to request a swap, and how far in advance.

The more the plan answers in advance, the fewer chances there are for conflict.

Communication rules

Write them into the plan:

  • Messages only through the agreed app or email.
  • Only about the children: schedule, health, school, activities.
  • Replies within an agreed time, like 48 hours, except emergencies.
  • Emergencies by phone call, followed by a written note.
  • No messages through the children.

Who decides what?

Each parent usually decides day-to-day matters in their own home: bedtimes, meals, screen time, chores. Bigger decisions need a plan:

  • Education: choice of school, special needs support.
  • Health: non-urgent medical treatment, therapy, vaccinations.
  • Religion and major activities.

Some plans give each parent responsibility for one area. Others require agreement, with a mediator or the court as a backstop. Where you have a court order, follow what it says.

Medical and school information

In most places both parents can get information from schools and doctors directly, unless a court order says otherwise. That means you don’t have to pass on every report card or appointment. Ask the school to send letters to both homes. If there’s a reason to limit the other parent’s access, you’ll need a court order; tell the school and doctor if you have one.

When the plan is broken

  • Record each breach: date, what was agreed, what happened.
  • Don’t argue about it at the handover.
  • Raise it once, in writing: “The plan says pickup at 5. Today you arrived at 7.30. Please keep to the agreed times.”
  • If breaches keep happening, take your record to your lawyer, a mediator, or the court.

Moving towards cooperation later

Sometimes, after a long time of calm, parallel parenting can loosen into something more cooperative. Go slowly and only if the other parent has been consistent for a long time. Loosen one thing at a time. If the old patterns come back, go back to the plan.

What do I say to my children?

Keep it simple: “In Dad’s house there are Dad’s rules, and in my house there are my rules. Both houses love you.” See Your children, and what to say.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine looks at the pressure parenting puts on mothers in The Motherhood Matrix.

How parallel parenting calmed things

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

For a year, every handover between Amira and her ex turned into an argument, and every message became a fight about the past. With a mediator, they agreed a detailed plan: school handovers, a co-parenting app for all messages, alternating holidays written out for two years, and each parent deciding day-to-day rules in their own home.

The first months were bumpy. Then the messages shrank to times and dates. Her son stopped having stomach aches on Sundays. Nothing about her ex had changed. The plan just gave him far fewer places to start a fight.

One clause at a time

If you can’t get a full plan agreed, start with the clause that causes most trouble, usually handovers or messages, and get that one in writing.

Handovers

How do I make custody exchanges safe with an abusive ex?

The short answer

A safe custody exchange keeps contact between you and your ex to a minimum. Options include handovers at school, in a busy public place with cameras, through a trusted third person, or at a supervised contact centre. Keep exchanges short, and write down anything that goes wrong.

Handovers are where many co-parents meet. They’re also where much post-separation abuse happens: the long conversation at the door, the comments in front of the children, the “accidental” lateness.

How do I make custody exchanges safe?

Choose the option that gives the least contact you can arrange.

School or nursery handovers. One parent drops off in the morning; the other collects in the afternoon. You never meet. This is often the simplest option for school-age children.

A busy public place. A supermarket car park, a café, a library. Choose somewhere with cameras and other people. Some police stations have designated exchange areas or cameras in their car parks.
