A trusted third person. A grandparent, friend or relative collects and returns the children. Choose someone calm, who won’t carry messages.

A supervised exchange or contact centre. Staff manage the handover so parents arrive and leave at different times and never meet. In the UK, search the National Association of Child Contact Centres. In the US, search “supervised exchange” plus your area, or the Supervised Visitation Network. Elsewhere, ask your court or a domestic abuse service.

Avoid handovers at your home if you can. If you must, hand over at the door, not inside.

A handover should take a minute. Hello, the bag, the medicine, goodbye.

What should the handover look like?

  • Arrive on time, and leave promptly.
  • Keep the children’s bags packed and ready.
  • Pass on only what’s needed: medicine, a school letter.
  • Don’t discuss anything else. If they start, say “I’ll reply in writing” and leave.
  • Keep your voice calm, for the children’s sake.

The Decoder describes the looks, comments and shows of force that often happen at handovers in intimidation, and the use of children as messengers in children used as leverage.

What do I say when they start something at the door?

Say: “I’m not discussing this now. Message me on the app.”

Then turn to the children: “Have a lovely time. See you Sunday.” And go.

What should I record about handovers?

  • the agreed time and the time they arrived
  • anything said in front of the children
  • the children’s state when they returned
  • any change to arrangements made at the door

See What to document and how.

What if my child doesn’t want to go?

Children resist handovers for many reasons: tiredness, missing you, not wanting to stop playing, or something more serious. Stay calm and neutral. Don’t promise they can stay. Listen afterwards, without leading questions. If the resistance is strong or ongoing, read When your child is pulled into it and get advice before refusing contact. In some places, refusing without a court order can count against you.

Handover options compared

OptionHow it worksGood for
Through school or nurseryOne parent drops off, the other picks upAvoiding contact completely on school days
A public placeMeet at a busy, well-lit spot with camerasShort, calm exchanges
A third personA trusted friend or relative does the handoverWhen seeing the other parent is too difficult
A contact centreStaff manage arrival and departure timesHigher risk, or when ordered by a court
KerbsideChild walks from one car to the doorOlder children, lower risk

Contact centres

Contact centres are run by charities or local services. Parents usually arrive and leave at different times, so they don’t meet. Some offer supervised time, with staff present, as well as handovers. Ask your local family court, a domestic abuse service or the national association in your country.

Handovers through school

If one parent drops the children at school in the morning and the other collects them in the afternoon, the parents never need to meet. Tell the school the schedule in writing, and give them a copy of any order. For weekends and holidays, choose another neutral option.

Preparing children for handovers

  • Tell them in advance when they’re going, and when they’ll be back.
  • Pack their bag together, with something from home to take.
  • Keep goodbyes short and warm: “Have a lovely time. See you on Sunday.”
  • Don’t let them see your worry if you can help it.

Late or no-shows

If the other parent is often late or doesn’t turn up:

  • Agree a waiting time in the plan, like 30 minutes, after which the visit is off for that day.
  • Record each time.
  • Don’t let your child wait for hours. Have a backup plan for the day.
  • Don’t explain the no-show by criticising the other parent: “Dad couldn’t make it today. Let’s do something nice.”

What if handovers become dangerous?

Change the arrangement now: use a contact centre, involve the police, or ask the court to vary the order. Update your safety plan. Handovers and court dates are among the times when risk can rise.

How the handovers moved to school

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Handovers at Tom’s door had become shouting matches, with his daughter watching from the stairs. He asked the court to change the arrangement so his ex collected her from school on Fridays and dropped her at school on Mondays. The school agreed to the schedule in writing.

After the change, Tom and his ex didn’t see each other for months. His daughter said she liked that “nobody shouts on Fridays any more.”

One small change

Look at your current handover and pick one change that would make it shorter or calmer. Ask for that change in writing.

Your children, and what to say

How do I explain the divorce, or the abuse, to my child?

The short answer

How to explain divorce to a child after abuse: give simple, true facts that fit their age, tell them it isn’t their fault and that they’re loved, and keep adult details out. Don’t use them as messengers, spies or confidants. Answer the questions they ask, not the ones you’re afraid of.

Children notice everything. Saying nothing leaves them to fill the gaps, often with the idea that it’s their fault. Saying too much burdens them with adult problems. The goal is the honest middle.

How do I explain divorce to a child?

Three messages matter at every age:

  1. This is a grown-up decision. “Mum and Dad decided to live in different houses.”
  2. It isn’t your fault. Say it plainly, and say it more than once.
  3. You are loved, and you’ll be looked after. Tell them what will stay the same: their school, their friends, their pets, their bedtime story.

Then fit the rest to their age.

What do I say to young children (under 5)?

Short, concrete, repeated. Young children need to know what happens to them, not why.

Say: “Daddy is going to live in a different house. You’ll sleep at my house on school nights and see Daddy at the weekend. We both love you. It’s not because of anything you did.”

Keep routines steady. Expect clinginess, tantrums or sleep changes. See How children react, and what helps them.

What do I say to children aged 6 to 11?

They’ll ask why. Give a true answer without blaming:

Say: “Mum and I weren’t able to keep our home calm and safe. Grown-ups sometimes can’t fix that together, so we decided to live apart. That’s our job to sort out, not yours.”

If they saw or heard abuse, you can name it without detail:

Say: “You saw some scary things happen. That wasn’t okay, and it wasn’t your fault. I’m keeping us safe now.”

What do I say to teenagers?

Teenagers often know more than you think. Be honest, answer their questions, and respect that they may have their own view of the other parent:

Say: “You’ve probably noticed things weren’t okay. I’m not going to ask you to take sides, and I won’t badmouth your dad. If you want to talk about anything you saw or felt, I’m here.”

Don’t make them your confidant. Their job is to be a teenager, not your support.

What should I never do?

  • Don’t use them as messengers. “Tell your mother she’s late with the money.”
  • Don’t ask them to report back. “Who was at Dad’s house?”
  • Don’t share adult details: court, money, what the other parent did to you.
  • Don’t run down the other parent, even when they run you down. It forces children to defend half of themselves.
  • Don’t make them choose.

The Decoder’s page on parentification describes what happens when children are made to carry adult worries. This applies whatever the other parent is doing. Matching a harmful tactic costs the children, not the ex. See Children as the channel.

What if they ask questions I can’t answer?

Say: “That’s a good question. I don’t know yet, but I’ll tell you when I do.”

Or: “That’s a grown-up thing I’m sorting out. You don’t need to worry about it.”

Explaining the abuse they saw or heard

Children who saw or heard abuse usually know more than adults think. Silence can leave them confused or blaming themselves.

Say (to a young child): “You heard shouting and saw some scary things. That wasn’t okay. It wasn’t your fault. My job is to keep us safe.”

Say (to an older child): “What you saw wasn’t how people should treat each other. It’s not your job to fix it or to choose. You can talk to me about it any time, and you can still love your dad.”

Answer what they ask, honestly and simply, without frightening details.

Explaining the police, a court or a protective order

Say: “The court has made some rules to help everyone stay safe and calm. It means Mum won’t come to our house for now. You haven’t done anything wrong.”

Keep it short and calm. Children take their cue from how you sound.

Explaining a move or a new school

Say: “We’re moving to a new home so we can be safe and settled. It’s okay to feel sad about leaving. We’ll take your things, and you can help choose where they go.”

If the address needs to stay secret, explain in a way they can manage: “Our address is a special family secret for now, to help us stay safe. You can tell teachers, but not other people.”

Questions children ask, with answers

  • “Is it my fault?” “No. Never. Grown-up problems are never a child’s fault.”
  • “Will you get back together?” “No, we won’t. But we will both always be your parents.”
  • “Why can’t I see Dad?” “The court is deciding what’s safest. When I know more, I’ll tell you.”
  • “Does Mum still love me?” “What I know is that I love you, always.”
  • “Are we going to be okay?” “Yes. We’re working it out, and you’re safe with me.”

Talking about safety without scaring them

Make safety plans feel like practical family habits, like a fire drill. Practise calling for help, learn your phone number, agree a code word. Keep your tone ordinary. Children feel safer when adults are calm and in charge.

What if they want to talk about the other parent?

Let them. Listen without reacting. If they love the other parent, let them. That isn’t disloyalty to you, and it isn’t a sign they didn’t see what happened. When your child is pulled into it covers what to do when what they say worries you.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine looks at motherhood under pressure in The Motherhood Matrix. Playground Protectors is written for children themselves, and can help you start conversations about safety.

Whose need is the conversation meeting?

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

Before a big conversation with your child, ask yourself:

  • Am I telling them this because they need to know, or because I need them on my side?
  • Is it something a child their age can carry?
  • Would I be comfortable if they repeated it word for word to their other parent?

If the honest answer points to your own need, find an adult to tell instead. Your child needs the simple, true version.

Practise out loud

Choose one hard question your child might ask, and practise your answer out loud, alone or with a friend, until it comes out calmly. Children notice tone more than words.

How children react, and what helps them

What are the effects of domestic abuse on children, and how do I help?

The short answer

The effects of domestic abuse on children can include anxiety, sleep problems, anger, regression, trouble at school, and taking care of a parent. Many children recover well with a safe, steady home, a parent who listens, and help when they need it. Children who still love the other parent aren’t confused; they’re children.

You left partly for them. Now you’re watching them, worried about every tantrum and every quiet day. Here’s what’s common, what’s worth more attention, and what helps.

If a child is in danger, or talks about wanting to hurt themselves, contact emergency services or a crisis line now. If you’re not safe right now has the numbers.

What are the effects of domestic abuse on children?

Children don’t have to see violence to be affected by it. Research on coercive control finds children are harmed by living inside it: the fear and the tension count as much as anything they witness. In England and Wales, the law now recognises children as victims of domestic abuse in their own right.

Common reactions by age:

Babies and toddlers: sleep and feeding problems, clinginess, distress when separated, startling easily.

Young children (3 to 6): going back to earlier behaviour (bedwetting, baby talk), tantrums, fears, worry about you, playing out what they saw.

School-age children (6 to 11): stomach aches and headaches, trouble concentrating, anger or sadness, blaming themselves, trying to be “perfect,” worrying about the parent who isn’t with them.

Teenagers: withdrawal, anger, risk-taking, trouble at school, protecting a parent, taking on adult roles, mistrust in their own relationships.

The Decoder describes two patterns that can carry on after separation: parentification, when a child becomes a parent’s carer or confidant, and golden child elevation, when one child is favoured and another blamed.

Many children recover well. What helps most is a safe, steady home and at least one adult who listens.

What helps children recover?

  • Safety and routine. Regular meals, bedtimes and school. Predictability tells a child’s body the danger has passed.
  • Your calm, as much as you can manage. Children read your mood. You don’t have to be perfect; you can say “I’m sad today, but I’m okay, and it’s not because of you.”
  • Permission to feel. Let them be angry, sad, or happy to see the other parent.
  • Listening without leading. Ask open questions: “How was the weekend?” Not “Did Dad shout?”
  • Telling school. A teacher who knows what’s happening can support them and notice changes.
  • Fun. Ordinary play and laughter are part of healing, not a distraction from it.

When should I get professional help?

Talk to your child’s doctor or school counsellor if:

  • reactions are getting worse, not easing, after a few months
  • they talk about hurting themselves, or about not wanting to be alive
  • they can’t go to school, or can’t sleep, for long stretches
  • they’re hurting others or animals
  • they tell you about being hurt, threatened or asked to keep secrets

Therapies with good evidence for children after abuse include trauma-focused cognitive behavioural therapy, and child-parent psychotherapy for young children. Ask for a therapist with experience of domestic abuse. See Finding a therapist who understands this.

What if my child still loves the other parent?

Most do. That isn’t a sign they didn’t notice, or that you were wrong to leave. Children can love a parent and be hurt by them at the same time. Let them love both. Don’t ask them to see the other parent as you do.

What if my child is angry with me?

Children often direct anger at the safe parent, because it’s safe to. It can feel deeply unfair. Hold steady, name the feeling (“You’re so angry that we moved”), and keep the rules. It usually eases as they feel more secure.

Signs by age

  • Babies and toddlers: trouble sleeping, clinginess, crying a lot, feeding problems, startling easily.
  • Young children: bedwetting, nightmares, tantrums, fears of separation, acting out what they saw in play.
  • Primary school age: stomach aches and headaches, worry, trouble concentrating, aggression or withdrawal, blaming themselves.
  • Teenagers: anger, withdrawal, risk-taking, school problems, taking a parent’s side strongly, or trying to protect you.

Many children show some of these for a while after separation. They usually ease with safety and steady routines.

Going backwards

Some children return to younger behaviours: wetting the bed, baby talk, needing help with things they could do before. It’s a common response to stress. Stay calm and supportive, without shaming or rewarding it. It usually passes as they feel safer.

Anger

Children who lived with abuse may lash out, often at the safe parent, because it’s safe to. Set calm limits on behaviour, while acknowledging the feeling: “You’re angry. You can be angry. You can’t hit.” Look for patterns, like anger after handovers, and note them.

Teenagers: self-harm and their own relationships

Teenagers who’ve grown up with abuse can be at higher risk of self-harm, low mood and controlling relationships of their own. Watch for withdrawal, secrecy about injuries, or a new partner who checks their phone and isolates them. Talk openly about healthy relationships. If you’re worried about self-harm, contact their doctor or a young people’s crisis service.

Getting school on side

Tell the school what’s happening, in brief. Ask for a named person your child can go to. Teachers can watch for changes, give a little flexibility on homework, and support your child at difficult times like handovers or court dates. Ask them to tell you if they notice anything.

Therapy that helps children

If your child is struggling for more than a few weeks, ask their doctor about therapy. Approaches with good evidence for children after trauma include trauma-focused cognitive behavioural therapy (TF-CBT) and, for younger children, child-parent psychotherapy, where you and your child take part together. A good therapist will include you and explain what they’re doing.

What about me?

Your recovery helps theirs. Looking after yourself isn’t selfish. See What your nervous system is doing, and When your child is pulled into it for problems that come from the other home.

How Lily’s son settled

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

After they left, Lily’s six-year-old started wetting the bed, crying at school drop-off and hitting his sister. Lily kept bedtimes the same, told the school, and spent fifteen minutes each evening playing whatever he chose. When the bedwetting didn’t ease after two months, his doctor referred them to a children’s therapist who worked with them together.

By the end of the year he was sleeping through, and drop-offs were calm. He still had wobbles before visits with his dad. Lily learned to expect them and plan a quiet evening afterwards.

Fifteen minutes a day

Give each child a short, regular time with your full attention, doing something they choose. It’s one of the simplest things that helps children feel safe.

When your child is pulled into it

What do I do when my child repeats what the other parent says about me?

The short answer

When a child is repeating what the other parent says about you, stay calm, correct the fact gently, and tell them it’s not their job to sort out grown-up problems. Don’t question them about the other home. If they’re asked to spy, keep secrets or refuse visits, keep a record and get advice.

Your seven-year-old says, “Daddy says you broke up our family because you’re selfish.” Your teenager won’t come back after a weekend away. Your child asks why you “don’t let” them see their other parent more.

A child repeating what the other parent says about you is one of the most common ways children get pulled into the conflict. You can respond without making it worse for them.

If your child talks about being hurt or threatened, or about harming themselves, contact the police, child protection services or a crisis line now. If you’re not safe right now has the numbers.

What do I say when my child repeats something cruel about me?

Take a breath first. Your reaction teaches them whether it’s safe to tell you things.

Say: “I can see that’s confusing. That isn’t true, and you don’t need to worry about it. Grown-ups sort out grown-up things. I love you, and that won’t change.”

Then move on. Don’t ask what else was said. Don’t attack the other parent, even though you may want to. Write it down later, in your child’s words, with the date. See What to document and how.

What if my child is being asked to spy or keep secrets?

Children are often asked “Who’s at Mum’s house?” or “What did she buy?” Tell your child, without blaming:

Say: “You don’t have to answer questions about my house. You can just say ‘I don’t know’ or ‘ask Mum.’ And you never have to keep secrets from me that make you feel worried.”

The Decoder describes children put in the middle in triangulation and children used as leverage.

What about gifts and promises?

Big gifts, special treats, or promises like “you can live with me full time” are common, especially around court dates. Don’t compete. Don’t criticise the gift. Keep your home steady and your promises small and kept.

What if my child refuses to see the other parent?

Children refuse visits for many reasons: fear, something that happened, loyalty pressure, wanting to stay with friends, or not wanting to leave you alone. Find out gently, without leading questions.

  • If you believe the child is unsafe there, get legal advice urgently, and contact child protection services if there’s immediate risk.
  • If there’s a court order, don’t stop contact on your own without advice. In many places that can count against you. Ask your lawyer about asking the court to review it.
  • Keep a record of what the child says and does around visits.

What if my child refuses to see me?

This is one of the most painful things a parent can go through. Keep the door open: send short, warm messages, turn up when you’re supposed to, don’t blame the child. Get legal advice, and keep a record. If you’re accused of being the cause, see When they say you’re the abuser and the parental alienation section of Family court, honestly.

Loyalty conflicts

Children love both parents, often fiercely, even a parent who hurt them. When one parent pulls them in, they can feel torn in two: guilty for enjoying time at your house, guilty for missing the other.

You can ease it by giving permission:

Say: “You’re allowed to love your mum and have fun with her. You don’t need to worry about my feelings. I’ll be fine, and I’ll be here.”

Children who feel free to love both parents tend to cope better, even when one parent is causing harm.

Claims of “alienation”

If you raise abuse, the other parent may say you’re turning the children against them. This is a common response in family court, and it can be distressing. Courts in many countries are now more cautious about these claims where abuse has been alleged.

What helps:

  • Show that you support the children’s relationship with the other parent where it’s safe.
  • Keep your record of the children’s own words and reactions.
  • Explain clearly why you’re worried, focusing on what happened, not on labels.
  • Ask for a professional assessment, not a quick judgment.

See Family court, honestly for more.

Keeping your bond strong

  • Spend regular one-to-one time with each child, doing something they choose.
  • Keep your home predictable: meals, bedtimes, rules.
  • Notice and name what they do well.
  • Say sorry when you get it wrong. It shows them repair is possible.
  • Keep your promises small and keep them.

Steady, ordinary love does more than anything you could say about the other parent.

If a teenager chooses the other home

Sometimes a teenager decides to live with the other parent, even an abusive one. It can feel like a terrible loss and a rejection. Often it’s about freedom, fewer rules, loyalty or guilt, not about loving you less.

Keep the door open. Stay in touch without pressure, and keep inviting them. Don’t criticise the other parent to them. If you’re worried for their safety, talk to a lawyer or children’s services. Many teenagers come back, or return to a closer relationship, in time.

Working with the school or a therapist

Professionals can help a pulled-in child, as long as they understand the situation. Give the school or therapist a brief, factual account, and any court order. Ask them to tell you if the child says things that worry them. Don’t ask them to take sides; ask them to support your child.

When should I get outside help for my child?

Talk to their doctor, school or a child therapist if your child seems frightened of the other parent or talks about being hurt. The same goes for sudden changes in behaviour, or a struggle that lasts for weeks. See How children react, and what helps them.

Reconnecting with adult children

Sometimes children who were turned against you stay distant into adulthood. Reconnection is still possible, often years later, when they begin to see things for themselves.

  • Keep a door open without pressure: a birthday card, a short message once in a while.
  • Don’t use contact to argue your case. Let them lead.
  • If they reach out, listen more than you explain.
  • Look after yourself in the waiting. Support groups for estranged parents exist in many countries.

Is my own hurt colouring what I see?

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

When your child comes back changed, it’s natural to see the other parent behind it. Sometimes that’s true. Sometimes your child is simply growing up, tired, or sad about two homes. Ask yourself:

  • Is this behaviour new, or has my child always reacted like this to change?
  • What did my child say, in their own words, before I interpreted it?
  • Could I be asking questions that pull them into the middle too?
  • Would a teacher or another adult see it the way I do?

If you’re unsure, ask a professional who knows your child. Getting it right protects them either way.

The sentence that helped

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Kwame’s twelve-year-old daughter came back from her mum’s saying she didn’t want to come to his house any more. He felt sick, but he didn’t argue or criticise her mum. Instead he said, “You’re always welcome here, and I’m not going anywhere. You can love both of us.” He kept sending her short messages about things she liked, and kept her room ready.

Three months later she asked to come for a weekend. She never explained why. He didn’t ask.

Keep the door open

Whatever your child says or does, keep a small, steady signal going: a message, a card, a room kept ready. It tells them they can come back.

Apps, calendars and the parenting plan

Which co-parenting app is court approved, and what goes in a parenting plan?

The short answer

A court-approved co-parenting app keeps every message timestamped and undeletable, which protects you and calms things down. Courts often order them in high-conflict cases. Pair the app with a detailed parenting plan that settles schedules, holidays and decisions in advance, so there’s less to argue about.

The less you have to negotiate, the less there is to fight about. Two tools do most of that work: a co-parenting app and a detailed parenting plan.

Which co-parenting app is court approved?

Courts don’t usually “approve” a single brand, but many order parents to use a dedicated co-parenting app in high-conflict cases. Well-known options include OurFamilyWizard, TalkingParents and AppClose. Look for:

  • messages that can’t be edited or deleted, with timestamps
  • a record you can export for your lawyer or the court
  • a shared calendar for the schedule
  • an expense tracker for shared costs
  • tone-checking features, in some apps, that flag hostile wording before it’s sent

Some apps charge a fee; some courts can waive it. Ask your lawyer which apps your local court is used to.

The Decoder’s page on controlled communication shows how the other side can use contact to control you. A dedicated app puts the rules in writing for both of you.

How do I use the app well?

  • Use it for everything. Block other channels if you can.
  • Keep messages short and factual. See Words you can use.
  • Check it at set times, not all day.
  • Don’t respond to anything that isn’t about the children. See Messages you don’t have to answer.

What goes in a parenting plan for a high-conflict ex?

The more detailed, the better. Vague plans (“reasonable contact,” “to be agreed”) give a controlling parent room to argue. Settle:

  • The regular schedule: days, times, who collects and drops off, and where.
  • Handovers: place and method. See Handovers.
  • Holidays, birthdays and special days, year by year, alternating if needed.
  • Lateness: how long to wait before the visit is treated as missed.
  • Communication: the app only, and response times.
  • Decisions: who decides about school, health and activities, or how disagreements are settled.
  • Travel: notice needed for trips, and passport arrangements. See Moving, passports and travel with children.
  • Phone or video calls with the children while they’re at the other home: when and how long.
  • Changes: how to request them, with how much notice.

How do we split children’s expenses without arguing?

Money for the children is one of the easiest places for control to carry on. A few things help:

  • Put the split in the plan. Who pays for school trips, clothes, phones and clubs, and in what share.
  • Use the app’s expense log. Most co-parenting apps let you upload a receipt and request payment. That leaves a record, and you never need to discuss it.
  • Set a limit. Agree that costs under an amount are each parent’s own, and costs over it need written agreement first.
  • Don’t chase in messages. Log it, request it, and let the record build. If it goes unpaid, the record goes to the child support service or the court.

See Money as the channel for when money becomes the weapon.

Should the plan be a court order?

If your ex won’t stick to an informal plan, ask the court to make it an order. An order can be enforced; an informal agreement usually can’t. See Family court, honestly.

What about a shared calendar?

Use the app’s calendar, or one shared calendar for the children only. Don’t share your personal calendar. It shows your routine.

Setting up the app

  • Agree which app to use, or ask the court to order one.
  • Set it up on your own account and your own device.
  • Turn off notifications if they cause you stress; check at set times instead.
  • Use the app’s built-in tone check, if it has one, before sending.

Calendar rules

  • Put the court order or agreed schedule into the calendar as the default.
  • Requests to change must go through the app, with a reason and a deadline to reply.
  • Don’t treat silence as agreement. If they haven’t confirmed, the original schedule stands.

What to log

Most apps keep messages automatically. Also log:

  • late or missed handovers, with times
  • expenses, with receipts
  • school and medical appointments
  • anything the children report that worries you

Exporting records for court

Most co-parenting apps can produce a report or printout of messages, calendar changes and payments, with timestamps that can’t be edited. Check how your app does it before you need it. Courts often accept these reports as evidence.

If they refuse to use the app

Keep using it yourself, and send a short note by email saying all messages about the children will be answered through the app. Keep proof of your offer. If they refuse completely, you can ask the court to order its use.

Export once, now

Find out how to export a report from your co-parenting app today, while nothing is urgent. Save a copy of the first export somewhere safe. You’ll know how to do it when you need to.

Moving, passports and travel with children

Can I move away from my abusive ex with my child, and how do I stop them taking the children abroad?

The short answer

Moving away from an abusive ex with your child usually needs the other parent’s agreement or a court’s permission, especially across borders, even when you’re the one escaping abuse. If you fear your ex will take the children abroad, passport alerts and court orders can help. Get legal advice before you move.

You want distance. A fresh start near family, or simply further away. But moving with children after separation has legal rules, and getting them wrong can hurt your case.

If you or your children are in immediate danger, your safety comes first. Go to If you’re not safe right now, then get legal advice as soon as you can.

Can I move away from my abusive ex with my child?

Often yes, but usually not on your own say-so once the other parent has rights:

  • Within the same country: many places need the other parent’s agreement, or a court order, for a move that affects contact. Courts look at the reasons for the move, the effect on the child, and how contact would work.
  • To another country: almost always needs the other parent’s written consent or a court order. Taking a child abroad without it can be child abduction, even for a parent fleeing abuse.

Courts can and do allow moves where abuse is part of the reason. Explain the abuse, the reasons for the move, and how contact with the other parent will work, if it’s safe. Your record helps. See What to document and how.

The Decoder’s page on mobility control describes how controlling people restrict where you can go. Courts can weigh that too.

What if I’ve already moved to escape?

If you moved to a refuge or another area for safety, tell your lawyer straight away. Leaving for safety is very different from moving to cut the other parent out, and courts generally understand that, especially with evidence.

How do I stop my ex taking the children abroad?

If you fear abduction:

  • United States: enrol your children in the State Department’s Children’s Passport Issuance Alert Program, so you’re told if anyone applies for a US passport for them. Both parents usually have to consent to a passport for a child under 16.
  • United Kingdom: apply for a prohibited steps order to stop a child being removed from the country. If removal is imminent, contact the police, who can issue an all-ports alert. You can also ask HM Passport Office not to issue a passport.
  • Elsewhere: ask a family lawyer about court orders that stop travel, and whether passports can be held by the court.

Keep copies of the children’s passports and recent photos somewhere safe.

What if the children are taken abroad?

Act fast. Contact the police and a family lawyer. If the country is part of the 1980 Hague Convention on child abduction, there’s a process to ask for the children’s return. In the US, contact the State Department’s Office of Children’s Issues; in the UK, the International Child Abduction and Contact Unit; in other countries, your government’s central authority.

What about holidays abroad?

Your parenting plan should say how much notice is needed for a trip, and how passports are handled. See Apps, calendars and the parenting plan. Get written consent for any trip abroad and carry it with you.

The Hague Convention in plain words

Many countries have signed the 1980 Hague Convention on child abduction. Say a child is taken from their home country to another signing country without consent. The courts there are meant to send the child back quickly, so the home country can decide where they should live.

It isn’t a custody decision. There are exceptions, including where returning would put the child at grave risk of harm. If you fled abroad with your children because of abuse, get specialist legal advice urgently. In the UK, the charity Reunite International gives advice on international child abduction.

Consent letters for travel

When taking children abroad, carry written consent from the other parent if you can get it, or a copy of a court order that allows travel. Include their contact details, travel dates and where you’ll stay. Border staff in some countries ask for this when a child travels with one parent.

Moving away: what courts weigh

If you want to move far away, or to another country, with your children and the other parent doesn’t agree, a court usually decides. Courts tend to consider:

  • the reasons for the move, including safety
  • the effect on the children’s relationship with the other parent
  • the practical plan: home, school, support, contact arrangements
  • the children’s wishes, depending on age

Abuse is a relevant reason. Bring evidence and a detailed plan for how the children will keep in touch with the other parent where it’s safe.

Travelling with an order

If you have a protective order or child arrangements order, carry a copy when you travel. If you’re moving within the US, protective orders are enforceable across states. If you’re moving country, check whether your order will be recognised, and how to register it.

When Ana feared a trip abroad

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Ana’s ex started talking about taking their son to visit his family abroad “for a long time.” She was frightened he wouldn’t come back. Her lawyer applied for a prohibited steps order, and the court ordered that the passport be held by her solicitor. In the US, she might have enrolled her son in the passport alert programme.

The trip didn’t happen. Ana kept a copy of the order with her, and gave one to her son’s school.

Check the passports now

Know where your children’s passports are, and who has them. If you’re worried, get advice before a trip is planned, not after.

Pregnant, or with a new baby

What should I know about leaving an abusive relationship while pregnant or with a newborn?

The short answer

Leaving an abusive relationship while pregnant or with a newborn is common, and abuse often starts or gets worse in pregnancy. Tell your midwife or doctor, plan the birth and the first weeks for safety, and get advice about the baby’s legal arrangements. You can protect yourself and your baby.

You’re pregnant, or you’ve just had a baby, and you’ve left or are about to. Leaving an abusive relationship while pregnant is more common than people think. You may be exhausted, frightened, and told by everyone that a baby needs both parents.

Your safety and your baby’s come first. If you’re in danger now, go to If you’re not safe right now.

Why does abuse often get worse in pregnancy?

Pregnancy can be a time when abuse starts or escalates. Researchers think the reasons include a partner’s jealousy, loss of control and attention, and the extra vulnerability pregnancy brings. Violence during pregnancy is one of the warning signs services take most seriously. See Warning signs to take seriously.

The Decoder’s page on vulnerability weaponization describes how controlling people use moments of weakness. Pregnancy and a newborn are exactly those moments.

Who should I tell?

Your midwife, obstetrician or doctor. In many countries midwives ask about domestic abuse routinely, often when you’re on your own. They can flag your notes, refer you to specialist support, and help you plan. You can ask for appointments without your ex present.

Say: “I’ve left an abusive relationship and I’m worried about my safety and the baby’s. Can we plan for that?”

How do I plan the birth safely?

  • Ask the hospital to keep your details private and to restrict who can visit or get information about you.
  • Choose your birth partner carefully. You don’t have to have the other parent there.
  • Plan how you’ll get home and who will be with you in the first weeks.
  • Make sure your safety plan covers the birth and the newborn weeks.

What about the baby’s legal arrangements?

Rules differ a lot. In some places an unmarried father gets legal rights by being named on the birth certificate; in others he has to apply. A court may need to decide on contact with a newborn, often with short, frequent visits at first.

Get legal advice before registering the birth if you’re worried. See Legal help with little or no money and Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.

Antenatal care and being asked about abuse

In many countries, midwives and doctors are meant to ask every pregnant person about domestic abuse, when they’re alone. If you’re asked, you can tell them. They can help with a safety plan, a referral and extra appointments. If the person you left comes to appointments, ask to be seen alone for part of it. Many clinics have a way to do this, like a sign in the toilets you can respond to.

Safety at the birth

  • Tell your midwife or hospital about the risk in advance.
  • Ask for your details not to be given out, and for a password system for visitors and phone calls.
  • Agree who can be with you during labour.
  • Ask for a copy of any protective order to be kept in your notes.

Registering the birth

  • England and Wales: if you’re not married, the father’s name only goes on the birth certificate if you register together, or with certain documents. Being named gives an unmarried father parental responsibility for babies registered since December 2003.
  • United States: rules for naming the father vary by state, and an unmarried father may need to sign an acknowledgement of paternity.
  • Elsewhere: search “[your country] registering a birth unmarried father.”

Get advice before registering if you’re unsure, because it can affect his legal rights.

Feeding and contact arrangements

Courts and parents often have to think about how contact works with a very young baby, especially if you’re breastfeeding. Short, frequent visits may suit a baby better than overnight stays. If there are safety concerns, contact may need to be supervised. Raise feeding and the baby’s routine when arrangements are discussed.

Postnatal depression and support

Having a baby while dealing with abuse puts you at higher risk of postnatal depression and anxiety. Signs include feeling low most days, not enjoying anything, intense worry, trouble bonding, or thoughts of harming yourself or the baby. Tell your midwife, health visitor or doctor. It’s common and treatable, and asking for help won’t mean your baby is taken away. If you’re in crisis, see When it’s more than aftermath.

How do I look after myself with a newborn?

  • Accept practical help: meals, laundry, someone holding the baby while you sleep.
  • Watch your mood. Postnatal depression and anxiety are common, and more common after abuse. Tell your midwife, health visitor or doctor if you feel low, hopeless or frightened most days. If you have thoughts of harming yourself or your baby, get help right away: see When it’s more than aftermath.
  • Keep a record of anything the other parent does that worries you. See What to document and how.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine looks at the pressures put on mothers in The Motherhood Matrix.

A birth plan that included safety

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

At her first midwife appointment, Ruth’s ex was in the waiting room. The midwife saw her alone for part of the appointment and asked about home. Ruth told her. Together they added safety to her birth plan: a password for visitors, no information given out by phone, and a note of the protective order in her records. A domestic abuse worker visited her after the birth.

Her daughter was born on a quiet Tuesday. Only the people Ruth chose knew about it.

Tell one professional

Tell your midwife, doctor or health visitor about the abuse, at the next appointment where you’re alone. They can do more than you might expect.

The trauma bond: why you miss someone who hurt you

What is a trauma bond, and how do I break it?

The short answer

A trauma bond is a strong attachment to someone who hurt you, built by cycles of harm and warmth. It explains why you can miss an abusive ex, defend them, or want to go back, even when you know you shouldn’t. It isn’t weakness, and it loosens with distance, time and support.

You know what they did. You’ve listed it. You’d never tell a friend to go back to someone like that. And still, at 11pm, you want to hear their voice.

That pull has a name.

What is a trauma bond?

A trauma bond is a powerful emotional attachment that forms between a person and someone who harms them, especially when the harm comes in cycles with warmth in between. The term comes from research in the early 1980s by psychologists Donald Dutton and Susan Painter, who studied why people stayed attached to abusive partners.

Two things build it:

  • A power imbalance. One person holds more control.
  • Intermittent harm and kindness. Cruelty, then relief. Coldness, then the person you fell for. You never know which you’ll get.

That unpredictable mix creates a stronger bond than steady kindness would. Behavioural scientists have known since the 1950s that rewards that come unpredictably are the hardest habits to break. The Decoder covers this as intermittent reinforcement and the bond itself as trauma bonding.

“Trauma bond” is a widely used term, not a medical diagnosis. It describes something real that many people recognise.

Feeling the pull is normal. You can feel it and still not act on it.

What are the signs of a trauma bond?

  • You miss them intensely, even remembering what they did.
  • You defend them, or explain their behaviour, to others or to yourself.
  • You remember the good times more vividly than the bad.
  • You feel you’re the only one who understands them.
  • You believe they’ll change if you’re patient enough.
  • You feel relief, not just anger, when they’re kind after being cruel.
  • You keep checking their social media. See Checking their profile.
  • Leaving felt, or still feels, like losing part of yourself.

What does trauma bond withdrawal feel like?

After leaving, many people describe something like withdrawal: obsessive thoughts, cravings to make contact, anxiety, low mood, poor sleep. Brain scans of people recently rejected in love show activity in reward and craving areas that overlap with addiction. That doesn’t mean love is a drug, but it explains why the urge can feel physical.

It peaks in the early weeks and months for many people, and it often spikes when they reach out. See Hoovering.

What are some examples of trauma bonding?

These are the kinds of moments people describe:

  • After a cruel weekend, they cook your favourite meal, and the relief feels like love.
  • You tell a friend what happened, then spend ten minutes explaining why it wasn’t that bad.
  • They threaten to leave, and you end up apologising for something they did.
  • You’ve blocked their number twice, and unblocked it twice, the same night.
  • Months after leaving, one kind message undoes a week of feeling clear.

Each one follows the same rhythm: harm, then relief from the same person. The Decoder’s trauma bonding page has more examples.

How do I break a trauma bond?

It often starts with something small: the phone in another room, their number blocked, a box of gifts taped shut. There’s no quick method. These are the things that tend to loosen it:

  • Distance. No contact, or the least contact you can manage. Every contact restarts the cycle. See No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid.
  • No intermittent contact. Occasional replies feed the bond more than steady silence.
  • Tell the story straight. Write down what happened, plainly, in order. Read it when the good memories crowd in.
  • Name the pull. “This is the bond, not a sign.” Naming it puts a little space between you and it.
  • Replace the comfort. The relationship met needs, even badly. Find other sources: friends, routine, rest, things you enjoy.
  • Support. A therapist who understands coercive control can help, especially if the bond keeps pulling you back. See Finding a therapist who understands this.
  • Patience with yourself. You’re unlearning something your body learned well.

How long does a trauma bond last?

There’s no reliable number, and you’ll be told many. It depends on how long the relationship lasted, how intense it was, whether you still have contact, and what support you have. Most people find the pull weakens noticeably once contact stops, with flare-ups on anniversaries or when the person reappears. It fades in waves, not in a straight line.

Trauma bond vs love: how do I tell the difference?

Love tends to feel safe and steady, even when it’s passionate. A trauma bond tends to feel urgent, anxious and consuming. Love survives your “no.” A trauma bond punishes it. You can have loved them and also be trauma bonded to them. The two often sit together, which is part of why it’s confusing.

Is a trauma bond the same as Stockholm syndrome?

They’re related, but they aren’t the same thing.

“Stockholm syndrome” comes from a bank siege in Stockholm in 1973. Some of the hostages defended their captors afterwards, and a psychiatrist advising the police gave the reaction a name. It describes a bond with a captor during a short, extreme ordeal.

A trauma bond grows over months or years inside a relationship you chose, with real warmth mixed into the harm. That’s why it can feel like love and not like fear.

Neither is a medical diagnosis. Stockholm syndrome has never been in the main psychiatric manuals, and researchers argue about whether it’s a distinct thing at all. Trauma bond is the more useful term for what happens between partners.

Is it codependency, or limerence?

You may meet both words online.

Codependency usually means a habit of putting someone else’s needs first, at your own cost. Some people find it a helpful word. Others find it blames them for a pattern the other person built. A trauma bond is about how the harm was delivered, not a flaw in you.

Limerence is an intense, obsessive longing for someone, often someone out of reach. It can sit alongside a trauma bond. The difference is the harm. A trauma bond forms with someone who hurt you, in cycles.

Whatever word fits, what loosens it’s much the same: distance, a straight story, and support.

Can a trauma bond become a healthy relationship?

Rarely, and only if the person who caused harm changes deeply, over a long time, with accountability and without pressure on you. Going back to test whether they’ve changed usually restarts the cycle. If you’re considering it, read If you go back and Trust as a practice first.

A 30-day plan to loosen it

Thirty days won’t break a bond built over years. It can loosen its grip enough that you feel some choice again.

  • Days 1 to 7: cut the supply. Block or mute them everywhere you can. Put photos and gifts in a box out of sight. Tell one person you’re doing this, and agree they’re the one you message when the pull comes.
  • Days 8 to 14: write the true story. Each day, write one thing that happened, plainly, with the date if you know it. Read the list when the good memories arrive.
  • Days 15 to 21: fill the gaps. Notice the times of day you miss them most. Put something there: a walk, a call, a class, a programme you like.
  • Days 22 to 30: look at what you’ve done. Count the days without contact, the urges you rode out, the nights you slept. Write down one thing you’ve done for yourself that they would have stopped.

If you slip, start again from the day you’re on. Slips are part of how bonds loosen.

When the bond is with a parent or family

Trauma bonds can form with a parent, a sibling or a whole family, as well as a partner. They can be even harder to loosen, because they started in childhood and come wrapped in duty and love.

The same things help: distance where you can, a written account of what happened, and support. Add patience with yourself for the guilt that comes with stepping back from family. See “Leaving a controlling parent or family” in If your story doesn’t look like the usual one.

The worst nights

Some nights the pull is overwhelming. For those nights:

  • Don’t decide anything after 10pm. Tell yourself you’ll think about it in the morning.
  • Put your phone in another room, or give it to someone for the night.
  • Hold something cold, or take a warm shower, to bring your body down.
  • Read the list of what happened.
  • Call a helpline. They talk to people in this exact state every night.

The night passes. The feeling usually feels smaller in daylight.

Explaining it to people who don’t understand

Friends may say, “Why would you miss someone who treated you like that?” It’s hard to explain, and you don’t owe anyone a full answer.

Say: “It’s called a trauma bond. The ups and downs made the attachment stronger. I know it doesn’t make sense from the outside. I don’t need you to understand it; I just need you to not make me feel stupid for it.”

You can also send them this chapter.

Is something wrong with me for feeling this?

No. Trauma bonds form in ordinary people under particular conditions. They say more about the pattern you were in than about your judgment or strength. What you’re allowed to feel about someone you left covers the mix of feelings this leaves behind.

The Sovereign Divine Feminine traces how the bond gets built, stage by stage, in The Manipulation Cycle.

What the pull wants you to forget

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

A trauma bond edits memory. It keeps the warm moments bright and fades the rest. On a hard night, ask yourself:

  • Am I missing the person, or the best version of them I saw a few times?
  • What happened the last time I believed things would change?
  • If I went back, what would I have to stop seeing again?
  • Who in my life would be worried, and why?

Write your answers down while you’re clear, and keep them with your list of what happened. Read both when the pull is strong.

What loosening looked like for Dev

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Dev knew his ex had been cruel. He still checked his phone every hour, hoping for a message. He started the thirty days. The first week he slipped twice and unblocked her. In the second week he wrote the list of what had happened and kept it on his phone. In the third week he joined a five-a-side team that played on the evenings he missed her most.

By the end of the month the pull hadn’t gone. But he noticed it, named it and let it pass most days. That felt like the first choice he’d made in years.

Name it when it comes

When the pull arrives, say quietly, “This is the bond, not a sign.” Then do one thing from your list.

What you're allowed to feel about someone you left

Why do I miss my abuser, and is it normal?

The short answer

If you’re asking “why do I miss my abuser,” you’re not alone. Missing someone who hurt you, feeling tenderness, relief and grief at once, is common and doesn’t mean leaving was wrong. Feelings don’t need to match the story other people expect. You’re allowed all of them.

You might miss them. At certain times: a time of evening, a smell, a song in the supermarket. You might remember something kind and feel a rush of warmth, alongside a clear knowledge that leaving was right. You might feel relief and grief in the same hour.

Why do I miss my abuser?

A song comes on in the supermarket, and for a second you’re back in the car with them, laughing. Because the relationship wasn’t only harm. There were real moments of closeness, real plans, maybe real love on your side. And because the bond was built by cycles of hurt and warmth, which make attachment stronger. The trauma bond explains that part.

Missing someone isn’t the same as wanting them back. You can hold both without either one cancelling the other.

Human attachment doesn’t switch off because the person turned out to be harmful. That isn’t a flaw in you.

What feelings are normal after leaving?

All of these are common, alone or together:

  • missing them, sometimes intensely
  • relief, even joy
  • grief for the relationship you hoped for. See The grief nobody will let you have.
  • anger, sometimes much later. See Anger, arriving late.
  • guilt and shame. See Guilt and shame.
  • fear
  • numbness
  • tenderness, or pity for them
  • loneliness, even in a full room

None of these feelings is evidence that the relationship wasn’t harmful, or that you secretly want to go back.

Why does this need saying?

Because the people around you often expect a simpler story: relief, a bit of sadness, then fine. When your feelings don’t match, it’s easy to decide something’s wrong with you, when the story was simply too simple.

When will these feelings settle?

This book won’t give you a date. Any number would be more comforting than true. Feelings tend to soften in waves, with harder days around anniversaries, contact and change. However long it takes, and however contradictory it feels, it isn’t a sign you’re doing it wrong.

What helps?

  • Let the feelings be there without acting on them. Missing them doesn’t mean you have to text.
  • Talk to someone who can hear the complicated version without telling you what to feel.
  • Write the full story down, good and bad, so neither side takes over.
  • Be wary of people who rush you. Pressure to “move on” usually comes from their discomfort, not from your progress.

What if I feel nothing at all?

Numbness is common. It’s often the nervous system protecting you while it’s overloaded. Feelings usually return as things settle. See What your nervous system is doing.

What if I feel happy, then guilty for feeling happy?

Happiness after leaving isn’t a betrayal of anyone, including your children. You’re allowed good days in the middle of a hard year.

Guilt often arrives because happiness feels unfamiliar, or because you were punished for it in the past. You may also feel you should be sad for the children’s sake, or that being happy proves the abuse wasn’t that bad. Neither is true. Happy moments are part of recovery. Let yourself notice them, and if the guilt comes, name it and let it pass.

What if I want to reach out to them?

Notice the urge, and wait. Tell someone first. Read Hoovering and The trauma bond. The urge usually passes, and it passes faster each time you don’t act on it.

What do I do with the good memories?

You may remember holidays, jokes, kindness, the early days. Those memories are real. They don’t cancel out the harm, and the harm doesn’t cancel them.

You don’t have to throw them away. You can put them in their place: one part of a story that also includes things that hurt you. When a good memory comes, you can let it be there, then remind yourself of the rest. Some people find it helps to write both columns side by side.

Missing them, or missing who you hoped they were?

Often what people miss is the person they believed in: the version that appeared at the start, or in the good spells, or in their hopes. That version was part real and part promise.

Asking yourself “Which one am I missing?” can make the grief clearer. You can grieve the hope without going back to the person.

Feeling sorry for them

You may feel pity: for their childhood, their loneliness, their struggles. Compassion is a good quality. It may also have been used to keep you there.

You can feel sorry for someone and still keep your distance. Their pain is real, and it’s not yours to fix.

Love that lingers

Some people still love the person they left, months or years later. That doesn’t mean you should go back, or that you’ve failed to move on. Love can fade slowly, and it can sit alongside the knowledge that the relationship wasn’t safe. Feeling love isn’t the same as acting on it.

Telling people about mixed feelings

People around you may want you to only feel anger. If you say you miss them, they may worry, or get frustrated. Choose who you tell. A therapist, a helpline or a support group can hear mixed feelings without needing them to be simple.

Both columns

Take a piece of paper and draw a line down the middle. On one side, write what you miss. On the other, what you don’t. Keep both. On hard days, read both. Holding the whole picture is how mixed feelings start to settle.

The grief nobody will let you have

Is grief after leaving an abusive relationship normal?

The short answer

Grief after leaving an abusive relationship is real grief. You may be mourning the person you thought they were, the future you planned, and years of your life. Others often only see the harm and expect relief, which can leave you grieving alone. You’re allowed to mourn something that also hurt you.

There’s a grief in this that almost nobody around you will let you have. Everyone who knows what happened sees the harm, and expects relief.

What am I grieving?

Not always the person, though sometimes that too. More often:

  • the relationship you hoped it would be
  • the future you planned: the house, the children, growing old
  • the version of them you fell for, who may have been real some of the time
  • the years you gave it
  • the family as it was, for your children
  • the person you were before

Why do people dismiss this grief?

Usually with good intentions: “But they were awful to you,” as if that settles it. It’s as if grief needs its object to have been good. It doesn’t. People grieve difficult parents and painful marriages all the time.

Grief that other people don’t recognise has a name: disenfranchised grief. It’s harder to carry because you carry it alone.

You can grieve the loss of something that was also harming you. Mourning one doesn’t mean you’re confused about the other.

Is grief after leaving an abusive relationship normal?

Yes. It’s one of the most common experiences after leaving, and one of the least talked about. It can arrive straight away, or months later, once the practical crisis has passed and there’s room to feel it. It’s often tangled with relief, anger and longing. See What you’re allowed to feel about someone you left.

What do I do with the grief?

  • Let it be grief. You don’t need anyone’s permission for it to be real.
  • Find one person who can hold it. If friends can’t, a therapist or support group can. See Finding a therapist who understands this.
  • Mark the losses. Some people write a letter they never send, or mark the day the future they planned would have happened.
  • Watch for hard dates. Anniversaries can bring grief back with force. See The anniversary effect.
  • Don’t confuse grief with a reason to return. Missing the hoped-for future isn’t the same as that future being available. See The trauma bond.

When should I get more help?

If grief stops you functioning for a long time, or turns into hopelessness that won’t lift, talk to a doctor or therapist. If you’re thinking about harming yourself, go to When it’s more than aftermath.

Can I grieve while I’m still fighting them in court?

Yes. Many people grieve and fight at the same time. The grief may surface in odd moments: in the car after a hearing, or at night. It’s part of the same experience, not a contradiction.

How long does grief like this last?

It doesn’t follow a timetable. It comes in waves that tend to get further apart. Some losses, like the family you hoped your children would have, may be felt again at milestones for years. That’s grief doing its job.

What about grieving for my children’s family?

Many parents grieve the family their children didn’t get. Your children can still have a loving home, or two homes, even if it isn’t the one you pictured.

The losses, named

Leaving abuse can mean losing many things at once:

  • the person you loved, or thought you loved
  • the future you’d planned together
  • your home, your neighbourhood, your belongings
  • in-laws, friends and a community
  • money, security, a career
  • years of your life
  • the version of you from before
  • the family your children had

Naming each one helps. Grief is easier to carry when you know what you’re grieving.

Grief in waves

Grief rarely moves in neat stages. It comes in waves: a song in a shop, a photo, a smell, a date. Some days are fine; some days you can’t get out of bed. Over time, the waves usually come less often, and they’re easier to ride. They may never stop completely, and that’s okay.

Rituals that help

  • Write a letter to what you’ve lost, not to the person. Keep it, or burn it, or bury it.
  • Box up reminders and put them away, to decide about later.
  • Mark a date with something gentle: a walk, a meal, a candle.
  • Make a list of what you’ve gained, alongside what you’ve lost.

Your children’s grief

Children grieve too: for the family they had, the house, the other parent, the routines. Let them be sad without having to make you feel better. Keep some familiar things the same. See How children react.

When grief and anger mix

One minute you miss them; the next you’re furious. Both are part of losing something that was also harming you. See Anger arriving late.

How Helen marked the loss

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Helen’s friends couldn’t understand why she was grieving a man who had hurt her for twelve years. She stopped talking about it, and the grief got heavier. A counsellor suggested she write a letter to the life she’d hoped for: the house, the children growing up with both parents, growing old together. She read it aloud once, cried for an hour, then put it in a drawer.

The grief didn’t vanish. It became something she could carry, instead of something she had to hide.

Name one loss

Write down one thing you’ve lost that nobody has acknowledged. Let yourself be sad about it for ten minutes, on purpose.

If the person you left has died

When an ex who hurt you dies, the feelings can be overwhelming and contradictory: grief, relief, anger, numbness, guilt for feeling relief. You may grieve the chance of an apology that will never come. People may expect you to be either devastated or glad, and you may be neither.

  • The funeral: you don’t have to go. If you do, bring someone, and leave when you need to.
  • The children: they may grieve a parent you feared. Let them, without having to share your feelings about it. A children’s bereavement service can help.
  • Practical matters: if you were still married, or there are children, there may be questions about inheritance, pensions or property. Get legal advice.
  • The fear: some people find their body stays on alert for a while, even knowing the person is gone. That usually eases with time.

There’s no right way to feel. Whatever comes is allowed.

Guilt and shame

Why do I feel guilty after leaving an abusive relationship?

The short answer

Guilt after leaving an abusive relationship is very common: guilt for leaving, for staying so long, for the children, for not seeing it sooner. Much of it was planted by the relationship itself. Guilt says you did something wrong; usually you didn’t. Shame says you are something wrong; you aren’t.

Guilt after leaving an abusive relationship is one of the most common feelings there is. You left, and you feel guilty. For breaking up the family. For staying so long. For the times you went along with it. For not seeing it sooner. For feeling relieved.

Why do I feel guilty after leaving?

Because guilt was often part of how the relationship worked. Controlling people often teach their partners that everything is their fault: the moods, the arguments, the children’s behaviour. That training doesn’t stop when you leave.

The Decoder describes the planting of shame in shame installation, and the steady pressure of guilt in guilt-tripping.

Guilt was one of the tools. It’s still working, even though the person using it is gone.

Was it my fault?

No. Abuse is the responsibility of the person who chose to do it. You may have made choices you’d make differently now. Everyone does. But choices made under pressure, fear and confusion aren’t the cause of someone else’s abuse.

Guilt or shame: what’s the difference?

Guilt says: “I did something wrong.” It can be useful when it’s accurate, because it points to something to repair.

Shame says: “I am something wrong.” It’s never useful. It makes you hide, isolate and believe you deserve bad treatment.

Most of what people feel after abuse is shame wearing guilt’s clothes.

What about the guilt of staying so long?

People stay for many reasons: love, hope, fear, money, children, nowhere to go, a bond built by the abuse itself. See The trauma bond. Staying wasn’t a failure of intelligence or courage. And many people leave more than once before it holds. See If you go back.

What about guilt about the children?

You may feel guilty that they saw things, or that they now have two homes. Children do better in one calm home than in one tense one. Leaving to protect them, or yourself, is a choice made for them, not against them. How children react, and what helps them covers what helps them now.

Guilt as a tool they used

In many controlling relationships, guilt was used on purpose. “After everything I’ve done for you.” “You’re tearing this family apart.” “Look what you made me do.”

Guilt that was trained into you over years doesn’t switch off when you leave. It can keep firing at things that aren’t your fault. When guilt arrives, ask: “Is this my voice, or theirs?”

The common guilts, answered

  • “I broke up the family.” The abuse broke it. Leaving was a response to that.
  • “The children saw too much.” Many parents feel this. Leaving was one of the ways you protected them from seeing more.
  • “I stayed too long.” You stayed as long as you needed to be able to leave safely. That is how leaving works for most people.
  • “I left the pets, the house, their family.” You left what you couldn’t take, to get out safely.
  • “I’m costing everyone money and stress.” Their behaviour is causing the cost.

Shame about what you did to survive

People in abusive relationships often do things they’d never normally do: lie to protect themselves, go along with things they disagreed with, cut off friends, snap at their children, use alcohol to cope. Afterwards, they feel deep shame.

These were survival responses in an impossible situation. Understanding that doesn’t mean pretending they didn’t happen. It means placing them in context, and treating yourself with the understanding you’d give a friend.

Shame in a community or faith

In some communities, separation and divorce carry stigma, or there’s pressure to keep the family together “for the children” or because of religious teaching. You may feel judged by people you’ve known all your life.

Find people inside or outside your community who support you. Many faith traditions have leaders and groups that recognise abuse. You don’t have to choose between your faith and your safety.

Self-compassion that isn’t fluffy

Self-compassion isn’t telling yourself everything’s fine. It’s talking to yourself the way a fair, kind friend would:

  • Acknowledge what’s hard: “This is painful.”
  • Remember you’re not alone: “Lots of people feel this after leaving.”
  • Offer one kind thing: “What would help a bit right now?”

Researchers have found that self-compassion is linked with lower anxiety and depression, and isn’t the same as letting yourself off the hook.

When guilt points to something real

Sometimes guilt is pointing at something you did that hurt someone. If so, you can take responsibility for that thing, make amends where it’s safe and possible, and learn from it. That’s different from carrying blame for the whole relationship. Owning your part doesn’t mean owning theirs.

How do I loosen guilt and shame?

  • Check the facts. Write the guilty thought down. Then ask: what did I know at the time? What choices did I have? What would I say to a friend who wrote this?
  • Say it out loud to someone safe. Shame shrinks when it’s spoken and met with understanding.
  • Notice whose voice it is. Often the critical voice in your head sounds like them.
  • Make repairs where they’re due, and only there. If you snapped at your children, apologise to them. That’s guilt doing its job.
  • Get support if shame is heavy or constant. See Finding a therapist who understands this.

Whose voice is it?

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

Every time Aisha’s children cried at handover, she heard her ex’s voice: “You did this to them.” She felt it as truth. Her therapist asked her to write the sentence down, then write who first said it. It was him, many times, for years. Then she wrote what she’d say to a friend in her position: “You got your children out of a frightening home. Their tears are about the change, not your failure.”

The guilt still came. She started recognising it as an old recording, not a verdict.

Write down the sentence

Write the guilty thought exactly as it sounds in your head. Then write whose words they first were.

Anger, arriving late

Why am I so angry now, long after the narcissistic abuse ended?

The short answer

Anger after narcissistic abuse or any coercive relationship often arrives late, months or years afterwards, when you finally feel safe enough to feel it. It’s a common, healthy sign that you’ve seen what happened clearly. It needs somewhere to go that doesn’t hurt you or the people around you.

In the early months there was fear, relief, grief, exhaustion. Then, a year or more later, something small happens, and you’re furious. Shaking-hands furious.

Why does anger arrive so late?

Because survival came first. In the early period, staying safe and getting through each day took everything you had. Anger needs room, and room needs safety.

Anger also needs a settled sense that what happened was wrong. If self-doubt was part of the abuse, that certainty can take a long time to arrive. When it does, anger often comes with it.

Late anger often means you can finally see what happened clearly.

Is anger after narcissistic abuse normal?

Yes. Anger is common after any kind of trauma, and research links it with post-traumatic stress. Many people describe it as a turning point: the moment they stopped blaming themselves.

What do I do with the anger?

Anger is a physical state as much as a feeling, so it helps to move it through your body:

  • Move: walking fast, running, swimming, hitting a pillow, anything that uses big muscles.
  • Write: an angry letter you never send. Say everything.
  • Say it: to a friend or therapist who can hear it without flinching.
  • Make something: art, music, a garden, anything that turns energy into form.

What doesn’t help:

  • aiming it at people who weren’t responsible, including your children
  • using it as fuel to contact or confront the person you left. That usually restarts the cycle. See Hoovering and No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid.
  • drinking or using drugs to quieten it. See Drinking, drugs and other ways of getting through.

What if my anger frightens me?

If you’re worried you might hurt someone, or you’re lashing out in ways you regret, get support. A therapist can help you understand where it’s coming from and what to do with it. See Finding a therapist who understands this. If you’re being accused of being the angry one, see When they say you’re the abuser.

Will it pass?

For most people, it settles over time into something quieter. It may come back briefly, years later, on an anniversary or when something reminds you. That doesn’t mean anything has gone wrong.

Is it normal to be angry with myself?

Very. Many people are angry that they stayed, that they didn’t see it, or that they went back. That anger usually belongs to the person who did the harm. See Guilt and shame.

What if I’m angry at the people who didn’t help?

Friends who didn’t believe you, family who stayed neutral, services that let you down. That anger makes sense too. You get to decide what to do with those relationships. See The friends who didn’t believe you.

Letting anger out safely

Anger needs somewhere to go. Some safe places:

  • Your body: fast walking, running, swimming, hitting a pillow, shouting into a cushion, physical work.
  • Paper: write it all, uncensored, then destroy it if you like.
  • A person: a friend who can hear it without being frightened, a therapist, a support group.

What tends not to help: sending angry messages, venting online, or replaying arguments in your head for hours.

Anger in the body

Anger shows up physically: a tight jaw, clenched fists, a hot face, a racing heart. Noticing these signs early gives you a moment to choose what to do next. Slow breathing out, cold water on your face or stepping outside can take the edge off before it spills.

Anger and the children

Your children may see your anger. That’s okay, as long as it’s not aimed at them and doesn’t frighten them. You can name it: “I’m feeling cross about something grown-up. You haven’t done anything wrong. I’m going to take a few minutes.” Avoid venting about the other parent in front of them.

Anger at the system

Many people feel furious at courts, police, social workers or family members who didn’t help. That anger is often justified. You can use it to complain, to ask for reviews, or to support others later. Try not to let it make you give up on all help; some people and services will get it right.

When anger gets stuck

Sometimes anger becomes a constant hum, and stays for years. It can keep you tied to the person you left. If you find you’re thinking about them angrily most days, long after leaving, it may help to talk to a therapist about what the anger is protecting. Often it’s grief or fear underneath.

Can anger be useful?

Yes. Anger can power a boundary, a complaint, a court application, or help for someone else. Used that way, it becomes energy for your life, not fuel for theirs.

When the anger wants something

An honest check, just for you. This is about what’s in your hands now, not blame for what was done to you.

Anger is fair. It can also start making plans. Ask yourself:

  • Do I want them to hurt the way I did?
  • Am I tempted to post, expose, or send something I can’t take back?
  • Would I be comfortable with a judge or my children seeing what I’m about to do?
  • Is my anger aimed at the right person, or landing on the people near me?

If any answer worries you, wait a day, and put the anger somewhere safe first: a run, a notebook, a friend.

When the anger came

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

For months after leaving, Pete felt nothing but tired. Then, about a year later, he was suddenly furious: at his ex, at himself, at the friends who’d laughed off what he told them. It frightened him. He started running three times a week, wrote angry letters he never sent and talked about it in a men’s support group.

The anger didn’t disappear, but it changed shape. He used some of it to write a clear statement for court, and some of it to set limits with people who’d let him down.

Somewhere for it to go

Pick one safe outlet for anger this week, like a run, a notebook or a call, and use it the next time anger comes.

Checking their profile

Why do I keep checking my ex’s social media, and how do I stop?

The short answer

Checking your ex’s social media after an abusive relationship is common and hard to stop, because it feeds the same craving as contact. It also slows recovery. Muting, blocking, asking a friend to tell you anything you need to know, and replacing the habit with something else all help.

It’s late. You tell yourself you’ll just have a quick look. Forty minutes later you’ve seen their new partner’s holiday photos, read their cryptic post, and feel worse than you have all week.

Why do I keep checking my ex’s social media?

For the same reasons you miss them. The bond is still there, and checking is a way of getting a little contact without making contact. Each check gives a small hit: relief, anger, curiosity, hurt. Unpredictable rewards are the hardest habits to break. The Decoder explains this pull in push-pull.

There’s also fear. You want to know what they’re doing, who they’re telling about you, whether they’re coming for you. And some people check hoping for a sign they’ll come back, which is the opening hoovering relies on.

Each check restarts the pull, and often costs you the rest of the evening.

Does checking slow recovery?
