If you’re back, keep one link to help: a saved helpline number, one friend who knows, a small amount of money somewhere safe. You don’t have to decide anything else today.

Helping someone in year one

How do I help a friend who left an abusive relationship?

The short answer

To help a friend who left an abusive relationship: believe them, let their feelings be complicated, don’t push a timeline, don’t constantly attack their ex, and stay around for the long haul. What feels helpful and what helps aren’t always the same thing.

This chapter is written to be forwarded, in full, to whoever is supporting someone in the first year after leaving. It’s short on purpose.

How do I help a friend who left an abusive relationship?

Believe them, fully, the first time. Not with careful questions that sound like you’re checking their story. See The friends who didn’t believe you for why this matters so much.

Let their feelings be complicated. They may grieve, miss the person, feel relief and sadness at once. None of this means they made a mistake, and none of it needs correcting. See What you’re allowed to feel about someone you left.

Don’t push a timeline. Questions about whether they’re “over it yet,” or about dating, land as pressure, however kind the intention.

Don’t keep attacking their ex, even if you’re furious on their behalf. It can make some people defend or minimise, because it’s easier than managing your anger on top of their own.

Help with the practical. Meals, lifts to court, an hour with the children, sitting with them while they read messages.

Learn the safety basics. Don’t share their location or news with mutual friends. Don’t pass on messages from their ex. See A safety plan for after you’ve left.

What feels helpful and what helps are sometimes different things. Believing them, being steady, and following their lead beats advice, deadlines and even righteous anger.

What if they go back?

Stay in touch. Don’t say “I told you so.” People often leave more than once, and they’re more likely to leave for good if they still have people to come back to. See If you go back.

What if I’m worried about their safety?

Tell them, once, calmly. Share Warning signs to take seriously. If you think they’re in immediate danger, call emergency services. If they talk about harming themselves, see When it’s more than aftermath.

The single most useful thing you can do

Stay. Check in next month, and the month after, not just in the first hard week. If you support survivors for work, see For professionals. And look after yourself too. See The friends who did, and are tired.

What should I say, and what should I avoid?

Helpful:

  • “I believe you.”
  • “That sounds exhausting.”
  • “What would help this week?”
  • “You don’t have to decide anything today.”

Best avoided:

  • “Why didn’t you leave sooner?”
  • “Just ignore them.”
  • “You need to move on.”
  • “At least you’re out now.”

How can I help with the practical side?

Offer lifts to court or appointments, childcare, a meal, an hour sitting with them while they read messages, help finding a lawyer, or a safe place to keep documents and a ready bag. See Find help.

What year one tends to look like

  • The first weeks: shock, practical crises, safety. They need help with tasks.
  • Months 2 to 6: court, money, children, exhaustion. Ups and downs. They may miss the person they left.
  • Months 6 to 12: some steadiness, then anniversaries and setbacks. People around them often expect them to be “over it” by now.

Your support matters at every stage, and often most in the middle months, when others drift away.

Practical help by stage

  • Early: a bed, a lift, childcare, meals, help with forms, keeping documents safe.
  • Middle: company at court or appointments, help finding lawyers and services, regular check-ins.
  • Later: invitations to ordinary things, celebrating small wins, remembering the anniversaries.

Helping with the children

Offering childcare during court dates or appointments is one of the most useful things you can do. Keep the children’s routines familiar. Don’t ask them about the other parent or the separation, and don’t criticise the other parent in front of them.

What not to do online

  • Don’t post about the abuse or the person they left.
  • Don’t tag them in posts or photos that show where they are.
  • Don’t confront the person they left on social media.
  • Don’t share their story without permission.

Looking after yourself as the helper

Supporting someone through abuse is draining. Set limits on what you can give, and say so kindly. Talk to someone about how it’s affecting you. Many domestic abuse helplines also support friends and family.

What a good friend did

This example is made up, built from patterns many people describe.

When Zoe’s friend left her husband, Zoe didn’t give advice. She drove her to court, minded the children during appointments and sent a message every few days that didn’t ask for anything back. When her friend went back to him for a month, Zoe didn’t say “I told you so.” She said, “I’m still here.” When her friend left again, Zoe was the first person she called.

Her friend said later: “You never made me choose between you and him. That’s why I could choose me.”

One practical offer

Offer your friend one concrete thing this week: a lift, a meal, an hour of childcare, a sit with them while they read the messages.

What this book might have wrong

How strong is the research behind this book on post-separation abuse?

The short answer

Post-separation abuse research is growing but still young. Some of what this book says is well supported, some is disputed, and some is this book’s own judgment. Here is which is which, published rather than buried.

This library holds itself to a simple standard: say what you know, what’s disputed, and what’s your own judgment.

What is well supported?

  • Separation is a time of raised risk, especially from very controlling partners. See Why it often gets worse first.
  • Abuse often continues after separation through children, money, courts and other people.
  • Past strangulation is a strong warning sign of later serious harm.
  • Stress and trauma affect sleep, memory and the nervous system.
  • Children are affected by coercive control directly, whether or not they see violence.
  • Many people leave more than once before leaving for good.
  • Ongoing conflict between parents harms children.

What is disputed?

  • How well family courts recognise coercive control, and how fast that’s changing. See Family court, honestly.
  • “Parental alienation”: how common it is, how to judge it, and whether claims of it are being used against parents who report abuse.
  • “Trauma bond” as a concept: widely used and recognised, but not a formal diagnosis, and not tested the way a clinical condition would be.
  • Grey rock: widely recommended by survivors and practitioners, with very little formal research.
  • Exact figures: how common post-separation abuse is, how many attempts it takes to leave.

What is this book’s own judgment?

  • The exact wording in Words you can use, drawn from practice, not controlled trials.
  • The conversational guidance on children, parents and new partners.
  • The working definition of recovery in What “recovered” means.
  • The order of steps in The first 30 days.

What is the biggest risk this book carries?

That the general patterns won’t match your life. Laws vary by country and region, and people vary even more. If your experience differs from what’s described here, it isn’t less real. Trust your own situation over this book’s generalisations wherever the two disagree.

What would change this book’s mind?

  • If separation were shown not to raise risk compared with staying, the safety chapters would need revising.
  • If hoovering were shown to be rare, the hoovering chapter would be overstating it.
  • If parallel parenting or written-only contact were shown to harm children, those chapters would change.
  • If the scripts in this book were shown to escalate conflict more than other approaches, they’d be rewritten.

How is this book checked?

Every chapter lists its sources. Legal notes are dated and need checking before you rely on them. Corrections are welcome. The book’s version history is kept on its main page.

How do I report an error?

If you find something wrong, especially a phone number, a law or a source, please report it through the Noble Father Creations homepage. Corrections are made and dated in the updates on the book’s main page.

What kinds of sources this book uses

The book draws on several kinds of evidence:

  • Research studies, listed by author and year, mostly from peer-reviewed journals.
  • Laws and official guidance, from government and court websites.
  • Specialist organisations, like national helplines and charities, for practical advice.
  • Clinical guidelines, like NICE in the UK, for health and therapy.
  • Experience, of people who’ve lived through it and those who support them, where research is thin.

Each chapter lists what it used, and Every source in this book gathers them all on one page, with links.

Where things change fastest

Laws, helpline numbers, benefits, court procedures and technology change often. Always check the current position where you live, especially for anything legal or financial. If you find something out of date, please report it.

What the book leaves out

This book can’t cover every country’s law, every culture, every situation. It’s written mainly with English-speaking countries in mind. It doesn’t give detailed legal advice for your case, and it can’t replace a lawyer, doctor or therapist who knows your situation.

Reading research claims online

When you see a claim online, like “it takes seven attempts to leave” or “narcissists never change,” ask:

  • Who said it, and how do they know?
  • Is there a study, and how many people were in it?
  • Is it a single study, or what most research finds?
  • Is someone selling something?

Healthy scepticism protects you from confident claims that aren’t true.

Tell us what we got wrong

If you find an error, an out-of-date number or something that doesn’t fit your experience, please report it. Every correction makes the book better for the next reader.

The long after

What does life after narcissistic abuse look like, years on?

The short answer

Life after narcissistic abuse, years on, isn’t a straight line, and it doesn’t have to be. What’s available is a life that isn’t organised around what happened: relationships built on trust, tastes that are yours, anger that has mostly settled, and the capacity to choose.

This isn’t a straight line, and it was never going to be. Some weeks feel like real progress. Some feel like every chapter of this book arriving at once, out of nowhere. Both are part of the same process. Neither one is the “real” direction.

What does life after narcissistic abuse look like, years later?

People who are years out describe things like this:

  • A life that isn’t organised around what happened. Days go by without thinking about them.
  • Relationships built on trust that was given slowly and earned. See Trust as a practice.
  • Tastes and habits that are simply yours, no longer a recovery project. See Your own tastes, rediscovered.
  • Anger that has mostly settled, with an occasional brief return that doesn’t mean anything has gone wrong. See Anger, arriving late.
  • A steadier body, with fewer jolts and better sleep.
  • Clearer judgment about people, and limits held without effort.
  • For some, contact that still exists because of children, but takes up a small corner of life instead of the centre.

Recovery leaves the past in place and gives you back your capacity to choose, to trust, to want things, and to build a life that isn’t defined by what you left.

What about the hard days, years on?

They still happen: an anniversary, a court letter, a child’s question, seeing them in town. They tend to pass faster, and to knock you down less far. If one turns into more than a hard week, When it’s more than aftermath is still here.

Where to go from here

  • When a week goes badly: What your nervous system is doing explains what your body is doing.
  • When the anger comes late: Anger, arriving late.
  • When they come back: Hoovering.
  • When you’re helping someone else: Helping someone in year one.
  • When you just need the short version: The card.

The close

Leaving was the middle, not the end. The first chapter said so, and everything since has been the part that comes after the door. This is the rest of it: unglamorous, uneven, and yours.

What do people wish they’d known in year one?

  • It doesn’t go in a straight line, and that’s normal.
  • The hard days get shorter, even when they still come.
  • The person you left doesn’t get to define the rest of your life.
  • You can build a good life alongside a difficult co-parent.
  • Asking for help sooner would have made it lighter.
  • Writing things down saved them more than once.

Will I ever stop thinking about it?

For most people it becomes a smaller part of life: remembered, sometimes painful, no longer in charge. You may think about it on certain days for a long time. That doesn’t mean it still runs your life.

What people say, years on

People who left abusive relationships years ago often say:

  • the person takes up far less space in their mind
  • they still have hard days, but they recover faster
  • they trust themselves more than they did before the relationship
  • they’re proud of leaving, even though it was the hardest thing they did
  • their life is full of things that have nothing to do with the abuse

The long tail

Some things can last for years: co-parenting, occasional court, anniversaries, a memory that surprises you. That’s what the long after looks like. Most people find that the long tail becomes background, not foreground.

What you might do with what you know

In time, you may find you know things that could help someone else: how to spot the patterns, what helped, what to avoid. You might share it with a friend, volunteer, or just be a calmer, wiser presence for someone in the thick of it. Or you might simply live your life. Both are good uses of what you’ve learned.

A last word

You left. Then you kept going, through everything that came after. Whatever today looks like, that’s worth remembering.

A note to come back to

Write down today’s date and one sentence about where you are. Put it somewhere you’ll find it in a year. When you read it, you’ll see how far the long after has carried you.

The record

A simple abuse documentation template you can copy.

The short answer

This abuse documentation template has four lines per incident: when, what happened, who saw it, and what it changed. Fill it in the same day. Below it is a timeline page for when you need to show a lawyer, a court or the police the pattern.

Use this abuse documentation template for every incident, however small.

Keep this somewhere they can’t reach: a notebook away from home, or an account only you can open. See Surveillance and technology.

The four-line entry

Copy this for each incident.

When: (date, and time if you have it)

What happened: (the act, in plain words: what was said or done)

Who saw it: (a name, or “nobody”)

What it changed: (the missed appointment, the money not paid, the child upset, the route you changed)

Evidence: (screenshot, photo, police reference number, if any)

A filled-in example

When: Friday 7 March, 5:40pm

What happened: Arrived 40 minutes late for handover. Said in front of the children, “Your mum made us wait again.”

Who saw it: The children; my neighbour Sam was outside.

What it changed: Missed my evening shift; daughter cried at bedtime.

Evidence: App message at 5:10pm saying “on my way.”

The timeline page

When you need to show the pattern, list every entry in date order, one line each:

DateWhatTypeEvidence
7 Mar40 min late, comment in front of childrenHandoverApp message
12 Mar£200 support paid 9 days lateMoneyBank statement
15 Mar14 messages outside the appContactScreenshots

Then add one summary line at the top: what the pattern is, over what period, with a count. “Since the order on 4 June: 11 late handovers, 4 late payments, 63 messages outside the agreed app.”

What to document and how explains why each part matters. The Sovereign Divine Feminine has an evidence routine in Appendix B, Liberation Logistics.

What not to write

  • Insults or speculation about their character. Stick to what happened.
  • Anything you’d be uncomfortable with a judge reading.
  • Plans to leave or move, if there’s a chance they’ll find the record.
  • Other people’s private details beyond what’s needed.

Keeping it safe

Keep the record where they can’t find or delete it: a notebook kept away from home, an email account only you can open, or a cloud folder with a new password. Back it up. If you’re worried someone might see it, give a copy to a trusted friend or advocate.

Turning it into a statement

When you need it for a court or police statement, pull out the most important entries in date order, summarise the pattern in a paragraph, and attach evidence. A lawyer or advocate can help. Keep the original record as well.

This safety plan worksheet for domestic violence is for life after you’ve left.

  • ☐ documents and court orders ☐ cash and card ☐ medicines ☐ charger ☐ spare keys ☐ written list of numbers

A safety plan for after you’ve left explains each section. Warning signs to take seriously helps you judge the risk. The Sovereign Divine Feminine has a go-bag list for leaving in Appendix B, Liberation Logistics.

Device and account audit

How to check your phone for stalkerware, and every other way you might be tracked.

The short answer

This checklist shows how to check your phone for stalkerware, and goes through shared accounts, location sharing, trackers and home devices. Do it from a device your ex never touched, in a planned order, because sudden changes can alert someone who is watching.

Here is how to check your phone for stalkerware, step by step, and everything else that can track you.

If there’s any risk of violence, plan the order with a domestic abuse service first. Removing tracking can alert the person watching.

Accounts (start with email)

  • ☐ Email: new password, two-step login on, backup codes on paper
  • ☐ Apple ID or Google account: password, devices list, family sharing
  • ☐ Banking and payment apps
  • ☐ Social media: password, logged-in devices, location tags off, account private
  • ☐ Cloud photos and storage: shared albums removed
  • ☐ Shopping, food delivery, streaming, ride apps
  • ☐ Removed as trusted contact, recovery contact or family member everywhere
  • ☐ Security questions changed (to answers they can’t guess)

Location

  • ☐ iPhone: Settings → your name → Find My → who can see my location
  • ☐ Android: Google Maps → Location sharing; Settings → Google → Location
  • ☐ Life360, Snap Map, fitness and family apps checked
  • ☐ Car’s own app checked

The phone itself

  • ☐ Apps list checked for anything I don’t recognise
  • ☐ Apps with access to location, messages, microphone checked
  • ☐ Phone allows apps from outside the store? (if I didn’t set that up, it’s a warning sign)
  • ☐ Security app with stalkerware detection run
  • ☐ If anything found: photographed, not deleted yet, advice sought

Trackers

  • ☐ iPhone alerts for unknown AirTags noted
  • ☐ Android unknown tracker scan run (Settings → Safety and emergency)
  • ☐ Car checked: bumpers, wheel arches, under seats, boot, glovebox, OBD port
  • ☐ Bags, coats and children’s belongings checked after visits

Home

  • ☐ Cameras, doorbells, speakers and thermostats reset or removed
  • ☐ Wi-Fi password changed; router admin password changed

Afterwards

  • ☐ Findings recorded. See The record.
  • ☐ Repeat after handovers if devices travel between homes

Surveillance and technology explains each step. The Decoder describes the pattern in monitoring and surveillance. The Sovereign Divine Feminine covers phones as control in The Digital Leash.

If you find something

If you find tracking software, a hidden device or someone logged into your account:

  1. Pause before removing it. Removing it can alert them.
  2. Photograph or screenshot what you found.
  3. Talk to a domestic abuse service or police about the safest next step.
  4. Then remove it, change passwords from a safe device, and turn on two-step login.

Children’s devices

Check the children’s phones, tablets, watches and games consoles too: location sharing, family accounts, apps you don’t recognise. Do it with them where possible, calmly, without blaming them.

Court day checklist

A family court checklist for before, during and after the hearing.

The short answer

This family court checklist covers the week before (protections, folder, plan), the day itself (what to bring and how to speak) and afterwards (the order, copies, safety). Print it or copy it into your phone.

Use this family court checklist for any hearing.

The week before

  • ☐ Asked the court for protections: separate waiting room, screen or video link, separate entrance or times
  • ☐ Supporter arranged, and court asked if they can come in
  • ☐ Folder: documents in date order, timeline summary on top, key evidence flagged
  • ☐ List of the points I want to make, in order
  • ☐ Travel and parking planned; plan to leave without meeting them
  • ☐ Childcare arranged
  • ☐ Someone to check on me after

What to bring

  • ☐ Folder and a spare copy of key documents
  • ☐ Notebook and pen
  • ☐ ID and the hearing letter
  • ☐ Water, snack, charger, something to read
  • ☐ Tissues

On the day

  • ☐ Arrive early; tell the usher about protections
  • ☐ Don’t agree to anything outside the room I’m unsure of; ask for time
  • ☐ Speak slowly; answer the question asked; then stop
  • ☐ Facts, dates, effect on the children
  • ☐ If I freeze: ask for a moment, breathe, look at my notes
  • ☐ Speak only to the judge, not to them

After

  • ☐ Copy of the order, or know when it arrives
  • ☐ Order read; dates and conditions checked
  • ☐ Copies to school and anyone who needs them
  • ☐ Safety plan updated for the next few days. See A safety plan for after you’ve left.
  • ☐ Something gentle planned for myself

Your day in court explains each step, and Family court, honestly gives the bigger picture.

If you represent yourself

  • Read any guidance the court website offers for people without a lawyer.
  • Bring three copies of every document: one for you, one for the judge, one for the other side.
  • Write a short list of the points you want to make, and the order you’ll make them in.
  • Ask the court about a support worker or a “McKenzie friend” (in England and Wales), who can sit with you and take notes.
  • If you don’t understand something, say so and ask for it to be explained.

Words you can use: the pocket card

How to reply to an abusive ex, on one page.

The short answer

How to reply to an abusive ex, in a few lines: keep it brief, factual, polite and final, and answer only what needs answering. These are the replies most people need most often.

Not sure how to reply to an abusive ex? Start here.

Accusation: “I don’t agree with that. Pickup is still 5pm Friday.”

Rant or insult: No reply, or “I’ll respond to anything about the children’s arrangements.”

Last-minute change: “The agreed time is 5pm. If you can’t make that, let me know by Thursday.”

“Answer me now”: “I’ve received your message. I’ll reply by Wednesday.”

Questions about my life: “That’s private. Is there anything about the children you need?”

Guilt: “I understand you’re unhappy with it. It stays as agreed.”

Threat of court: “That’s your choice. I’ll follow the current order.”

“Emergency”: “If it’s an emergency, please call [emergency number]. If it’s about the children, I’m happy to discuss it here.”

“Let’s just talk”: “I’ll only communicate in writing about the children.”

Apology or “I’ve changed”: “Thank you. I’ll keep our contact to the children’s arrangements.”

Closing contact: “I’ve read your message. I’m not going to reopen contact. Please don’t contact me again.”

To a friend passing messages: “Please don’t pass on messages. If it matters, they can contact me in writing.”

Before sending: Would I be fine with a judge reading this? Then wait an hour.

More in Words you can use, Messages you don’t have to answer and Hoovering.

Find help

Domestic abuse help near me: numbers and the words to search.

The short answer

Domestic abuse help near you is usually one phone call away. Here are national helplines for the US, UK, Canada, Australia and Ireland, crisis lines, specialist services, and the exact words to search to find local help anywhere else.

Domestic abuse help near me: start with your country’s national helpline below.

In danger now: call your emergency number. Thinking about harming yourself: call a crisis line now, and see When it’s more than aftermath.

United States

  • Emergency: 911
  • National Domestic Violence Hotline: 1-800-799-7233, or text START to 88788
  • 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline: call or text 988
  • RAINN (sexual assault): 1-800-656-4673
  • love is respect (young people): 1-866-331-9474, or text LOVEIS to 22522
  • SAMHSA (drugs and alcohol): 1-800-662-4357

  • Emergency: 999 (can’t speak? call from a mobile and press 55 when asked; say your location if you can)
  • National Domestic Abuse Helpline (England): 0808 2000 247
  • Scotland’s Domestic Abuse and Forced Marriage Helpline: 0800 027 1234
  • Live Fear Free (Wales): 0808 80 10 800
  • Men’s Advice Line: 0808 801 0327
  • Galop (LGBT+): 0800 999 5428
  • Karma Nirvana (honour-based abuse, forced marriage): 0800 5999 247
  • Rape Crisis (England and Wales): 0808 500 2222
  • National Stalking Helpline (Suzy Lamplugh Trust)
  • Revenge Porn Helpline
  • Samaritans: 116 123

Canada

  • Emergency: 911
  • Find a shelter: sheltersafe.ca
  • Connect to local services: 211
  • Crisis line: call or text 988

Australia

  • Emergency: 000
  • 1800RESPECT: 1800 737 732
  • Lifeline: 13 11 14
  • National Alcohol and Other Drug Hotline: 1800 250 015
  • eSafety Commissioner (image-based abuse): esafety.gov.au

Ireland

  • Emergency: 112 or 999
  • Women’s Aid: 1800 341 900
  • Men’s Aid: 01 554 3811
  • Rape Crisis Centre: 1800 77 8888
  • Samaritans: 116 123
  • HSE Drugs and Alcohol Helpline: 1800 459 459

Anywhere else: what to search

  • “[your country] domestic abuse helpline”
  • “[your country] emergency number”
  • “[your country] crisis line”
  • “[your area] legal aid domestic abuse”
  • “[your area] protective order”

Where to find the right body explains which agency does what.

More countries

  • New Zealand: Women’s Refuge crisis line, 0800 733 843. Shine, 0508 744 633. Family violence information line, 0800 456 450.
  • South Africa: Gender-Based Violence Command Centre, 0800 428 428. If it doesn’t connect, call the police on 10111.
  • India: Women Helpline, 181. Emergency, 112.

Help by need

  • Men: UK Men’s Advice Line, 0808 801 0327. In the US, the National Domestic Violence Hotline helps everyone.
  • LGBTQ+: UK Galop, 0800 999 5428. Elsewhere, search “LGBTQ domestic violence [your area].”
  • Older people: UK Hourglass, 0808 808 8141, open day and night.
  • Deaf people: in the UK, SignHealth offers domestic abuse support in British Sign Language. In the US, the National Domestic Violence Hotline has options for Deaf callers; search “Deaf domestic violence hotline” for the current details.
  • Young people: US love is respect, 1-866-331-9474, or text LOVEIS to 22522. UK Childline, 0800 1111.

A to Z: the words, plainly

What do these post-separation abuse terms mean?

The short answer

The post-separation abuse terms people search for, from address confidentiality to vexatious litigant, each explained in a line or two, with a link to the chapter that covers it and, where there is one, the Pattern Decoder page.

These are the post-separation abuse terms people search for, explained plainly.

Address confidentiality program. A substitute address for public records, so an ex can’t find you through them. Your address, your records, your mail.

BIFF. A way of replying to hostile messages: brief, informative, friendly, firm. Words you can use.

Blame-shifting. Making you responsible for what they did. Guilt and shame; Decoder: blame-shifting.

Breadcrumbing. Small scraps of attention that keep you hoping, with nothing behind them. Hoovering; Decoder: breadcrumbing.

Cafcass. The service in England that advises family courts about children, often by writing a report. Family court, honestly.

Clare’s Law. A scheme in the UK that lets you ask the police whether a partner has a history of abuse. Police and reporting.

Coerced debt. Debt in your name that you didn’t freely agree to. Money as the channel; Decoder: coerced debt.

Coercive control. A pattern of behaviour that takes away someone’s freedom and sense of self. A crime in some places. What post-separation abuse is.

Collect and Pay. The UK Child Maintenance Service option where the service collects payments, so you don’t deal with the other parent directly. Money as the channel.

Complex PTSD. A recognised condition linked to prolonged trauma, with lasting effects on emotions, self-worth and relationships. What your nervous system is doing.

DARVO. Deny, attack, reverse victim and offender. When they say you’re the abuser; Decoder: DARVO.

DASH. A checklist many UK services use to assess the risk of serious harm. Why it often gets worse first.

Discard. Ending a relationship suddenly and coldly, often after blaming you for everything. If they left you; Decoder: blame and discard.

Dissociation. Feeling far away, numb or unreal, a survival response that can continue after leaving. What your nervous system is doing.

Economic abuse. Using money to control: restricting it, taking it, or running up debt. Money as the channel; Decoder: financial control.

EMDR. Eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing, a therapy recommended for PTSD. Finding a therapist who understands this.

Fact-finding hearing. A family court hearing that decides whether disputed allegations, such as abuse, happened, before deciding arrangements for children. When they say you’re the abuser.

Fawning. Going along with someone to keep the peace, a survival response. What your nervous system is doing; Decoder: fawning.

Flying monkeys. People who carry an abuser’s messages or pressure to you. Your family and mutual friends; Decoder: proxy recruitment.

Future faking. Big promises about the future to pull you in or back. Hoovering; Decoder: future faking.

Gaslighting. Making you doubt your memory or perception. Memory, and doubting yourself; Decoder: gaslighting.

Grey rock. Being as dull and unreactive as possible in necessary contact. Grey rock and written-only contact.

Guardian ad litem. In the US, a person appointed to represent a child’s interests in a court case; a custody evaluator plays a similar role. Family court, honestly.

Hague Convention. An international agreement for returning children taken across borders without consent. Moving, passports and travel with children.

Hoovering. Trying to pull a former partner back into contact. Hoovering; Decoder: hoovering.

Hypervigilance. Staying on high alert after the danger has passed. What your nervous system is doing.

IDVA. Independent Domestic Violence Adviser: a UK specialist who supports people at higher risk and works with agencies for them. Where to find the right body.

Intermittent reinforcement. Unpredictable warmth and cruelty, which builds a strong bond. The trauma bond; Decoder: push-pull.

Intimate image abuse. Sharing, or threatening to share, intimate images without consent. Intimate images and online threats; Decoder: intimate image abuse.

Isolation. Cutting someone off from friends and family. Making new people from scratch; Decoder: isolation.

Legal abuse syndrome. A popular term, not a diagnosis, for the stress caused by long or abusive court cases. Systems abuse and litigation abuse; Decoder: legal abuse.

Limerence. An intense, obsessive longing for someone, which can sit alongside a trauma bond. The trauma bond.

Litigation abuse. Using courts to harass or wear someone down. Systems abuse and litigation abuse; Decoder: legal abuse.

Love bombing. Overwhelming attention and affection early on, or on return. Dating, when you’re ready and when you’re not; Decoder: love bombing.

MARAC. A UK meeting where police, health, housing and support services plan together for someone at high risk. Why it often gets worse first.

McKenzie friend. In England and Wales, a person who sits with you in court to support you and take notes when you don’t have a lawyer. Court-day checklist.

Minimisation. Making what happened sound smaller than it was. Abuse, or a hard breakup?; Decoder: minimization.

No contact. No communication of any kind. No contact, low contact, or contact you can’t avoid.

Non-molestation order. A UK court order that stops someone using or threatening violence, harassing or intimidating you. Protective orders.

NPD. Narcissistic personality disorder, a clinical diagnosis only a trained clinician can make. Is my ex a narcissist?.

Occupation order. A court order in England and Wales deciding who can live in the family home. Still under the same roof.

Parallel parenting. Raising children in two homes with minimal contact between parents. Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.

Parental alienation. A disputed term for one parent turning a child against the other. Family court, honestly.

PEP. Post-exposure prophylaxis: medicine that can prevent HIV if started within 72 hours. If there was sexual abuse or reproductive coercion.

Planning Together for Children. An online course the family court in England and Wales may refer parents to. Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.

Post-separation abuse. Control, fear or punishment that continues after a relationship ends. What post-separation abuse is.

Power and Control Wheel. A 1984 diagram from Duluth, Minnesota, showing the tactics of control; post-separation versions exist. What post-separation abuse is.

Prohibited steps order. A court order in England and Wales that stops a parent doing something, such as taking a child abroad. Moving, passports and travel with children.

Protective order. A court order to stay away and stop contact. Protective orders.

Reactive abuse. A popular term for provoking someone until they react, then using the reaction against them. When they say you’re the abuser; Decoder: baiting.

Reproductive coercion. Control over contraception, pregnancy or fertility. If there was sexual abuse or reproductive coercion; Decoder: reproductive coercion.

Sanctuary scheme. Extra security fitted to your home so you can stay there safely. Housing and benefits.

Shuttle mediation. Mediation where you and your ex stay in separate rooms and the mediator moves between you. Divorce and separation proceedings.

Silent treatment. Withdrawing communication as punishment. Messages you don’t have to answer; Decoder: silent treatment.

Smear campaign. A sustained effort to damage your reputation. The narrative campaign; Decoder: smear campaigns.

Special measures. Protections for witnesses in court, like screens or giving evidence by video. Police and reporting.

Stalking. A pattern of unwanted pursuit that causes fear. Stalking and harassment after a breakup; Decoder: location tracking.

Stalkerware. Hidden software that reports what you do on your phone. Surveillance and technology.

Stockholm syndrome. A bond with a captor, named after a 1973 bank siege. Related to a trauma bond, but not the same. The trauma bond.

Stonewalling. Refusing to engage, as a tactic. Grey rock and written-only contact; Decoder: stonewalling.

StopNCII. A free tool that helps block intimate images from being shared, without uploading them. Intimate images and online threats.

Trauma bond. A strong attachment to someone who hurt you, built by cycles of harm and warmth. The trauma bond; Decoder: trauma bonding.

Triangulation. Bringing a third person in to unsettle or compete. The new partner; Decoder: triangulation.

VAWA. The US Violence Against Women Act, which includes housing and immigration protections for survivors of any gender. Housing and benefits.

Vexatious litigant. Someone a court has restricted from filing repeated cases without permission. Systems abuse and litigation abuse; Decoder: legal abuse.

Questions people ask

The post-separation abuse questions people search for most, answered briefly.

The short answer

The post-separation abuse questions people ask most, each with a short, plain answer and a link to the chapter with the full detail.

These are the post-separation abuse questions people ask most.

What is post-separation abuse? Control, fear or punishment from a former partner that continues after the relationship ends, through children, money, courts, other people or technology. Read: What post-separation abuse is.

Is post-separation abuse a crime? Parts of it are crimes almost everywhere (threats, stalking, breaking an order), and coercive control itself is a crime in some places. Read: What post-separation abuse is.

Does post-separation abuse ever end? For many people it fades or stops; for some it continues in a smaller form for years. Read: When it stops.

Is leaving the most dangerous time? It can be, especially with a very controlling partner. Plan for safety. Read: Why it often gets worse first.

What is hoovering? An attempt to pull you back into contact, often just as you’re doing well. Read: Hoovering.

Will my ex come back? Many do, at least once. You don’t owe a reply. Read: Hoovering.

What is the difference between hoovering and breadcrumbing? Hoovering pulls you back into contact; breadcrumbing keeps you hoping with scraps. Read: Hoovering.

What is a trauma bond? A strong attachment to someone who hurt you, built by cycles of harm and warmth. Read: The trauma bond: why you miss someone who hurt you.

How long does a trauma bond last? There’s no fixed time; it usually weakens once contact stops, in waves. Read: The trauma bond: why you miss someone who hurt you.

Is a trauma bond the same as Stockholm syndrome? Related, not the same: Stockholm syndrome describes a short ordeal with a captor; a trauma bond grows inside a relationship. Read: The trauma bond: why you miss someone who hurt you.

What are examples of trauma bonding? Feeling relief that feels like love after cruelty; defending them; unblocking them the same night. Read: The trauma bond: why you miss someone who hurt you.

Why do I miss my abuser? Because the relationship wasn’t only harm, and the bond was built strong. Missing isn’t wanting back. Read: What you’re allowed to feel about someone you left.

Is my ex a narcissist? Only a clinician can diagnose that, and you don’t need a diagnosis to protect yourself. Read: Is my ex a narcissist?.

What do narcissists do after a breakup? Common patterns: silence, a smear campaign, fast replacement, and a return. Read: Is my ex a narcissist?.

Can you co-parent with a narcissist? Usually not in the usual way; parallel parenting works better. Read: Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.

What is parallel parenting? Two homes, minimal written contact, and a detailed plan. Read: Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.

Do I have to take a co-parenting class? Many courts require one. If there was abuse, ask to attend separately. Read: Co-parenting or parallel parenting?.

What is the grey rock method? Short, flat, factual replies that give them nothing to react to. Not for every situation. Read: Grey rock and written-only contact.

Should I respond to my ex? Only when a message needs an answer: children, court, money with a deadline. Read: Messages you don’t have to answer.

How do I document post-separation abuse? Four lines, the same day: when, what, who saw it, what it changed. Read: What to document and how.

What is litigation abuse? Using the courts to harass or exhaust you. Courts can limit it. Read: Systems abuse and litigation abuse.

What is legal abuse syndrome? A popular term, not a medical diagnosis, for the stress caused by abusive court cases. Read: Systems abuse and litigation abuse.

Is coercive control grounds for divorce? Most places now allow no-fault divorce, so you don’t need grounds; the abuse can still matter for children and safety. Read: Divorce and separation proceedings.

Where can I get free legal help? Legal aid, domestic abuse legal services, law clinics and court help desks. Read: Legal help with little or no money.

How does a protective order work? A court order to stay away and stop contact; breaking it can lead to arrest. Read: Protective orders.

Can I report post-separation abuse to the police? Yes. Describe the pattern and ask for a reference number. Read: Police and reporting.

What is reactive abuse? Being provoked until you react, then having the reaction used against you. Read: When they say you’re the abuser.

Is my ex tracking me? Check accounts, location sharing, your phone and your car, safely. Read: Surveillance and technology.

What if my ex threatens to post pictures of me? It’s a crime in many places, and tools can block images from spreading. Read: Intimate images and online threats.

How do I explain the divorce to my child? Simple, true facts for their age; not their fault; loved by both. Read: Your children, and what to say.

How do I make handovers safe? School handovers, a public place, a third person, or a contact centre. Read: Handovers.

Why can’t I stop thinking about my ex? Obsessive thoughts are part of the withdrawal from a trauma bond, and they ease with distance. Read: Checking their profile.

What should I expect after leaving an emotionally abusive relationship? Self-doubt, missing them, jumpiness, tiredness and late anger are all common. Read: Leaving was the middle.

Do I have to forgive? No. Forgiveness is a choice, not a requirement for healing. Read: Closure you give yourself, and whether you have to forgive.

How many times does it take to leave? There’s no single number; leaving more than once is common. Read: If you go back.

Will I ever be the same? Not exactly, and much of you comes back, plus what you’ve learned. Read: The version of you from before.

What does recovery look like? Getting back choice and capacity, not never having a hard day. Read: What “recovered” means.

How do I help a friend who left? Believe them, follow their lead, and stay. Read: Helping someone in year one.

For professionals

A free post-separation abuse resource for advocates, therapists and lawyers to share.

The short answer

The Long After is a free post-separation abuse resource for advocates, therapists, lawyers and anyone supporting survivors. You may print and share it freely with credit. Here is what it covers, which pages work best as handouts, and how to cite it.

A free post-separation abuse resource for advocates, therapists, lawyers and anyone supporting survivors.

What it is

A free book on what happens after leaving an abusive relationship: post-separation abuse, safety after leaving, contact, courts and systems, children, feelings, health, and rebuilding. Every chapter opens with a short plain answer, lists its sources, and links to related chapters and to the Pattern Decoder, which names hundreds of tactics, one page each.

Pages that work as handouts

  • The card: the whole book on one screen
  • Safety plan worksheet
  • The record: a four-line incident log and timeline
  • Device and account audit
  • Court day checklist
  • Words you can use: the pocket card
  • Helping someone in year one: for families and friends
  • Find help

Sharing and licence

The book is free to read and share under a Creative Commons licence (CC BY-NC-ND 4.0): share it with credit to Shae Stovell, don’t sell it, and don’t share edited copies. Link to the chapter page so readers always get the current version.

How it’s written

  • Answers first, in plain language, for readers in distress, often on a phone.
  • No diagnosis of readers or of any named person.
  • No timelines for recovery, and no prescribed feelings.
  • Legal information as categories with country examples, never as advice.
  • Disputed areas are labelled as disputed. See What this book might have wrong.

How to cite it

Stovell, S. (2026). The Long After: What happens once you’ve left (2nd ed.). Noble Father Creations. noblefathercreations.com/longafter

Corrections

If you spot an error, especially in the legal notes or helpline numbers, please report it through the Noble Father Creations homepage. Corrections are made and dated.

Using it in sessions and groups

  • Share one chapter at a time, matched to what the person is facing.
  • The “Say” boxes work well as role-play material.
  • The record and safety plan worksheets can be filled in together.
  • Short answers can be read aloud at the start of a group.

Citing the evidence

For the full list of research, laws and services the book relies on, with links, see Every source in this book.

Printing

Every page prints cleanly with its sources. For a printed pack, start with Where are you right now?, What post-separation abuse is, The record and Safety plan worksheet.

Every source in this book

Where does the information in this book come from?

The short answer

This page lists every source this guide cites as domestic abuse research sources, 263 in all: research studies and books, laws and official guidance, and the services and organisations named in the chapters. Each one shows which chapters use it, with a link where one could be confirmed.

Every chapter of this book ends with its own list of sources. This page brings them together, so you, an advocate or a researcher can see the whole base the book stands on. It’s here for checking, not for reading straight through, and it doubles as a list of domestic abuse research sources for anyone studying the subject.

How are the sources chosen?

The book uses published research where it exists, the laws and official guidance that apply, and the national services that people can call. Where research is thin or disputed, the chapter says so. What this book might have wrong explains the weak spots, and For professionals explains how to cite the book.

How do the links work?

Research articles link to their DOI, a permanent address for the article, where one could be matched with confidence. Services link to their own websites. Laws in the UK link to a search on the official legislation site. 104 of 263 sources have a link. Laws, helpline numbers and services change, so check the current position where you live. The A to Z explains the terms.
